## [Paper Derives Information-Theoretic Reliability Ceiling for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14112) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2607.14112v3) argues that LLMs have an information-theoretic reliability ceiling that no model can exceed, determined by how much output uncertainty is resolvable from observable context. It derives a first-principles scaling law that generalizes the Chinchilla scaling law and explains when scaling improves reliability. This work challenges the common assumption that sufficient scale can achieve perfect reliability on any task, offering a structural account of when scaling fails. It could influence how researchers allocate compute and data, design evaluation benchmarks, and reason about retrieval-augmentation and catastrophic forgetting. The framework decomposes the reliability gap into a resolvable component, closable with additional context, and a subjective component inherent to task ambiguity; autoregressive generation further degrades the ceiling at a rate governed by the task's dependency kernel, which quantifies inter-token correlations. The derived scaling law states that performance is bottlenecked by the scarcer resource—training data or model capacity—and recovers Chinchilla scaling as a special case.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Scaling laws describe how neural network performance changes as model size, data, and compute are increased. The Chinchilla scaling law, proposed by DeepMind researchers in 2022, showed that for compute-optimal training, model parameters and training tokens should be scaled roughly equally, shifting the field away from simply making models larger. Information theory provides mathematical tools for quantifying uncertainty and the limits of communication or prediction, which this paper applies to generative language tasks.

## [Self-State Attacks on Self-Hosted AI Agents Expose OS Defense Limits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.17986) ⭐️ 8.0/10

Researchers Zeyu Chen, Léon Denis, Roberto Di Pietro, and Jürgen Schmidhuber published a paper (arXiv:2607.17986v2) formalizing 'self-state attacks' on self-hosted AI agents and empirically evaluating how far existing OS defenses can prevent, detect, and recover from them. Using four agent workloads and a Linux telemetry pipeline, they found consistent limitations across all defense dimensions. Self-hosted AI agents are increasingly deployed with persistent memory and configuration, so their compromise could silently corrupt future behavior in ways that are hard to distinguish from legitimate updates. The findings suggest generic OS mechanisms are fundamentally insufficient, pointing to a need for self-state-aware security designs in the AI agent ecosystem. File-level controls either leave alternative mutation paths open or, when complete over tested operations, also block legitimate updates; detectors flag substantial legitimate activity while selective methods cover only part of the attack space. Protected backups can restore corrupted state but require a trusted recovery point and may incur rollback cost, and the core limitation is not OS observability but the lack of decision context to combine broad coverage with selective decisions.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Self-hosted AI agents maintain persistent memory, instructions, and configuration files that shape their future behavior, and they read and write these files using legitimate OS system calls. A self-state attack occurs when an attacker exploits the agent's own legitimate write permissions to corrupt this state, making malicious changes hard to distinguish from benign ones at the OS level. This paper formalizes that attack space and tests representative OS defenses against it.

## [Teach-and-Grow Learning: Training-Free Robot Skill Acquisition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17209) ⭐️ 8.0/10

Researchers present Teach-and-Grow Learning (TGL), a training-free architecture that converts a few successful demonstrations into reusable robot skills without any gradient updates, fine-tuning, or reinforcement learning. The implementation uses OpenAI GPT-6 Astra for multimodal reasoning and Codex to connect the agent to robot tools, achieving 99.9% mean success on four LIBERO suites and 92.4% on seven LIBERO-Plus perturbation categories. This approach departs from the dominant VLA and world-action paradigm, where robots must repeatedly collect data and optimize policies to absorb unfamiliar manipulation tasks. By keeping pretrained weights fixed and expanding an explicit Skill Library instead, TGL could substantially lower the cost and time required to teach robots new behaviors, affecting both robotics research and industrial deployment. The agent identifies subgoals shared across demonstrations, expresses them as closed-loop Skill Blocks, and grounds each block in the current scene, with physical feedback guiding the next action and any recovery. Verified behaviors enter a persistent Skill Library while Experience Memory records the conditions and repairs that inform later decisions; teaching is described as an accelerator rather than a precondition, since the agent can also drive the robot directly.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language-action (VLA) models combine a pretrained vision-language model as a perception and reasoning core with an action output, letting a robot follow natural-language instructions from camera input. Traditionally, adapting such models to new manipulation tasks requires additional robot data collection and policy optimization, a recurring retraining burden. TGL instead relies on a pretrained multimodal LLM, GPT-6 Astra, plus Codex for tool connection, so the robot's knowledge grows through explicit, reusable skill structures rather than weight updates.

## [InterTrack: A Behavior World Model for Cross-Terrain Humanoid Whole-Body Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18234) ⭐️ 8.0/10

Researchers introduce InterTrack, described as the first behavior world model (BWM) for robust humanoid whole-body tracking with environment interaction, built on a Transformer that jointly predicts the next action, state, and behavior distribution. It reports an 81.3% success rate on terrain interaction tasks (4.3x the best evaluated baseline) and a 99.3% fall-recovery rate, plus the first demonstration of real-time cross-terrain whole-body teleoperation on a humanoid robot. Most general-purpose motion trackers are trained only on flat ground, while cross-terrain controllers tend to be task-specific or limited to low-dimensional locomotion commands, so InterTrack points toward a single learned model that can generalize across terrain and interaction tasks. If the results hold up, this could meaningfully advance humanoid robots operating in unstructured real-world environments such as disaster response, construction, and logistics. To scale interaction training data, the authors use an automatic annotation pipeline that reconstructs 3D support geometry from retargeted motions, and at deployment the policy handles commands that are implausible in the current environment in a "best-effort" manner. The system also improves free-space tracking and outperforms three leading tracking baselines, while additionally demonstrating object interaction, stable responses to missing supports, and robust recovery from falls.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: A world model is a learned internal simulator that predicts how the environment evolves in response to an agent's actions, and it is often used in model-based reinforcement learning to improve planning and control. Whole-body tracking means a humanoid robot follows reference whole-body motions, such as those captured from humans, while keeping balance. Transformer-based world models apply the attention architecture behind modern language models to sequence prediction over states and actions, and this paper combines that idea with cross-terrain locomotion and interaction.

<a id="item-44"></a>
## [New Mathematical Theory Extends Shannon to Pragmatic Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10986) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.10986v3) proposes a mathematical theory of pragmatic information built on the isoteleia mapping, which formalizes equifinality by treating distinct semantic paths that lead to the same optimal action as pragmatically equivalent. The paper defines pragmatic entropy, up/down pragmatic mutual information, channel capacity, and rate-distortion, and proves lossless source coding, channel coding, and rate-distortion theorems that extend Shannon's classical results. This framework shifts the focus of information theory from symbol fidelity to the effectiveness of information in guiding actions, offering a common language for task-oriented communication, networked control, autonomous systems, and embodied AI. It could influence how engineers design resource-constrained intelligent systems that must extract value from information under real-world constraints. The paper introduces pragmatic value of information (VoI) and pragmatic cost of information (CoI) as decision-theoretic duals to rate-distortion and capacity, and develops a Lagrangian dual framework for cross-layer optimization. It also derives a pragmatic efficiency bound E_p(λ)=sup_R[Φ_p(R)−λCoI_p(R)] characterizing the maximum net utility of a resource-constrained intelligent system, with closed-form expressions for Gaussian channels and sources and a Bellman equation for sequential decision-making.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Shannon's information theory quantifies information at the syntactic level, focusing on the faithful transmission of symbols regardless of their meaning or usefulness. Equifinality is the principle that in open systems a given end state can be reached by many different means, and this paper formalizes that idea for information by grouping semantic paths that lead to the same optimal action. The result is a three-tier hierarchy of syntactic, semantic, and pragmatic information, where each successive abstraction removes distinctions irrelevant to the task.

<a id="item-45"></a>
## [Double descent explained via statistical mechanics and least action](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19076) ⭐️ 8.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.19076v2) explains the double descent phenomenon in machine learning using statistical mechanics, arguing that stochastic gradient training behaves like a particle diffusing over a loss landscape at an induced temperature T. The paper shows that finite training time induces an effective weight decay, making each parameter a quadratic degree of freedom, so the equipartition theorem distributes energy as T/2 per parameter. Double descent challenges the classical bias-variance tradeoff, and this work offers a physics-based mechanism that could unify understanding of generalization in overparameterized deep networks. If correct, it may influence how researchers think about model size, regularization, and training dynamics in deep learning theory. The argument relies on the fundamental postulate of statistical mechanics—that an equilibrated run visits every parameter vector of a given training loss equally often, with probability given by the Boltzmann distribution—and on the fact that adding parameters lowers the temperature at fixed training loss, driving the distribution toward the stationary path. It also notes that adding parameters can only lower the L2 norm of the stationary path, effectively increasing weight regularization.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Double descent is the phenomenon where test error first decreases, then peaks when the model can just fit the training data, and then decreases again as the number of parameters grows. Statistical mechanics provides tools like temperature, the Boltzmann distribution, and the equipartition theorem to describe how energy is shared among degrees of freedom in a physical system. This paper maps those concepts onto stochastic gradient-based training to explain why overparameterized models can generalize well.

Cactus released Needle 3, a family of ultra-compact automation models (8-29MB binaries, 25-121 million parameters at 2-bit) that handle tool calls and structured JSON output instead of chat. The 20-layer model scores 86.0 on Mobile Actions, beating LFM2.5 1.2B (82.4), Qwen3.5 0.8B (76.0), and Apple's on-device model (57.6), and the team claims DeepSeek V4 Flash-grade performance on narrow tasks after finetuning with just 4 layers. Needle 3 uses a Monarch Hadamard MLP that replaces the dense FFN with Walsh-Hadamard-initialized Kronecker factor pairs, achieving O(d√d) parameters and compute instead of O(d²). It supports English, French, Spanish, German, Dutch, Italian, and Polish, runs on macOS, Linux (x86-64, ARM64, ARMv7, RISC-V, MIPS32), Windows, Android, iOS, watchOS, tvOS, WebAssembly, and WASI, and adds regex-based triggers plus calibrated confidence scores for escalation.

OpenJev is a new open-source project that reproduces the interface pattern of TypeSafe's Jev, a 'System One' model that returns typed decisions instead of free-form text, using a 4B open model (Qwen3.5-4B) to read typed option probabilities directly from logits in a single forward pass. It was presented on Hacker News, where it reached 473 points and 231 comments, and an independent evaluation showed 84.5% agreement with Jev's reference answers versus Jev's own 88.3% on the same subset. OpenJev explicitly states that it reproduces the interface pattern of Jev but not Jev's undisclosed model or training, and it reads typed option probabilities off a 4B open model's logits in one forward pass. A vLLM patch that turns DiffusionGemma into Jev is also available, and community evals on an DGX Spark showed similar latency and matching results within a few points, while a Qwen36 model clearly lost to both.

## [C++26 Makes Trivial Infinite Loops Defined Behavior](https://www.sandordargo.com/blog/2026/09/16/cpp26-trivial-infinite-loops) ⭐️ 7.0/10

C++26 adopted proposal P2809R3, which makes trivially empty infinite loops such as `while(true);` well-defined instead of undefined behavior. The proposal was also accepted as a defect report, so compilers may apply the fix to earlier C++ modes like C++20. This change prevents compilers from optimizing away infinite loops that have no side effects, which is important for embedded and microcontroller code where such loops are used intentionally, for example in STM32 HAL error handlers. It also affects how C++ developers reason about forward-progress guarantees and compiler optimizations. When both conditions are met, the loop body is replaced with a call to `std::this_thread::yield()`, which gives the loop forward-progress semantics it previously lacked. However, the loop must be a trivially empty iteration statement — using `continue` instead of an empty body restores the undefined behavior.

hackernews · ibobev · Sep 17, 20:52 · [Discussion](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49746406)

**Background**: In C and C++, an infinite loop with no side effects was historically undefined behavior, allowing compilers to assume the loop terminates and optimize it away. This caused problems for programs that intentionally spin forever, such as embedded firmware. P2809R3 changes the standard so that trivial infinite loops are well-defined, but the implementation inserts a yield call to preserve forward progress.

<details><summary>References</summary>
<ul>
<li><a href="https://www.sandordargo.com/blog/2026/09/16/cpp26-trivial-infinite-loops">C++26: Trivial infinite loops are no longer undefined ...</a></li>
<li><a href="https://www.open-std.org/jtc1/sc22/wg21/docs/papers/2024/p2809r3.html">P2809R3: Trivial infinite loops are not Undefined Behavior</a></li>
<li><a href="https://news.lavx.hu/article/c-26-eliminates-undefined-behavior-for-trivial-infinite-loops">C++26 eliminates undefined behavior for trivial infinite loops</a></li>

</ul>
</details>

**Discussion**: Commenters strongly criticized the hidden insertion of `std::this_thread::yield()`, calling it a surprising system call in an otherwise empty loop and arguing that an infinite loop should compile to an infinite loop. Others noted that `continue` still triggers undefined behavior, and some pointed out valid use cases in microcontroller programming. The overall sentiment is that the change introduces hidden code and breaks the forward-progress guarantee.

## [Thomas Ptacek: Use LLMs as Copyeditors, Never as Writers](https://simonwillison.net/2026/Sep/17/how-to-write-with-an-llm/) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Thomas Ptacek published a blog post titled "How To Write With An LLM" arguing that LLMs should be used as copyeditors rather than writing assistants, with the strict rule that "you may not use a single word an LLM suggests to you." Simon Willison endorsed the principle, noting he uses LLMs for fact-checking, spelling, grammar, and as an occasional thesaurus, but never lets them write content for his blog. The post offers a memorable, actionable heuristic for anyone integrating LLMs into writing workflows, addressing growing concerns about AI-generated text losing a writer's individual voice. It also reflects a broader industry debate about where the line should be drawn between AI assistance and AI authorship. Ptacek frames the rule as "intellectual personal protective equipment" and urges writers to be strict about it, while also sharing a screenshot of his personal LLM copyediting tool, a kickstart prompt, and his system prompt in a Hacker News comment. Willison links to his own proofreading prompt and says LLM-suggested phrasing has a "weird smell" that makes it easy to spot.

## [Regularized Emphatic TD Learning Achieves Stability Under Constant Stepsizes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19170) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper constructs an ergodic two-state counterexample showing that emphatic temporal-difference learning (ETD) can be unstable with constant stepsizes, even though its mean map contracts, because the sampled product has a positive top Lyapunov exponent. It introduces regularized emphatic TD (RETD), a normalized first-order post-shock repair that stores the emphatic TD signal in a leaky scalar state and releases a delayed correction, and proves almost-sure convergence for harmonic diminishing stepsizes plus a conditional constant-stepsize moment-contraction result. This work identifies a fundamental stability gap in ETD, a widely studied off-policy reinforcement learning method, and offers a theoretically grounded fix with convergence guarantees. It matters for RL researchers and practitioners who rely on constant stepsizes in practice, since the counterexample shows that existing convergence intuitions based on mean-map contraction can be misleading. RETD's raw equilibrium is an affine shift of the ETD equilibrium, and single- and two-regularization readouts recover the ETD fixed point exactly; it has certified negative exponents on the two-state construction and one Baird point, while the positive Baird ETD sign remains numerical. Paired 10,000-run experiments validate both separations, fixed-point recovery, a nonmonotone stability region, and task dependence, but RETD changes post-shock dynamics without reducing the shared follow-on-trace variance.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Temporal-difference (TD) learning is a core reinforcement learning method that updates value estimates from bootstrapped targets. Off-policy TD learns from data generated by a behavior policy different from the target policy, which introduces stability challenges; emphatic TD (ETD) addresses these by using follow-on traces to reweight updates and change the effective state distribution. Stability of such stochastic algorithms is often analyzed via Lyapunov exponents, which measure the asymptotic growth rate of products of random matrices, but a negative mean contraction does not guarantee sampled stability.

## [Mapping 14,767 LLM Benchmark Papers Reveals Shifting Evaluation Trends](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19182) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper systematically maps 14,767 papers introducing or updating LLM evaluation resources from arXiv submissions between January 2022 and August 2026, using staged screening and automated full-text coding. It finds growing emphasis on action, interaction, and professional applications, with uneven adoption of model-based scoring and model-generated materials. This meta-analysis provides a rare longitudinal view of how the research community's expectations for LLMs are translated into concrete tests, which can inform benchmark design and reveal potential blind spots. It matters for AI researchers, evaluators, and policymakers who rely on benchmarks to assess model progress and safety. The study covers arXiv submissions from January 2022 to August 2026 and uses automated full-text coding to analyze target systems, domains, evaluation materials, conditions, and scoring mechanisms. It notes that LLM-based scoring grows within both agent and non-agent groups, while model-generated materials show no comparable sustained increase in recent cohorts.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: LLM benchmarks are standardized tests used to evaluate model capabilities, such as MMLU, HellaSwag, and Chatbot Arena, and they are central to how progress in large language models is assessed and communicated. A systematic mapping study is a methodology for structuring and categorizing a large body of research to identify trends and gaps. As AI increasingly participates in constructing tests, performing tasks, and judging responses, questions arise about whether expanding evaluation provides independent evidence or reproduces model biases.

## [Paper Proposes a Foundation Model Operating System Layer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19203) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv position paper (2609.19203v1) argues that the AI field needs a Foundation Model Operating System (FMOS), a system layer that virtualizes foundation model interactions so applications see dedicated, trustworthy FM instances with effectively unbounded capabilities. Internally, the FMOS would orchestrate knowledge across memory tiers, handle model selection and resource allocation, and enforce verification and policy, learning when to intervene much like the brain switches between fast intuition and slow deliberation. Today's compound agentic stacks are fragmented: even as protocols like MCP and A2A ease connectivity, each framework embeds its own implicit runtime for state, memory, budgets, and guardrails, making behavior non-portable and governance brittle. An FMOS layer could standardize portability, resource governance, and policy enforcement across heterogeneous models and agents, reshaping how AI stacks are designed and governed. The paper is a position piece without empirical results, so its claims about orchestration, verification, and self-evolving policies remain conceptual rather than benchmarked. It explicitly frames FMOS as analogous to virtual machines abstracting physical hardware, and positions itself against the pre-OS era when every program re-implemented basic services.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Foundation models are large pretrained AI models that can be adapted to many tasks, and recent AI applications increasingly combine several of them into compound agentic systems rather than relying on a single monolithic model. The Model Context Protocol (MCP), introduced by Anthropic in November 2024, standardizes how AI systems connect to external tools and data, while the Agent2Agent (A2A) protocol provides a common language for agents built on different frameworks to communicate. The paper's analogy is to computing before operating systems, when each program had to reimplement basic services that an OS later abstracted and shared.

## [TranSGrid Testbed Exposes Gaps in Systematic Generalization Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19212) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces TranSGrid, a testbed that unifies deductive, inductive, and abductive reasoning to evaluate systematic generalization. Experiments with seven Transformers on 4,800 instances show the largest model solves 79.6% of a standard held-out test set but only 55.3% of TranSGrid and 15.8% of its hardest subset. This work reveals that existing systematic generalization benchmarks are too simplified and overestimate model capabilities, which could misguide AI research toward false confidence in reasoning abilities. It calls for more comprehensive evaluation that includes all three reasoning types, affecting how the community measures progress toward general intelligence. The performance gap persists within the training length range, showing that productivity alone is insufficient for evaluating systematic generalization. When the authors reintroduce either approximately linear action composition or action-explicit goals into TranSGrid, solve rates return to roughly the held-out test set level, indicating that either simplification alone reduces the task to an ordinary test set.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Systematic generalization is the ability to solve novel problems by recombining known atomic elements, a hallmark of human intelligence. Existing studies often simplify it using approximately linear action composition, productivity-based tests, and action-explicit goals, which make it easier to study but omit essential aspects. Deductive reasoning deals with logical certainty, inductive reasoning with probability, and abductive reasoning with inference to the best explanation.

## [AutoTuring Tests Whether AI Agents Truly Understand Computer Architecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19387) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces AutoTuring, an evaluation that gives the same AI agent the same 15-dimensional accelerator design space twice: once as named architectural knobs with simulator counters, and once as anonymous variables on [0,1], with the evaluator, legal space, and reachable optima held identical. On a nine-kernel FP16 GEMM basket, the architecture-aware agent beat a modeled H200 by 5.4% and its blind counterpart by 12.3% on average while using 70.1% fewer simulator calls. Existing evaluations of AI agents for hardware design only show that a design improved, not why, so they cannot distinguish genuine architectural reasoning from competent blind search over meaningless knobs. AutoTuring's controlled comparison offers a methodology for measuring whether AI-driven hardware design knowledge will transfer to the next architecture, which matters for anyone relying on agents to design accelerators. The paper reports preliminary findings based on only five to six runs per condition on a single modeled accelerator, and notes that a critic loop recovers most of the gap for the blind agent while buying the architect nothing, suggesting architectural knowledge and structured critique act as substitutes rather than complements. The authors emphasize that the comparison itself, not the accelerator, is the contribution.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: AI agents are increasingly used to design hardware accelerators, and many reports claim success, but such reports typically hold the problem framing fixed while varying the agent, making it impossible to tell whether the agent reasons about the machine or merely searches anonymous knobs. AutoTuring inverts this by holding the agent fixed and varying whether the design space carries meaning, using a 15-dimensional accelerator space and an Actor-Critic loop. FP16 GEMM (general matrix multiplication) kernels are a standard workload for benchmarking accelerator performance, and the modeled H200 is a reference NVIDIA data-center GPU.

## [Paper Exposes Tool Hallucination as Blind Spot in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19425) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.19425) by Laxmipriya Ganesh Iyer introduces a five-class taxonomy (H1-H5) of tool hallucination in tool-augmented LLM agents and a training-free closed-world resolver called the Resolution Rung, which checks registry membership and argument signatures. Across ten hosted models and two invocation surfaces, the study measured 322 genuine hallucinations, with fabricated-tool calls concentrating on the unconstrained raw-JSON surface (34 vs. 3), and a 675B model performing no better than 7-8B models. This work identifies a structural blind spot that existing tool-selection and tool-gating defenses cannot address, because a hallucinated call is by construction not a decision any gate made. It matters for AI safety and reliability in agentic systems, especially as the Model Context Protocol (MCP) merges multiple servers into one namespace, creating new hallucination surfaces even frontier models fall into. The paper proves that hallucination defense must precede any causal gate and characterizes an irreducible residue: borrowed arguments that are schema-indistinguishable from a valid call. It extends the taxonomy to MCP with a second set (M1-M5), measuring 154 hallucinations on the live MCP surface, and releases the versioned Hallucinated-Tools Benchmark (HTB) for comparable resolver evaluation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Tool-augmented LLM agents extend language models by letting them emit structured calls to external tools such as APIs for sending messages or transferring funds. Existing defenses focus on selecting the right tool or gating what an agent may do with real tools, but both assume the emitted call refers to a real tool. Tool hallucination occurs when a model calls a tool that was never registered or passes arguments no schema declares, and the Model Context Protocol (MCP) is a standard for connecting agents to multiple tool servers, whose namespace merging can amplify such errors.

## [RL Post-Training Shows Decomposed-to-Composed Asymmetry in LMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19465) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.19465) proposes a dependency-graph framework that formalizes compositional reasoning into three levels of increasing complexity, and empirically tests it using data-structure tasks with deterministic rewards. The study finds a consistent decomposed-to-composed asymmetry: training on decomposed skills does not reliably transfer to composed tasks, while training on composed tasks transfers back to decomposed tasks, with a theoretical explanation provided. This work challenges the common assumption that teaching models individual skills will automatically compose into complex reasoning, which has direct implications for how RL post-training data should be designed. If the asymmetry holds broadly, it suggests practitioners should prioritize composed-task training to build generalizable reasoning in language models. The framework is instantiated with data-structure tasks that offer deterministic reward computation and clear compositional structure, and the authors further evaluate compositional generalization under length extrapolation, structural distribution shift, and transfer to tasks requiring unseen skills. A pilot study on real-world tool-calling benchmarks provides preliminary evidence that the asymmetry may extend to practical settings, though the paper remains a preprint without peer review or community discussion yet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Compositional reasoning refers to a model's ability to combine previously learned skills in novel ways to solve problems it has not seen before, which is essential because training data can never cover every possible situation. Reinforcement learning post-training is a stage after initial pre-training and fine-tuning where models are optimized via reward signals to improve reasoning, and it has driven much of the recent progress in large language model capabilities. This paper asks whether such RL post-training actually improves the ability to compose skills, rather than just individual skills.

## [MLP Probes on LLaMA-3.1-8B Activations Detect Harmful Prompts Efficiently](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19472) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.19472) trains lightweight MLP classifier probes with only 12.6M parameters on activations extracted from LLaMA-3.1-8B to detect harmful prompts. Evaluated on WildJailbreak, Beavertails, and AEGIS 2.0, the probes achieve F1 scores of 99%, 83%, and 84% respectively, competitive with guard models roughly 1000x larger while cutting latency and compute costs. This work addresses a real gap in efficient safety infrastructure: external guardrail models add latency and compute overhead and remain blind to the model's internal workings, limiting utility in resource-constrained, time-critical deployments. If internal latent states already encode harmfulness, safety checks could become cheaper and more tightly integrated into autonomous LLM systems. The probes are trained on LLaMA-3.1-8B activations and are tiny (12.6M parameters) compared to the guard models they match, but performance varies by dataset — near-perfect on WildJailbreak (99% F1) yet notably lower on Beavertails (83%) and AEGIS 2.0 (84%). The paper frames the core question as whether the model already knows when content is harmful, and the results suggest internal representations carry a usable safety signal.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: LLM safety is often enforced by external guardrail models that scan prompts or outputs, but these add latency and compute overhead and cannot see what happens inside the model. Activation probing is an interpretability technique that trains a small classifier — here a multi-layer perceptron (MLP) — on a model's intermediate activations to predict a concept such as harmfulness. LLaMA-3.1-8B is Meta's open-weight 8-billion-parameter transformer model, and WildJailbreak is an open-source synthetic safety dataset of 262K vanilla and adversarial jailbreak prompt-response pairs used to evaluate such detectors.

## [QVAC Genesis III: 191B-Token Open Synthetic STEM Corpus for Small Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19513) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers released QVAC Genesis III, a 191.43B-token open synthetic STEM corpus spanning 19 domains and multiple difficulty levels, built with a dual teacher-student distillation strategy in which a weak edge-scale student's failures become corrective explanations and its successes become contrastive option-level reasoning. Controlled from-scratch ablations with 1.7B-parameter models show consistent gains over Cosmopedia-v2 and Cosmo-1B on ARC, GPQA Diamond, and MMLU STEM, with up to +28.57% on ARC-E and +21.35% on ARC-C. High-quality pre-training data remains a bottleneck for STEM-focused and educational language models targeting edge AI and on-device deployment, where token budgets are tightly constrained, and the open ecosystem has lacked STEM-specific synthetic corpora with high per-token learning value. By releasing a large, openly available corpus and demonstrating measurable gains for small models, this work could lower the barrier for researchers and developers building efficient educational or edge-deployed models. The corpus uses an LLM-as-a-parser evaluation protocol that extracts final answers from free-form outputs and tracks both accuracy and answer validity, reaching a Valid Answer Rate of up to 99.45%. Validation was limited to controlled from-scratch ablations with 1.7B-parameter models, so the results may not directly transfer to larger model scales or other domains.

**Background**: Knowledge distillation is a common technique in which a smaller "student" model learns to mimic a larger, more capable "teacher" model, often used for model compression. ## [EconSkills Distills Web Agent Trajectories into Reusable Economic Data Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19523) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces EconSkills, a skill library and evaluation framework that distills verified EconWebArena trajectories into parameterized standard operating procedures for retrieving live economic data. Each skill records its scope, navigation procedure, site-specific guidance, verification checks, and recovery steps, with source-instance values replaced by placeholders. The work shows that abstracted, reusable procedures transfer better to held-out tasks than replaying raw trajectories, offering a concrete design target for coverage-aware skill selection and context delivery in web agents. This could reduce redundant site revisits and improve efficiency for agents operating on live economic data sources. In controlled transfer experiments, matched skills improved success over no-skill prompting and required fewer steps on paired successes, while abstraction substantially outperformed raw trajectory replay. At library scale, retrieval was competitive with the no-skill baseline overall and performed best on directly covered tasks, but approximate matches on uncovered tasks offset these gains.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Web agents are AI systems that autonomously navigate websites to complete tasks such as retrieving data or filling forms. EconWebArena is a benchmark of 360 curated economic tasks from 82 authoritative sources across ten domains, designed to evaluate agents on realistic, multimodal economic tasks grounded in live websites. Most existing evaluations discard the procedures learned during earlier successful interactions, meaning agents must relearn navigation each time they revisit a site.

## [Unified Framework Evaluates Trustworthiness of LLMs, Agents, and Multimodal AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19524) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (arXiv:2609.19524v1) proposes a unified evaluation framework that connects output-level, trajectory-level, and cross-modal assessment through eight trustworthiness dimensions: capability, robustness, safety, fairness, transparency, governance, oversight, and efficiency. It adds a meta-evaluation layer that examines the validity, reliability, and reproducibility of the evaluation itself, alongside uncertainty estimates and traceable evidence. Current benchmarking practices rely heavily on aggregate scores that can mask critical failures, and LLMs, agentic systems, and multimodal models each require different forms of assessment. A framework that maps native metrics to common performance bands while linking to governance frameworks, international standards, and EU regulatory requirements could influence how future evaluation standards are designed and how oversight is conducted. The framework preserves system-specific metrics while mapping native measurements to common performance bands, and includes safety-critical overrides that prevent aggregate scores from masking critical failures. The authors acknowledge that empirical validation across deployment contexts remains an essential next step, and the paper is an arXiv preprint that has not yet undergone peer review.

Agentic AI evaluation has separately emphasized trajectory-level assessment—judging the sequence of actions an agent takes rather than only its final output—while multimodal large language models (MLLMs) require cross-modal evaluation of how they integrate visual and linguistic information. This paper attempts to unify these fragmented evaluation strands under a single framework with a meta-evaluation layer.

## [SIFT: Sample-Efficient Self-Improvement for Coding Agents via Fast Tree-Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19526) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduce SIFT (Recursive Self Improvement via Fast Tree-search), a framework that lets coding agents recursively modify their own implementations under strict compute budgets. It replaces expensive re-running of benchmark tasks with an LLM-as-a-judge signal that performs pairwise comparisons between candidate patches, aggregates win-loss records with a regularized Bradley-Terry model, and uses the resulting strength scores to drive rank-based parent sampling in a lightweight disaggregated tree search. SIFT reportedly outperforms existing tree-search-based self-evolution frameworks on the full Polyglot benchmark while using significantly less CPU time, wall-clock time, and API cost. Self-improving coding agents are a promising path to recursive capability gains, but their main bottleneck has been the cost of evaluating each candidate self-modification. By cutting evaluation cost with judge-based signals and reserving expensive task runs for only the most promising nodes, SIFT makes self-improvement loops practical under tight budgets, which could accelerate research on autonomous agent evolution and lower the barrier for smaller labs. The core technical trick is a disaggregated tree search pipeline in which the LLM judge provides intermediate signal to guide exploration without waiting on slow downstream evaluations; expensive benchmark runs are reserved only for the most promising nodes. The judge's pairwise win-loss records are aggregated with a regularized Bradley-Terry model to produce strength scores used for rank-based parent sampling. The paper reports gains on the full Polyglot benchmark with lower CPU hours, wall-clock time, and API cost, though the abstract does not specify exact numbers or the base models used.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Coding agents are LLM-based systems that can write, edit, and run code; recursive self-improvement means the agent modifies its own implementation, then is re-evaluated to see if the change helped. Prior work showed this can boost benchmark scores, but evaluating each candidate modification by re-running benchmark tasks is slow and expensive. LLM-as-a-judge is a technique where a language model scores or compares outputs instead of relying on human annotation or reference-based metrics, and the Bradley-Terry model is a standard statistical method for turning pairwise comparison outcomes into a ranking of item strengths. Tree search explores a branching space of candidate modifications, and SIFT combines these ideas to guide that search cheaply.

## [Two-Agent Resume Screening Changes Who Advances](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19530) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.19530) studies a two-agent resume screening procedure in which employer-side and candidate-side agents exchange evidence and update judgments before deciding who advances, tested on 600 constructed resume-job pairs using GPT-5.5 and Claude Opus 4.7. Two-agent screening raised overall pass rates (33.3% to 39.3% for GPT-5.5; 34.0% to 35.5% for Opus 4.7), and on a 191-pair borderline pool pass-instance rates jumped from 4.5% to 26.2% and from 6.5% to 16.1% across three runs. As hiring becomes agent-mediated on both the employer and candidate sides, this work shows that the screening procedure itself, not just the underlying model, shapes who reaches human review and how reliably that access recurs. The finding that two-agent screening changes decisions in both directions, and that no single-call threshold recovers its selections, has direct implications for fairness, access, and the design of AI hiring pipelines. The effect is not a uniform relaxation: two-agent screening rejects some applications that one-call screening advances, and at similar pass volumes the two procedures advance different applications. Among discovery-selected cases re-executed in fresh runs, two-agent-only selections recurred less often than shared selections (clearly under GPT-5.5, less certainly under Opus 4.7), while a separate one-call follow-up showed no comparable decline.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Resume screening is typically automated as a static, one-call judgment over a resume-job pair, even though real hiring is bilateral: employers assess fit while candidates present and defend their qualifications. Multi-agent LLM systems, in which two or more LLM agents collaborate or compete on a task, have become a common architecture for handling complex workflows. This paper applies that paradigm to hiring's first gate, using state-of-the-art models GPT-5.5 (released by OpenAI in April 2026) and Claude Opus 4.7 (Anthropic's flagship Opus model).

## [Continual Enterprise World Model Discovery Benchmark on ServiceNow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19551) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.19551) introduces EnterpriseWorldShift, a benchmark built on a live ServiceNow environment with nine tables, 25 hidden business rules, and 600 evaluation actions, designed to test continual enterprise world model discovery. The authors also propose a Continual Discovery Agent (CDA) that learns hidden rules through interaction and revises its world model as rules are modified, added, and removed across four versions of the same enterprise world. This work addresses a practical and under-explored problem: enterprise AI agents cannot predict the effects of their actions without knowing organization-specific business rules that change over time. The benchmark and CDA approach could advance research in AI agents, enterprise automation, and continual learning, where agents must adapt to evolving rules without catastrophic forgetting. CDA predicts the effects of hidden rules more accurately than the prior approach of looking them up for each question, improving by up to 8.98 IoU points, and it answers from its own model without querying the running system. The benchmark presents four versions of the same enterprise world with tables and records held fixed while a rule is modified, then added, then removed, testing discovery, revision, extension, and retirement in turn.

**Background**: In enterprise platforms like ServiceNow, business rules are not built into the platform but written by each organization and revised over time; updating one field can set another field, create a record, or start an approval. A world model is an internal representation that helps an AI agent predict how its environment will change in response to actions, enabling planning without relying entirely on real-world interactions. Continual learning refers to sequentially training a model on new tasks while preserving previously learned knowledge, avoiding catastrophic forgetting.

## [SimLife Benchmark Tests Long-Horizon Pattern Understanding in Human-Agent Partnerships](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19610) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced SimLife, a scalable simulation platform for long-term household life with rich visual observations, ground-truth action logs, and synthetic dialogues with audio, along with SimLife-BP, a benchmark for long-context pattern understanding. SimLife-BP contains 106 episodes averaging 15.49 hours and 38.57 in-game days, plus 1,439 question-answer pairs probing direct, counterfactual, noisy, and inverse reasoning under varying levels of rule hints. The findings show that frontier models often achieve surface-level prediction without comprehensive rule understanding, rely on frequency-based heuristics rather than if-then reasoning over evidence, and struggle to adapt when behavioral patterns change. This suggests long-context pattern understanding remains a major bottleneck for future embodied agents, while SimLife opens a broader space for studying memory, personalization, adaptation, and long-horizon planning in everyday human-AI interaction. The benchmark evaluates models under different levels of rule hints, covering direct, counterfactual, noisy, and inverse reasoning tasks, and the platform provides ground-truth action logs and synthetic audio dialogues. The paper reports that current models rely on frequency-based heuristics rather than if-then reasoning over evidence, and fail to adapt when behavioral patterns change.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Long-horizon reasoning in AI agents involves multi-step planning and inference over extended periods, and traditional approaches often struggle with the combinatorial explosion of potential future states. Counterfactual reasoning, or what-if reasoning about alternative outcomes, is widely recognized as one of the most challenging aspects of causality in AI. Rule-based machine learning typically produces decision rules in the form of IF-THEN expressions, and SimLife-BP tests whether models can learn such latent behavioral rules from weeks or months of everyday observations.

## [New Benchmark Exposes LLM Safety Gaps in Vehicle Voice Command Authorization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19630) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.19630) introduces a 202-scenario benchmark with a seven-class decision taxonomy to evaluate how well LLMs authorize vehicle voice commands before execution. Testing two local open-weight models and three API-based LLMs, it found Decision Alignment ranging from 40.1% for Llama 3.2 3B to 89.1% for Gemini 3.1 Pro Preview, with even top models producing two to three False Executes across 161 non-execution scenarios. As LLMs are increasingly embedded in vehicle voice assistants, this work highlights a safety-critical gap: models must decide whether to execute, refuse, clarify, confirm, defer to manual control, trigger emergency response, or make no tool call. The findings suggest structured LLM decisions alone are insufficient as a safety mechanism, meaning automakers and developers need an independent enforcement layer before invoking any vehicle function. The benchmark uses Decision Alignment and safety-specific error metrics, and a controlled Llama 3.2 3B ablation raised alignment to 40.1% under a structured authorization policy versus 28.2–29.2% under schema-only and generic-safety baselines, yet False Executes persisted. API-based models scored between 83.2% and 89.1% with no statistically significant differences among them, and errors remained concentrated in confirmation and manual-control decisions.

LLM safety alignment refers to techniques that steer model behavior toward human values and safe outcomes, and research shows alignment can degrade under fine-tuning or adversarial prompting.

## [Checkpoint Handoff Protocol Separates REACH from SOLVE in Agentic RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19636) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.19636) introduces checkpoint handoff, an evaluation protocol that clones a state reached by one released checkpoint and hands it to another checkpoint without retraining, splitting endpoint success into REACH (how often a policy arrives at a state a fixed number of actions from success) and SOLVE (how often it finishes from an identical cloned state). Across two benchmarks and two independently released pipelines, the reacher-by-solver interaction is positive in all five conditions, and the authors show that naive state-restricted comparisons can flip the sign of the measured effect. Endpoint success in agentic RL conflates where an agent arrives with what it does once there, so reported gains may overstate improvements in decision-making; this protocol gives the RL and LLM-agent communities a way to attribute gains to state visitation versus policy quality. Because the effect sign can flip under naive state-restricted comparison, prior evaluations that restricted comparisons to shared states may have drawn misleading conclusions. The protocol requires only that one checkpoint's history can be replayed under another, and it crosses a reacher role and a solver role over SFT and RL checkpoints; on ALFWorld, RL improves both REACH and SOLVE, and the SFT solver never succeeds where the RL solver fails. Independent REACH and SOLVE gaps predict the aggregate interaction, suggesting long-horizon evaluation can report arrival and completion alongside endpoint success.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Agentic RL trains language-model agents that act over dozens of steps in live environments, where each observation follows from the agent's own earlier actions, so late-episode states are partly self-generated. This means an SFT checkpoint and an RL checkpoint are scored from different states even on identical tasks, and endpoint success mixes two distinct changes: where the agent arrives and what it does once there. Restricting comparison to states both policies reach does not separate these changes because it selects on an outcome.

## [When2Think: Difficulty-Aware Length Control for Hybrid Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19671) ⭐️ 7.0/10

When2Think is a post-training framework for hybrid reasoning models that dynamically allocates computation based on problem difficulty, introducing Instance-level Difficulty-Aware Control (IDAC), a reward-shaping mechanism using pre-computed reference statistics on accuracy and token usage. On AIME24, it raises Pass@3 by 10.0% while cutting token usage by 27.9% versus the base model, and reaches 40.0% Pass@3 on AIME25, outperforming compression and routing-only baselines. This work targets a well-known inefficiency of large reasoning models — overthinking easy problems and underthinking hard ones — and offers a critic-free post-training recipe that could reduce inference cost without sacrificing accuracy on hard instances. If validated, such instance-adaptive computation allocation could influence how future hybrid reasoning models balance System 1 and System 2 behavior in production. IDAC combines verifier-based rewards with batch-wise standardized advantages to enable stable critic-free optimization without learned reward models or online reference-model queries, encouraging direct answering on easy instances while preserving extended reasoning on hard ones. The results are reported on mathematical benchmarks (AIME24/AIME25) and come from an arXiv preprint that has not yet been peer-reviewed or independently validated.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) generate long chains of thought to solve complex tasks, but they often spend excessive tokens on easy questions and too few on hard ones. Hybrid reasoning models aim to switch between a fast, direct "NoThink" mode (System 1) and a slower, deliberative "Think" mode (System 2), as popularized by models such as Claude 3.7 Sonnet. Existing efficiency methods typically apply uniform length penalties or rigid routing rules, which trade accuracy on hard problems for savings on easy ones; When2Think instead frames efficient reasoning as an instance-adaptive computation allocation problem.

## [FINSKILLOPS: Self-Evolving Multi-Agent System for SEC Filing QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19680) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced FINSKILLOPS, a multi-agent system for SEC filing question answering that improves after deployment by converting typed failure diagnoses into scoped, reusable skill patches. Across six financial QA benchmarks, a single frozen skill registry achieved the highest verdict-weighted correctness and reference consistency, raising correctness from 3.70 to 4.55 on an enhanced benchmark, while a 12-round operational study promoted only 6 of 33 proposed skills and cut the monitoring non-correct rate from 20.0% to 12.5%. The work reframes post-deployment improvement of financial QA as controlled behavioral maintenance, where each correction must earn deployment without breaking previously correct answers. This matters for regulated domains like finance and accounting, where reliability, auditability, and regression control are as important as raw accuracy, and it offers a concrete governance model for self-evolving agent systems. FINSKILLOPS governs skills through targeted validation, protected-case regression checks, negative controls, and versioned replacement or retirement, and it derives skills from evidence-grounded, typed failure diagnoses covering period, entity, evidence use, and calculation errors. The low promotion rate (6 of 33) and the use of a single frozen skill registry highlight that the system prioritizes avoiding regressions over aggressively adding new behaviors.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: SEC filings are dense regulatory documents that public companies must file with the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission, and answering questions about them requires retrieving evidence scattered across periods, entities, and tables. Multi-agent systems split such tasks among specialized agents, but their reliability is typically fixed at deployment, so new questions repeatedly expose the same classes of errors. Self-improvement methods can turn failures into new behaviors, yet they offer limited control over where a correction applies or which previously correct answers it might break.

## [LearnActCoder Uses Role-Aware Error Memory to Improve Clinical Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19721) ⭐️ 7.0/10

LearnActCoder introduces a Learn-Then-Act inference-time adaptation framework that converts errors from a small labeled LEARN batch into a structured Mistake Knowledge Database (MistakeKDB), routing false-negative lessons to a recall-oriented Coder and false-positive lessons to a precision-oriented Judge. On 150 matched MIMIC-III notes, the structured memory improves CPT F1 by 5.9 percentage points, while raw-example and reflection-style memories stay near the no-memory baseline. Clinical coding is a high-impact, labor-intensive task where LLM agents repeatedly hit the same failure modes, and this work shows that structured, feedback-derived error memory can adapt coding behavior across cases without weight updates or workflow changes. If validated, such inference-time adaptation could offer a cheaper path to improving medical coding accuracy than retraining or fine-tuning large models. The ICD-9 improvement is not statistically significant, and on a matched MIMIC-IV cohort the memory shifts ICD-10 coding toward higher precision at a recall cost, leaving F1 statistically unchanged; applying the same memory to 1,000 held-out MIMIC-III notes maintains a stable ICD operating point. The authors caution that absolute CPT/HCPCS performance remains low and that the system was evaluated retrospectively rather than in clinical deployment.

## [SAIGE Paper Questions When Multi-Agent Collaboration Actually Helps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19759) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.19759) systematically analyzes the capability boundaries of multi-agent collaboration versus single-agent harnesses, finding that multi-agent setups only provide systematic benefits in long-horizon tasks with sparse dependencies. The authors introduce SAIGE (Semantic-Aware Incremental Graph Evolution), a lightweight collaboration mechanism that models collaboration as a dynamically evolving graph where nodes are on-demand agent instances and edges encode semantic dependencies established via content-based information retrieval. The paper challenges the widely held assumption that adding more agents always improves system intelligence, showing that multi-agent superiority is bounded by task structure rather than universal. This has direct implications for how practitioners design autonomous agent systems, potentially saving significant context overhead and compute by choosing single-agent harnesses for tightly coupled sequential workflows. SAIGE models collaboration as a dynamically evolving graph with on-demand agent nodes and semantic dependency edges, and experiments on long-horizon complex task benchmarks show it achieves a favorable trade-off between context efficiency and task performance. Notably, the authors find that scaling the agent pool or deepening recursion levels does not consistently improve outcomes, underscoring diminishing returns.

**Background**: Large language models have increasingly been deployed as autonomous agents, often wrapped in an "agent harness" — the execution scaffolding that manages tools, context, and control flow. As individual agent capabilities scale, a common intuition is that combining multiple agents in a collaborative system should yield better results, but this introduces growing context overhead and coordination costs. The paper formalizes when this intuition holds and when it fails, and proposes a graph-based mechanism to structure collaboration more efficiently.

## [Adversarial Social Media Signals Can Poison LLM Multi-Agent Trading Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19789) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.19789) introduces GMATS, a framework modeling LLM-based multi-agent trading architectures, and demonstrates that black-box, input-only attackers injecting budget-constrained social-media content can materially degrade risk-return profiles, sharply reducing Sharpe ratios. The authors define contagion metrics — belief-shift scores at analyst and coordinator layers plus attack-clean deltas on backtest metrics — and show that well-designed multi-agent topologies and coordinator prompts can dampen these adversarial shocks. As LLM-based multi-agent trading systems move from prototypes into quantitative finance, their unknown robustness to adversarial inputs becomes a systemic risk concern; this work provides a reusable stress-testing protocol and shows that simple social-media poisoning can undermine deployed trading stacks. It matters to AI security researchers, quant developers, and regulators evaluating the safety of autonomous financial agents. The attack is strictly black-box and input-only, entering solely through admissible social-media feeds where an LLM acts as a post generator, so no model weights or gradients are needed. Experiments use a safe offline benchmark with historical market and social data, and the authors report that suitably designed multi-agent topologies and coordinator prompts improve average robustness under identical poisoning budgets.

**Background**: Multi-agent trading systems built on large language models split responsibilities across specialized agents — such as analysts, coordinators, and execution modules — that communicate to make trading decisions. Because these agents consume external text like news and social media as evidence, an attacker who controls part of that input stream can potentially influence downstream beliefs and trades. Contagion metrics are a way to quantify how such adversarial content propagates from one agent layer to another and ultimately affects portfolio performance.

## [Reference Policy as a Knob for Nash Equilibrium Selection in Regularized Self-Play](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19820) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.19820) shows that in regularized self-play, anchoring the entropy-regularized reference policy at a desired member of a value-equivalent Nash equilibrium polytope and then refining steers learning to that specific equilibrium. Across five exactly solvable games plus a 2-D polytope, the method achieves mean coordinate error of 0.007 at median exploitability of 5×10⁻⁵, with selection following the reach-weighted I-projection (slope 0.969). This reframes the KL anchor used in RLHF-style reinforcement learning as a deliberate selection knob rather than merely a stability leash, which matters for multi-agent RL and game-theoretic alignment where games often have many value-equivalent equilibria. It gives researchers a principled way to pick which equilibrium a self-play agent converges to, though the benefit only materializes against fixed, non-equilibrium opponents. The paper reports with equal emphasis where the method breaks: fixed off-manifold references cost 0.08–0.25 exploitability, stiff or flat regularizer families require a smaller mirror step set by a pre-registered rule, and boundary targets undershoot with curvature predicting boundary saturation (rank correlation 0.90, p=0.037). Matched control arms show attention's robust signature is excess seed variance, with any systematic shift bounded at 0.018 and not significant, and the selection-robustness trade-off is degenerate against a best-responding opponent.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Regularized self-play is the family of methods behind DeepNash's expert-level Stratego play, in which a two-player zero-sum policy is driven toward a Nash equilibrium by best-responding to a slowly moving, entropy-regularized reference policy ρ. When a game has a polytope of value-equivalent equilibria, the regularizer silently breaks the tie — a uniform reference selects the maximum-entropy member, which is the I-projection of ρ onto the Nash set. This work asks whether that reference can instead be used to choose the equilibrium on purpose, connecting to RLHF-style KL regularization and information projection concepts.

## [BATON: Dual-Axis Policy Optimization for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19830) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.19830) introduces BATON, a dual-axis policy optimization framework for LLM agents that combines Bayesian Feedback Attribution (BFA) for intra-trajectory credit assignment and Trajectory Mass Normalization (TMN) for inter-trajectory objective aggregation. Experiments with GRPO and GiGPO on ALFWorld, WebShop, and SearchQA show that each axis yields independent gains and their combination achieves the strongest overall performance across model scales. Reinforcement learning for LLM agents is increasingly important for training tool-using and long-horizon reasoning systems, and BATON offers a general, algorithm-agnostic way to improve credit assignment and batch aggregation. Its demonstrated gains with both GRPO and GiGPO suggest the method could be broadly adopted by researchers and practitioners working on agent RL. BATON decomposes agent RL into two orthogonal dimensions: Intra-Trajectory Feedback Attribution, addressed by constructing a feedback-conditioned posterior over sampled actions, and Inter-Trajectory Objective Aggregation, addressed by assigning equal optimization mass to complete trajectories. The paper reports that both axes provide independent gains and that their combination is consistently strongest across model scales, though no specific numerical improvements are given in the abstract.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Reinforcement learning (RL) is a machine learning paradigm where an agent learns to make decisions by interacting with an environment to maximize cumulative reward. Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) is a critic-free RL algorithm that compares groups of sampled responses, and Group-in-Group Policy Optimization (GiGPO) extends it with fine-grained, step-level credit assignment for long-horizon LLM agent tasks. BATON builds on these group-based methods by separately addressing how feedback is used within a single trajectory and how whole trajectories are weighted across a batch.

## [LLM GUI Agents Are Vulnerable to Digital Nudges, Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19843) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.19843) empirically tests whether LLM-based GUI agents are susceptible to digital nudges, running a randomized online shopping experiment with 3,600 agents and 21,600 simulations across six frontier models from three providers. It finds agents vulnerable to both automatic (Type 1) and reflective (Type 2) nudges, with reasoning configuration reducing susceptibility to default nudges but increasing it to social influence nudges. The findings suggest that adding more reasoning to agents does not make them more robust to manipulation but merely redirects how interface design influences their choices, which matters for AI safety and for organizations delegating decisions to autonomous agents. It positions interface design as a governance concern rather than a purely cosmetic UX matter. The study used a randomized online shopping setup with 3,600 agents and 21,600 total simulations across six frontier models from three providers, and an exploratory analysis showed the redirection effect is systematically structured by model scale. As a preprint without community discussion yet, its impact has not been independently validated.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: LLM-based GUI agents are autonomous systems that use large language models, often multimodal or visual LLMs, as their cognitive engine to perceive and act on graphical user interfaces in a human-like way. Digital nudges are behavioral interventions built into user-interface design elements, such as default settings or social cues, that steer users' decisions. Dual-Process Theory distinguishes fast, automatic Type 1 thinking from slow, effortful Type 2 reasoning, and this paper applies that framework to agent behavior.

## [Reproducible LLM measures can still fail construct validity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19866) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.19866) uses data from the European Commission's AI Act consultation to show that LLM annotations of stakeholder submissions are highly reproducible (intraclass correlations above 0.99) yet converge poorly with survey-reported measures of the same nominal construct. Divergence varies systematically by stakeholder group, with business associations showing greater concern about AI risks in text than in surveys (g = +1.0), and divergences exhibit positive spatial autocorrelation across European countries (Moran's I = 0.347, p = 0.036). The findings challenge the common practice of treating high inter-annotator or test-retest reproducibility as sufficient evidence that an LLM-based measure is valid, which matters for social scientists, AI evaluators, and regulators who increasingly rely on LLMs to code text at scale. It implies that validation pipelines should separately assess reproducibility, construct validity, and variation tied to communication context, especially when measuring politically sensitive attitudes such as AI risk concern. The study links structured survey responses to free-text consultation submissions from the same stakeholders, so the survey serves as an external criterion for the construct the LLM was meant to approximate. Even at very high reproducibility, the LLM-based text measures diverged from survey measures, and the direction and size of divergence depended on stakeholder type and geography; notably, survey-reported concerns remained strongly associated with support for explainability across all divergence levels.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Construct validity asks whether a measurement actually captures the concept it claims to measure, while reproducibility (often indexed by intraclass correlation, or ICC) asks whether repeated measurements agree with one another. LLMs are increasingly used as measurement instruments to annotate or score large text corpora, and the EU AI Act consultation gathered both survey responses and free-text submissions from stakeholders such as business associations, public authorities, and civil society groups. This paper tests whether the two properties — reproducibility and construct validity — can come apart in a real regulatory dataset.

## [Bank-scale semantic user profiling shifts LLM inference from per-user to per-pattern](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19928) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.19928) presents a deployed three-phase pipeline — Resolve, Profile, and Tag — that re-casts attribute inference from per-user to per-transaction-pattern, so a single LLM call per frequent pattern emits categorical labels, free-text attributes, and per-attribute prevalence estimates. On the public Open e-commerce corpus the resulting database is statistically indistinguishable in AUC from an LLM reading each user's raw history, and the pipeline is deployed at a major Japanese bank profiling tens of millions of users with roughly a three-order-of-magnitude reduction in LLM inference targets. This work addresses the core scalability bottleneck of applying LLMs to financial and e-commerce user data, where per-user inference costs grow linearly with user count and become prohibitive at bank scale. By decoupling inference cost from user count, it makes semantic profiling practical for large institutions while preserving accuracy, and the public code release lowers the barrier for others to reproduce or adapt the approach. The pipeline runs in three phases: Resolve abstracts item names with optional web grounding, Profile infers attributes for each frequent pattern, and Tag clusters free-text attributes into a queryable database. Because inference runs over patterns rather than users, the budget grows with pattern count rather than user count, and the prevalence estimates emitted per pattern carry discriminative signal between positive and negative users; the code is available at github.com/CyberAgentAILab/profiling-agent-open-ecommerce.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Attribute inference is the task of deducing user characteristics (such as demographics or preferences) from behavioral data, and prior work has shown LLMs can infer such attributes from language and transaction patterns. Traditional per-user pipelines feed each user's full history into an LLM, which is accurate but scales poorly because cost is proportional to the number of users. This paper instead groups users by frequent transaction patterns and profiles the patterns themselves, then maps users to those patterns, trading a small amount of individual granularity for a large reduction in inference volume.

## [Paper Critiques Depth Truncation Metric, Proposes Depth Control Protocol](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19934) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.19934) argues that depth truncation, the standard metric for evaluating depth utilization in depth-recurrent language models, is fundamentally flawed because it conflates three distinct effects: fewer block applications, reduced distinct computation, and an out-of-distribution readout head. The authors introduce the Depth Control Protocol (DCP), a diagnostic suite with three positive controls, a negative control on dense transformers, and a controlled training intervention to disentangle these factors. If adopted, DCP could change how researchers measure whether depth-recurrent models genuinely benefit from their recurrent depth, affecting architecture design and efficiency claims across the LLM community. The critique also highlights a broader methodological risk in evaluating efficient inference techniques with cheap, training-free interventions. The linchpin control—running the full budget of block applications while executing only a single distinct iteration—is strictly realizable only in depth-wise weight-sharing architectures, since repeating a layer in a dense network yields a different model rather than the same model in an alternative configuration. DCP also includes a negative control applying identical interventions to dense transformers to rule out measurement artifacts.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Depth-recurrent language models iteratively apply a small shared layer stack, decoupling per-token compute from the number of distinct parameters, which allows flexible compute scaling at inference time. To check whether such models actually use their depth, prior work truncates depth at inference, plots quality against retained depth fraction, and reads off the slope—a cheap, training-free metric. This paper argues that slope conflates multiple simultaneous changes and proposes a controlled protocol to isolate them.

## [Study Characterizes Resource Dynamics of LLM-Based AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19947) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.19947) empirically characterizes latency, local resource demand, and container bottlenecks for LLM-based AI agents across three representative tasks: retrieval-augmented question answering, web search, and software coding. The authors show that even the same tool can exhibit substantially different resource dynamics depending on the task, and they propose CPU-aware tool admission and task-aware CPU allocation, which improve CPU-sensitive task latency by about 5.4x and reduce average multi-task latency by roughly 32%. Agent serving is a fast-growing but poorly understood systems problem, and this work shows that naive assumptions such as 'faster LLM responses or more CPU cores always help' are wrong. The findings give systems and ML engineers concrete guidance for scheduling, admission control, and resource allocation when deploying agent workloads, which could reduce significant resource waste in current agent ecosystems. The study measures concurrent execution of multiple requests and tasks, exposing task-dependent bottlenecks in CPU, disk I/O, and memory, and notes that faster LLM responses or additional CPU cores do not always accelerate agents. The proposed optimizations are CPU-aware tool admission and task-aware CPU allocation, evaluated against native agent baselines.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: LLM-based AI agents typically work by iteratively reasoning and calling tools, often combining remote LLM APIs with local tool containers. This mixed execution model makes it hard to optimize serving because latency, local resource usage, and container bottlenecks interact across requests. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is a common pattern in which a retriever fetches relevant documents and an LLM generates an answer from them, while container-based sandboxes are widely used to isolate agent tool execution.

## [Data Attribution Fails to Reliably Filter Subliminal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20027) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.20027) evaluates three gradient-based data attribution methods — GradCos, a contrastive GradCos variant, and EK-FAC — across three language models for filtering subliminal learning. It finds that EK-FAC partially mitigates the effect at the token level, while the other methods provide little benefit, and all methods mostly fall short of divergence tokens, a strong baseline that requires access to counterfactual teacher models. This matters for AI safety because subliminal learning lets models transmit behavioral traits through semantically unrelated data, defeating content-based filtering; if data attribution cannot reliably identify the responsible examples, it cannot serve as a robust safety intervention. The inconsistent success across methods and settings also warns practitioners against assuming any single attribution technique is dependable. Filtering at the token level is more effective than filtering entire samples for every method, though EK-FAC often gives a stronger signal than divergence tokens in the sample-level setting. Success is inconsistent across model-preference combinations, and the authors do not identify a consistent explanation for these differences, suggesting some approximations are more reliable than others.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Subliminal learning is a recently documented phenomenon in which a teacher model with a trait (such as liking owls or being misaligned) generates data that is semantically unrelated to that trait — for example, only number sequences — yet a student model trained on that data still acquires the trait. Because the data contains no obvious semantic signal, content-based filtering cannot catch it. Training data attribution offers an alternative by tracing a model behavior back to the training examples responsible for it, independent of semantic content, using gradient-based approximations such as EK-FAC (a Kronecker-factored approximate curvature method) to estimate influence. Divergence tokens are a previously proposed baseline that localizes subliminal learning but requires access to counterfactual teacher models.
