## [CoreSense: Auditable Robot Decisions via Failure Recall and Belief Gating](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19512) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers present CoreSense, a robot-system integration architecture that combines traceable episodic evidence with a conflict-aware belief gate to produce bounded, auditable recommendations. On CableTrace-120 and BotFails-200, belief gating reduces protocol-defined unsafe proceeds from 20% and 40% to 0%, and a 20/20 cloud recall test validates a CockroachDB Cloud–Amazon Bedrock deployment path. Robot safety failures often stem from acting on stale or contradictory memories, so a gate that checks scope, provenance, time, contradiction, and support before allowing PROCEED could make autonomous systems more trustworthy. The work targets auditable integration rather than certified safety, which matters for operators who need to explain and review robot decisions in regulated or high-stakes environments. The gate can PROCEED, request re-observation, abstain, or escalate, and evaluation spans offline real-robot data, a frozen signal-level simulation, and a live cloud deployment path without commanding a physical robot. A ViFailback-BotFails visual detector reaches 0.778 AUROC but remains all-blocking, while cycle-disjoint UR3 telemetry yields 0% unsafe proceed with 36.1% overblocking and 61.9% coverage; grip-loss transfer remains a negative result.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Episodic memory in robotics lets a robot store and later recall specific past experiences, but recalled evidence may be outdated, conflict with current sensor observations, or be insufficient to justify an action. Belief gating is a decision-layer mechanism that evaluates whether recalled evidence is trustworthy enough to act on, and auditability means the system can trace and justify why a decision was made. CoreSense builds on these ideas by adding traceable provenance and bounded recommendations to the recall-and-act loop.

## [AURORA: Natural-Language Agentic Framework for Verifiable Air-Ground Co-Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19527) ⭐️ 7.0/10

AURORA is a natural-language-driven agentic framework that treats air-ground scenario generation as a process of compilation with verification, centered on the Air-Ground Scenario Graph (AGSG), a typed intermediate representation linking agents, aerial missions, events, communication links, success conditions, and cross-domain dependencies. The paper also introduces AURORA-Bench, which evaluates whether generated scenarios faithfully realize requested interactions rather than merely executing, and experiments across multiple language models show structured execution substantially improves reliability while runtime verification exposes silent failures that completion-based evaluation overlooks. Air-ground transportation research increasingly depends on co-simulation, but constructing and validating scenarios remains labor-intensive, and a scenario can execute successfully while failing to realize the spatial, temporal, communication, or behavioral relationships the user requested. AURORA's verification-driven approach and AURORA-Bench benchmark could improve the reliability of language-driven simulation workflows, benefiting researchers in autonomous aerial and ground robotics who rely on trustworthy scenario generation. The AGSG intermediate representation enables simulator-grounded parsing, joint road-airspace grounding, temporal planning, pre-execution feasibility checking, trace-based runtime verification, failure localization, and bounded repair within a unified workflow. Localized repair resolves many violations without regenerating the entire scenario, and the results argue that reliable scenario generation requires verifying realized behavior, not merely executable code.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Co-simulation combines multiple simulators—such as road traffic and aerial vehicle simulators—to study air-ground systems together, but building scenarios that correctly capture cross-domain interactions is difficult. A typed intermediate representation is a normalized data model created before code generation that resolves raw input into explicit types, relationships, and metadata, similar to how compilers use intermediate representations like LLVM IR. AURORA applies this compilation analogy to natural-language scenario generation, adding verification and repair steps to catch mismatches between what a user asks for and what actually runs.

## [PaRID: LLM-Tuned Instruction Duplication for Soft Error Detection in Parallel Software](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19531) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose PaRID (PaRallel Instruction Duplication), a compile-time software-directed framework that detects soft errors in multithreaded programs by combining parallel-aware code transformation with LLM-tuned performance modeling. Evaluated on NAS Parallel Benchmarks, PaRID cuts average protection overhead from 162.79% to 59.84% and achieves up to 5x speedup while preserving full error detection effectiveness. Soft errors caused by cosmic radiation or hardware faults can silently corrupt data in long-running parallel applications, and traditional instruction duplication imposes prohibitive overhead. By making protection roughly 2.7x cheaper without dynamic profiling, PaRID could make fault tolerance practical for multithreaded workloads in HPC, cloud, and safety-critical systems. PaRID handles programs with mixed serial and parallel regions and avoids costly dynamic profiling by using eight generalizable findings from an offline characterization study to guide LLM-tuned performance modeling. The evaluation is limited to NPB benchmarks, and as an arXiv preprint its results have not yet undergone peer review or validation on broader real-world workloads.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: A soft error is a transient fault, often triggered by cosmic radiation or electrical noise, that flips a bit in memory or a register without permanently damaging hardware; it can silently corrupt an instruction or data value. Instruction duplication is a classic software fault-tolerance technique that executes each instruction twice and compares results, but it typically doubles execution time and is complicated in parallel programs where threads interact. LLM-tuned performance modeling means using a large language model to help predict and optimize the cost of the duplicated code, replacing expensive runtime profiling.

## [Compressed Active Subspaces Enable Scalable Bayesian Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19539) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.19539) proposes Compressed Active Subspaces (CAS), a method that first maps model parameters into a compressed space via a structured isometric embedding and then constructs the active subspace within that reduced parameterization. This substantially cuts the memory needed to store full-dimensional gradients, allowing Bayesian inference on neural networks of increasing size while preserving predictive performance and robust uncertainty estimates. Active subspace methods are a practical route to uncertainty quantification, but their memory cost has blocked their use on large models; CAS removes that bottleneck and makes Bayesian uncertainty estimates feasible for bigger neural networks. This matters for researchers in scalable Bayesian inference and for any application where knowing how confident a large model is matters as much as its predictions. The core technical move is a structured isometric embedding that preserves distances while compressing the parameter space, so the active subspace is built in the reduced space rather than from many full-dimensional gradients. The paper reports experiments on neural networks of increasing size, showing maintained predictive performance and robust uncertainty estimates, though it remains a specialized preprint without peer review or released code mentioned.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Active subspace methods are a gradient-based dimension reduction technique: they identify the directions in the input or parameter space along which a model output varies most, typically via eigenvalue decomposition of the gradient covariance matrix, and then perform inference only along those dominant directions. This works well for high-dimensional models but requires storing many full-dimensional gradients, which becomes prohibitive as models grow. Bayesian inference, meanwhile, seeks to quantify predictive uncertainty by treating model parameters as probability distributions rather than fixed values, and it is computationally demanding for large models. CAS combines these ideas by doing the active-subspace construction inside a compressed parameterization.

Researchers present CliniCIRCA, a multi-stage LLM framework for Calendar-anchored, Imprecision-aware Reconstruction of Clinical Annals, which temporally classifies clinical events across unstructured discharge summaries without event-level timestamps. From 14,882 MIMIC-III mental health admissions, they built a benchmark of 52 discharge summaries producing 15,891 temporally tagged events, corrected 629 errors via clinician-in-the-loop evaluation to create verified gold-standard labels, and scaled the framework to generate 1,000 silver-standard timelines for training evaluation. This work addresses a core challenge in clinical NLP: recovering the temporal progression of biological, psychological, and social events that are scattered across unstructured mental health narratives, which is essential for reasoning over patient journeys. By combining clinician-in-the-loop verification with instruction tuning across five open-weight models, it offers a scalable path toward training data and evaluation resources for longitudinal mental health record analysis. The framework compresses each source 1.52 times into a date-grouped chronological record during a temporally grounded summarization stage, and instruction tuning generally improved five open-weight models on event extraction, temporal tagging, and summarization compared with zero- and few-shot prompting. The benchmark is small (52 discharge summaries) and the paper is a preprint without community discussion yet, so broader generalizability remains to be established.

## [Gradient-Based Data Attribution Tracks Answer Format, Not Task Content](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19589) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.19589) resolves a debate about what gradient similarity measures in data attribution for supervised fine-tuning by independently varying task and answer format. It finds that benchmark pairs sharing an answer format align strongly (disattenuated cosine near 0.4), while the same benchmarks rendered in different answer formats show essentially no alignment (near 0.0), and this ordering holds from early pretraining checkpoints through post-training across model scales and families. This finding challenges the common interpretation that gradient similarity identifies task-relevant skills, suggesting instead that it largely captures surface-level answer formatting. It has direct implications for gradient-based data selection methods like LESS and for how researchers should interpret gradient semantics, potentially affecting the reliability of data attribution for LLM training. The study analyzes the released selections of LESS, a gradient-based data selection method for instruction tuning, and finds that each target's selections over-represent the target's own answer format. The authors recommend that gradient-based attribution methods and semantic interpretations of gradients be tested on data where answer format and task vary independently for greater robustness.

**Background**: Data attribution methods using gradient similarity, such as influence functions, are widely used to analyze and select training data for large language models without retraining. Supervised fine-tuning trains models on labeled input-output pairs, often question-answer or instruction-following data. However, what gradient similarity actually measures has been debated: some interpret it as identifying task-relevant skills, while others report that surface form dominates. This paper resolves that debate for supervised fine-tuning by independently varying task and answer format.

## [DeltaSelect Enables Affordable A/B Testing for Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19607) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.19607) introduces DeltaSelect, an open-source method that selects a small fixed set of coding-agent benchmark tasks whose one-run results reliably track full-benchmark performance, using Pearson correlation and linear regression within a dollar budget. In a case study on gpt-5.6-luna low-reasoning, 13 evaluations cost USD 27.86, and the adopted version cut cost by 58.1% (USD 1.75 vs USD 4.18, p=0.008) while raising the calibrated score from 36.46% to 42.36%. Coding-agent benchmarks are designed for broad comparisons, not the frequent baseline-versus-candidate decisions developers make daily, and full suites are expensive and noisy. DeltaSelect gives practitioners a budget-aware way to run repeated A/B tests during development, potentially speeding up iteration on agent skills and instructions without sacrificing signal quality. In a resampling analysis of DeepSWE's published trials, only 19.5% of tasks (22 of 113) had a fifth-percentile Pearson correlation of at least 0.50 with full-benchmark performance, showing most tasks are poor proxies. DeltaSelect maps fractional verifier results to a common score via linear regression and is explicitly intended for repeated baseline-versus-candidate comparisons, not model rankings.

**Background**: Coding-agent benchmarks such as DeepSWE evaluate frontier AI agents on long-horizon software engineering tasks across languages like TypeScript, Go, Python, JavaScript, and Rust. Because individual runs vary and full suites are costly, developers often cannot afford to rerun them for every small change. Pearson correlation measures the linear relationship between two variables on a scale from -1 to 1, and resampling methods like bootstrapping estimate how stable a statistic is by repeatedly drawing from the observed data.

## [TacSushi: Tactile World-Action Model for Dexterous Sushi Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19613) ⭐️ 7.0/10

TacSushi introduces a tactile-grounded, Cosmos3-based world-action policy that learns from recorded future consequences while acting only on current observations, with a training-only decoder that predicts future visual observations, task progress, contact risk, and tactile summaries. Trained on 340 successful and 50 failed real-robot trials, it achieves 68.3% average in-distribution success and 37.5% out-of-distribution success, versus 36.7%/10.0% without future-consequence supervision and 25.0%/17.5% with direct tactile concatenation instead of gated fusion. This work shows that training-time predictive supervision and feature-wise gated tactile fusion can substantially improve dexterous manipulation of deformable food under occlusion and uncertain contact, a setting where vision alone is unreliable. It is relevant to robotics and embodied AI researchers working on world models, tactile sensing, and real-robot policy learning, and it offers a concrete recipe for exploiting failed trials without imitating them. The backbone encodes current RGB, language, and hand state, while fingertip tactile features are incorporated into the action representation through feature-wise gated fusion; the predictive decoder is removed at deployment. Failed trials contribute consequence supervision but their actions are excluded from imitation, and terminal outcomes are scored by an anchored visual-quality protocol that equally weights five human ratings and three vision-language-model ratings per rollout.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Dexterous food manipulation is hard because ingredients deform, contacts are uncertain, and the hand often occludes the object, making pure vision insufficient. World-action models learn a predictive model of how the world evolves and use it to guide action, while tactile sensing provides direct contact information that vision cannot capture. Cosmos3 is a family of omnimodal world models designed to jointly process and generate language, image, video, audio, and action sequences within a unified mixture-of-transformers architecture, and TacSushi builds its policy on this backbone.

A new arXiv preprint (2609.19717) proposes Abstract Token Curriculum (ATC), a curriculum learning framework that elicits continuous intermediate 'thought' representations in LLMs without explicit chain-of-thought supervision or manually designed scratchpads. The authors provide theoretical analysis showing that, for learning parity functions with single-layer softmax attention, attention naturally focuses on the CoT tokens that offer the 'easiest path' to predicting the next token, and they demonstrate ATC's effectiveness on graph reachability and arithmetic tasks. Chain-of-thought prompting has become the dominant way to improve LLM reasoning, but it depends on large amounts of task-specific supervision and hand-crafted scratchpads, which limits scalability. ATC points toward a more autonomous form of reasoning in which models develop internal continuous thoughts on their own, potentially reducing data and engineering costs while connecting to broader interest in latent-space reasoning such as Meta AI's COCONUT. The theoretical result is limited to a specific setting—learning parity functions with single-layer softmax attention—so it is not yet a general proof for arbitrary architectures or tasks. Experiments are confined to graph reachability and arithmetic learning, and the abstract is truncated, so the full empirical scope and comparisons with prior continuous-thought methods remain to be assessed.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting asks a model to write out intermediate reasoning steps as text before giving a final answer, which improves accuracy on math and logic tasks but requires supervised 'thinking tokens' and task-specific data. Curriculum learning is a training strategy that presents examples in increasing order of difficulty, and continuous or latent thought representations encode reasoning in vector space rather than as discrete text tokens. ATC combines these ideas: it gradually increases problem complexity so the model learns abstract thoughts without explicit CoT labels.

## [Single-Budget Evaluation of LLM Evolutionary Search Is Misleading](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19799) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.19799) evaluates three LLM-driven evolutionary search strategies across five optimization tasks using a full grid of seed counts and iteration counts, rather than the single budget setting typically reported in the literature. It finds that the optimal split between seeds (width) and iterations (depth) depends on the strategy, the task, and the total budget, and that strategy rankings themselves flip with budget changes. The paper challenges a widespread methodological practice in LLM-driven evolutionary search, where methods are ranked from a single seed-and-iteration configuration. Because rankings can reverse depending on budget, prior comparisons may be unreliable, and the authors propose a measurement protocol reporting the seeds-by-iterations frontier to make evaluations more robust. On one task, the strategy that appears worst with a single seed becomes the best when run with forty seeds, and on another task the optimal number of iterations is well below the value commonly used in practice, meaning extra depth wastes budget that more seeds would convert into better scores. The authors provide practical guidance for using the seeds-by-iterations frontier as a reporting standard.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: LLM-driven evolutionary search is a method where a large language model generates candidate programs or solutions, then iteratively refines them in an evolutionary loop, much like classic evolutionary algorithms that mimic natural selection. In practice, researchers launch several independent "seeds" (parallel starting runs) and apply a fixed number of "iterations" (refinement steps) to each, but papers usually report results from just one such budget configuration. This paper argues that this single-point evaluation hides how performance actually depends on the width-versus-depth trade-off.

## [CoRELoop Boosts Audio Deepfake Detection via Parameter-Efficient Recurrent Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19818) ⭐️ 7.0/10

CoRELoop introduces a parameter-efficient recurrent refinement method that reuses the outputs of a frozen self-supervised learning (SSL) based audio deepfake detector to improve cross-domain generalization. On 14 cross-domain test sets, a 24-layer model reduces pooled equal error rate (EER) from 4.85% to 3.74% with two passes, training only about 10M parameters out of 598M. Audio deepfake detection models often fail on unseen attacks, and collecting training data for every possible attack is impractical. CoRELoop shows that significant generalization gains can be achieved without retraining the entire detector or adding new data, making it a practical and resource-efficient solution for the audio security community. The method adapts recurrent inputs to the frozen encoder, controls state updates, and aligns refined outputs with the frozen classifier, preserving the original first-pass prediction. An optional halting head can selectively choose the refinement depth per utterance, achieving 3.73% pooled EER with an average of 1.18 passes.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Self-supervised learning (SSL) models for speech, such as wav2vec 2.0 and HuBERT, learn general-purpose representations from large amounts of unlabeled audio and are widely used as feature extractors in audio deepfake detection. Low-rank adapters (e.g., LoRA) are a parameter-efficient fine-tuning technique that freezes the pre-trained model and injects small trainable matrices into its layers. Equal error rate (EER) is the standard metric for audio deepfake detection, representing the point where false acceptance and false rejection rates are equal; lower EER indicates better performance.

## [AI-Generated RTL Verification Plans Fail Semantic Validation Despite Schema Acceptance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19844) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.19844) presents SecTB-RTL, an auditable framework with 31 tasks and 124 authored hardware-security regressions, in which a confirmatory run completed 1,860 calls and the provider accepted 1,857 responses, but only nine passed the production semantic validator. The authors preserve the run as an instrument-validation incident and report no prompt-effect estimate, concluding that provider or schema acceptance does not establish execution validity. This finding is a striking, actionable insight for researchers and practitioners in hardware security and AI-assisted verification, because it challenges the common assumption that schema-valid AI outputs are safe to execute in EDA workflows. It underscores the need for full production-path validation of saved artifacts rather than relying on compilation or coverage diagnostics. A deterministic non-AI baseline killed 36, 75, and 78 mutants at increasing resource limits, and the first confirmatory run (C1-R2) failed before model execution because the provider rejected its response schema. After a schema-only repair made without viewing outcomes, a separately frozen follow-up run (C1-R3) completed 1,860 calls, but the generation and execution rules did not match, and a subsequent follow-up was excluded for failing the preregistered evidence-completeness gate.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: RTL (register-transfer level) verification plans are used in chip design to check that hardware behaves correctly, and hardware-security regression tests are automated tests that prevent previously fixed security flaws from being reintroduced. Mutation killing is a metric where injected faults (mutants) are used to measure how many defects a verification testbench can detect. Large language models are increasingly used to generate such verification plans, but this paper shows that passing a provider's schema check is not the same as passing a semantic validator that ensures the plan can actually execute.

## [Action-Similarity Supervision Boosts Cross-Embodiment Transfer in Latent Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19846) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.19846) introduces action-similarity supervision for latent action models (LAMs), training the similarity between any two latent actions to match the similarity of their corresponding ground-truth robot action sequences rather than predicting those actions directly. Evaluated on RoboTwin 2.0 with two bimanual robots demonstrating disjoint task sets, the approach more than doubles cross-embodiment success compared to predicting latent actions instead of ground-truth actions, and outperforms an auxiliary loss that predicts ground-truth actions during LAM training. Cross-embodiment transfer is a central bottleneck for scaling robot learning, since demonstrations are costly and tied to the robot that recorded them. This work shows that reusing the same action labels for similarity supervision, rather than direct prediction, yields a latent action space that is less embodiment-specific and transfers better, which could make LAMs more practical for sharing data across different robot platforms. The best configuration computes similarities on end-effector motion rather than joint-space motion and lets the loss compare latent actions across the two robots; the study fixes the policy architecture, hyperparameters, dataset, and evaluation protocol to isolate the effect of the supervision signal. Because ground-truth actions are never predicted, the latent action does not need to encode embodiment specifics, though the method still relies on having paired ground-truth action labels during LAM training.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Latent action models (LAMs) learn compact latent actions from action-free videos so that knowledge can be shared across embodiments, but in practice they are sensitive to background visual noise and may encode the same motion from two different robots with different latents. A common fix is an auxiliary loss that predicts the robot action from the latent action, which ties the latent space to the embodiment-specific action space. Cross-embodiment transfer refers to reusing policies, skill representations, or demonstrations across robots with different morphologies, sensor modalities, and dynamics, and is essential for scaling robot learning beyond per-robot data collection.

## [PACE: A Typed Spec and Compiler for Script-Grounded AI Previsualization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19853) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PACE (Precise AI Cinematic Expression) introduces a typed representation that captures screenplay evidence — characters, props, locations, subject positions, and camera behavior — and a compiler that converts it into both a diffusion-model prompt and a metric 3D scene, with a camera solver placing the camera so the declared framing matches the built framing. On the 11-scene Automatic Drive screenplay, every staged single-subject panel places its subject within 1.2% of frame width of the declared position, and declaring pose on 30 shots raised the described action drawn from 58.9% to 74.4% without moving the framing. AI filmmaking tools typically let a diffusion model settle spatial planning by its own defaults, which makes camera placement and staging hard to control or verify; PACE instead makes the plan explicit, measurable, and inheritable across script, scene, shot, and panel levels. This matters for directors, previs artists, and VFX pipelines that need reproducible, geometrically conformant shots rather than prompt roulette. The system reports residuals rather than absorbing them: with two or three subjects, one camera pose cannot satisfy every declared position, so the mismatch is measured field by field. On 204 external director-storyboard shots, delivered head height was 1.906 times the staged target from the director's words, 1.733 from the compiled prompt, and 0.955 with the greybox control, showing that the condition holding framing best draws the described action least; transitions, fitted motion, and human review of generated panels remain open.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Previsualization (previs) is the pre-production practice of visualizing scenes, camera angles, and staging before shooting, traditionally via storyboards and increasingly via 3D simulation. Diffusion models are generative models that produce images from text prompts but have no inherent notion of metric space or camera geometry, while camera solving (match moving) infers a camera's motion and characteristics to integrate CGI with live-action footage. PACE sits at the intersection of these fields, giving screenplays a typed, metric intermediate representation that can drive both image generation and 3D staging.

## [Zarya: Hybrid Autoregressive-Masked Diffusion Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19868) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Zarya is a family of hybrid language models that jointly optimizes an autoregressive (AR) objective and a masked-diffusion objective within a single architecture, released publicly in sizes 0.6B, 1.7B, and 4B. It structures training data into variable-size slots with a curriculum that gradually increases slot granularity, and offers two inference modes: MDM sampling with first-hitting denoising and slotted speculative decoding with full KV cache reuse. This work addresses two key limitations of existing approaches: the sequential, left-to-right constraint of autoregressive models and the high computational overhead and incoherent generation of masked diffusion models. By decoupling training and inference regimes and enabling dual-mode deployment, Zarya could influence future language model design and offer more flexible efficiency-quality trade-offs. The curriculum-based training smoothly transitions from fine-grained AR learning to coarse-grained diffusion learning, and the slotted speculative decoding interleaves inter-slot diffusion-based selection with intra-slot autoregressive infilling. Extensive configurability includes grouped noise patterns (Prefix Completion, Fill-In-the-Prefix, Fill-In-the-Middle), ordered sampling schedules, and noise-level permutation strategies.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Autoregressive language models (ARMs) generate text strictly sequentially from left to right, which limits parallelism, while masked diffusion models (MDMs) enable parallel decoding but suffer from high computational overhead because they cannot reuse the Key-Value (KV) cache and from incoherent generation due to learning dependencies over an intractable space of token combinations. KV cache is a mechanism in autoregressive transformers that stores past attention keys and values to enable efficient token decoding without recomputation, and speculative decoding accelerates LLMs by using a smaller draft model to generate preliminary predictions that the main model confirms or refines. Zarya aims to combine the strengths of both paradigms in a single architecture.

## [PetriBench: A New Scalable Benchmark for LLM Reasoning Over Dynamic State Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19883) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced PetriBench, a compact, fully self-contained, and scalable benchmark that uses Petri nets to evaluate LLM reasoning over dynamic state spaces. It organizes reasoning into four task families with Easy, Medium, and Hard levels generated by increasing structural complexity, and is evaluated against exact ground truth across a diverse set of proprietary and open-weight models. Existing LLM reasoning benchmarks often isolate specific skills, rely on external knowledge, or are costly to extend, so PetriBench offers a unified and extensible setting for probing the strengths, limits, and scaling behavior of LLM reasoning. Its findings of consistent accuracy drops with difficulty and distinct task-specific capability profiles are valuable for AI reasoning and benchmarking research. Accuracy decreases consistently with difficulty, and harder instances expose increasingly distinct task-specific capability profiles. Additional analyses show that test-time compute improves performance but interacts differently with different reasoning tasks, and that procedural generation yields smooth scaling with structural complexity.

A new arXiv paper (2609.19906) studies whether coding agents can treat complete closed-loop robot implementations as reusable execution experience, archiving validation-selected policies and reusing them for new tasks. On four RoboCasa source tasks, iterative optimization raised mean success from 28.3% to 64.2%, and across nine target tasks optimized source-code references achieved 57.0% mean success versus 45.2% with no references and 41.5% with initial source code. This suggests that improved control code itself, not just model weights or demonstrations, can serve as transferable knowledge for robot learning, potentially lowering the cost of acquiring new closed-loop policies. It connects the fast-growing area of coding agents to robotics, where manual design and tuning of observation processing, state management, and branching logic remains expensive. The agent generates policy code from a few successful demonstrations, iteratively improves it with simulation feedback, and the final policy is frozen to execute without further model calls. Optimized references beat initial references in all three independent runs on the nine-task average with a mean gain of 15.6 percentage points, but initial references remained better on two target tasks when averaged across runs.

## [JANUS: Post-hoc Weight Rectification Mitigates Catastrophic Forgetting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19985) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces JANUS, a purely post-hoc and tuning-agnostic weight rectification framework that projects parameter updates into the Jacobian null space to achieve Parameter Space Orthogonality, the necessary and sufficient condition for preserving historical performance to first order. It further proposes a Multi-step Adaptive Rectification mechanism, along with ghost projection, ghost orientation comparison, and sequence-level singular value decomposition compression, to overcome the local validity of the Jacobian approximation while maintaining temporal and spatial efficiency. Catastrophic forgetting is a central obstacle in continual learning and fine-tuning of foundation models, and JANUS offers a theoretically grounded, tuning-agnostic solution that can be layered on top of existing fine-tuning methods without interfering with them. This could make it easier to adapt large models to new tasks while retaining prior capabilities, benefiting researchers and practitioners in continual learning and parameter-efficient fine-tuning. Unlike prior work that relies on an overly restrictive Subspace Orthogonality condition, JANUS enforces Parameter Space Orthogonality via Jacobian null space projection, which is necessary and sufficient for first-order preservation of historical performance. The multi-step adaptive rectification uses the JANUS shift to dynamically verify a valid trust region and adjust step sizes, and the ghost projection, ghost orientation comparison, and sequence-level SVD compression techniques keep the method efficient in time and memory.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Catastrophic forgetting is the tendency of artificial neural networks to abruptly and drastically forget previously learned information upon learning new information, a well-known problem when fine-tuning foundation models on new tasks. Continual learning research seeks to mitigate this, often by enforcing orthogonality between parameter updates for old and new tasks so that new learning does not overwrite old knowledge. Null space projection is a classical technique, originally from robotics, where a vector in the input space is projected so that it produces zero output in the Jacobian, allowing secondary objectives without disturbing the primary one.

## [EPIG-Tree: Compute-Optimal Branching for Policy-Gradient RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20004) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.20004) introduces EPIG-Tree, a compute-allocation strategy for tree-based rollouts in policy-gradient reinforcement learning that uses a law-of-total-variance decomposition of the local policy-gradient random variable to decide where to place branches. It derives two allocation laws — new branches reduce decision uncertainty while repeated suffix rollouts reduce continuation uncertainty — and reports empirical gains including the highest final Wordle win rate of 0.850 versus 0.790 for flat GRPO. Efficient credit assignment and compute allocation are central bottlenecks in reinforcement learning for language models, and EPIG-Tree offers a principled variance-based rule that could influence how future RL training pipelines spend rollout compute. If the gradient-estimation advantage transfers to stateful, large-action settings as claimed, it may improve both training efficiency and final policy quality for LLM post-training. The EPIG-Tree score allocates branches using already computed rollouts, estimating occupancy- and score-weighted value uncertainty together with a suffix law n_e ∝ w_e ‖∇_θ log π(a_e|h_e)‖ σ_e / √c_e. Empirically it reduces gradient MSE in cloned-state control (winning all nine dense continuous-control environments in a 13-environment sweep) and improves frozen-LLM gradient calibration over entropy branching, though in online single-turn math branch placement proved secondary to token-level credit assignment.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) is a reinforcement-learning algorithm for fine-tuning large language models in which completions to the same prompt form a group and advantages are computed relative to other rewards in that group, collapsing an entire stochastic trajectory into a single scalar reward. The law of total variance (also called Eve's law or the conditional variance formula) decomposes total variance into within-group and between-group components, and EPIG-Tree applies this decomposition to the policy-gradient random variable. Tree-based rollouts, such as the LATR method, construct branching trajectories to improve exploration diversity in RL with verifiable rewards.

## [TP-DATE Brings Dynamic Gromov-Wasserstein Optimal Transport to Spatial Transcriptomics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20008) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.20008) introduces Travelling Pair Dynamical Alignment and Trajectory Estimation (TP-DATE), a theoretical and computational framework that generalizes Gromov-Wasserstein optimal transport (GW-OT) to a dynamic setting in a simulation-free manner. The authors formulate a broad class of static and dynamic Quadratic-form OT (QOT) through path actions, prove a static-dynamic equivalence, and develop travelling-pair flow matching that marginalizes interacting conditional paths into a single vector field. Static GW-OT has become a standard tool for structure-aware alignment, but it cannot reconstruct continuous trajectories, which limits its use in dynamic biological processes. TP-DATE fills this gap and, on synthetic and real spatial transcriptomics data, better preserves spatial structure and improves continuous 3D dynamics reconstruction, potentially benefiting researchers in optimal transport and computational biology. The framework is simulation-free, meaning it avoids costly numerical integration of trajectories during training, and it works by marginalizing interacting conditional paths into a single vector field. As a preprint without peer review or community discussion yet, its practical scalability and robustness on larger datasets remain to be validated.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Gromov-Wasserstein optimal transport (GW-OT) extends classical optimal transport by comparing metric measure spaces through their internal structure rather than direct feature correspondence, making it useful when two spaces are not directly comparable. Spatial transcriptomics measures gene expression while preserving the spatial position of transcripts within intact tissue, so aligning such datasets requires matching expression patterns while respecting tissue geometry. Flow matching is a generative modeling approach that learns a vector field transporting one distribution to another, and it underlies the simulation-free trajectory reconstruction proposed here.

## [SERBench and MSS-Complement: Set-Level Evidence Recovery for Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20050) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper formulates state-conditioned minimal sufficient evidence recovery for coding agents, arguing that sufficiency is a property of an evidence set rather than of individual ranked passages. It introduces SERBench, a benchmark of 500 held-out agent states drawn from 45 repositories, and MSS-Complement, a method that builds a jointly sufficient set of 4-8 intact source units within 6,144 tokens, recovering a complete set for 73.0% of states at five items and 80.6% at eight. This reframes retrieval for coding agents from re-ranking passages by similarity to recovering exactly what a decision still lacks, which could make agent context budgets far more effective on long-running repository tasks. If the approach generalizes, it may influence how agent memory, tool-use context, and code-assistance pipelines are designed across the LLM agent ecosystem. MSS-Complement uses three semantic calls to propose a jointly sufficient set, search for what it lacks, and return intact source units; a matched control that ranks by similarity alone reaches only 66.6%, indicating the gain comes from the set-level policy rather than extra computation. On AMA-Bench it answers from a 76.2% smaller answer prompt with accuracy 2.08 points above that benchmark's own memory agent, and removing one required group from a complete set costs 12.3 and 11.1 points of repair-localization precision under two executors.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Coding agents working on a GitHub issue typically retrieve context with a ranker that scores each passage independently, but a budget filled with near-duplicate variants of one fact can still leave the next decision unsupported. The paper borrows the statistical notion of sufficiency, where a sufficient statistic contains all the information a sample provides about a parameter, and applies it at the level of evidence sets conditioned on the agent's captured state. SERBench measures this by recording what the agent has already seen and crediting only sets that cover every fact the current decision was annotated to require.

## [MIT Paper Urges AI to Facilitate Democratic Deliberation at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20059) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv position paper (2609.20059) from MIT researchers, including Alex Pentland, argues that AI should facilitate democratic deliberation at scale rather than restructure representation through mechanisms like liquid democracy. The authors propose four guiding principles—preserving agency and autonomy, encouraging mutual respect, promoting equality and inclusiveness, and augmenting rather than substituting active citizenship—and call on the machine learning community to build deliberation-focused AI evaluated on discourse quality rather than engagement metrics. As AI systems increasingly mediate how people access information and form opinions, this paper stakes out a normative direction for AI for social good: lowering barriers to meaningful civic engagement without letting machines substitute for human judgment. It matters for AI governance, computational social science, and platform design, and it directly challenges the ML community to rethink what success metrics should look like for political and deliberative applications. The paper is a position paper rather than an empirical study, so its four principles and its treatment of challenges such as alignment, sycophancy, training bias, and over-reliance on AI remain untested at scale. It explicitly contrasts AI-assisted deliberation with liquid democracy, arguing that vote delegation restructures representation while deliberation support preserves human choice.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Liquid democracy is a hybrid of direct and representative democracy in which voters can either vote directly or delegate their votes to proxies, sometimes on a per-issue basis. AI alignment research studies how to steer AI systems toward intended goals and values, while AI sycophancy describes the tendency of large language models to tell users what they want to hear rather than what is accurate. Online deliberation platforms and experimental research on them provide the empirical evidence the authors draw on.

## [ROGUE: Dual-Agent Adversarial Learning for Robust Audio Deepfake Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20063) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose ROGUE, a framework that dynamically constructs robust audio deepfake detection workflows by orchestrating multiple detection tools, formulating workflow generation as a sequential decision-making problem. It introduces a dual-agent paradigm in which a perturbation agent generates audio perturbations while a policy agent learns to select and execute detection tools under perturbed conditions via adversarial learning. Existing audio deepfake detectors perform well under controlled conditions but often fail under real-world perturbations and corruptions, which is a serious problem as voice cloning enables fraud, phishing, and misinformation. ROGUE's adversarial workflow-generation approach could improve the reliability of detection systems in real deployment settings and inspire further work on adversarial robustness and tool orchestration. ROGUE enables perturbation-aware tool selection, adaptive execution strategies, and improved robustness to distribution shifts, and experiments across multiple datasets and real-world corruptions show it consistently outperforms strong baselines in both robustness and generalization. As an arXiv preprint, it has not yet undergone peer review and no code or community discussion is currently available.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Audio deepfakes are AI-generated or manipulated speech that convincingly mimics specific individuals, and they are increasingly used in scams, phishing, and misinformation campaigns. Detection systems typically rely on machine learning models trained to distinguish synthetic speech from genuine recordings, but these models can degrade when the audio is noisy, compressed, or otherwise corrupted. Adversarial learning is a technique in which a model is trained against an opponent that deliberately generates challenging examples, and sequential decision-making models a task as a series of choices made over time, which together allow ROGUE to adaptively combine multiple detection tools.

## [Discriminative Adaptation of SpeechLLMs Improves Emotion Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20081) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new paper proposes a discriminative adaptation for SpeechLLMs that reads the final prompt token's hidden state through a single linear classification head, producing an emotion label in one forward pass without modifying the backbone. On the IEMOCAP dataset, across two SpeechLLM architectures, this approach improves Macro F1 and eliminates hallucinations, with the largest gains on realistic ASR transcripts. Generative decoders are poorly suited for classification because they can emit labels outside the target set and favor frequent classes, so this controlled comparison of generative versus discriminative inference offers a practical, interpretable fix for emotion recognition. The approach could generalize to other classification tasks built on top of speech and language models, benefiting the speech and affective computing community. The classification head is kept as a single linear layer, trading a small amount of accuracy for interpretability: each emotion becomes one direction in the LLM output token space, revealing associated tokens. The analysis shows these emotion directions encode indirect associations that mirror biases in web-scale text.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: SpeechLLMs are multimodal large language models that integrate speech and language within a single framework, enabling end-to-end speech understanding and generation. Emotion recognition is typically framed as a classification task, but SpeechLLMs read the predicted emotion off a generative decoder, which is not designed for classification. IEMOCAP is a widely used acted, multimodal, multispeaker emotional speech database from USC containing about 12 hours of audiovisual data, commonly used to benchmark emotion recognition systems.

## [MATCH: Model-Aware Curriculum and Gated Rewards for LLM Tool Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20082) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.20082) introduces MATCH, a closed-loop reinforcement learning framework for LLM tool learning that combines Model-Aware Curriculum Learning (MACL) with a Hierarchical Tool-call Gated Reward (HTGR). MATCH achieves 72.19% overall accuracy on API-Bank and 62.87% on BFCL V3, outperforming the main supervised and RL-based baselines, with consistent gains across four backbones from two model families. Tool learning is central to making LLMs useful agents, and MATCH directly targets two known weaknesses of current RL methods: fixed-threshold curricula that drift out of sync with the policy's evolving ability, and additive rewards that leak credit when the predicted tool is wrong. If the results hold up, the model-aware curriculum and gated reward design could become a standard recipe for training reliable tool-calling behavior. MACL maintains reward-derived sample difficulty that co-evolves with the policy, selecting each epoch samples near the current capability boundary plus a top-k pool of harder cases; HTGR scores tool name, argument key, and argument value as a gated chain, granting credit at each level only when prerequisites hold. The same HTGR rewards drive both GRPO updates and MACL's difficulty refresh, closing the loop between policy optimization and sample scheduling.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Tool learning lets large language models call external tools (APIs, search engines, calculators) to handle tasks beyond their parametric knowledge, and reinforcement learning can optimize this tool-call behavior from feedback. Curriculum learning in RL trains an agent on a sequence of tasks of increasing difficulty rather than dropping it into the hardest task immediately, while credit assignment is the long-standing problem of determining which action actually caused a future reward. MATCH combines these ideas: it schedules samples according to the model's current ability and assigns reward credit hierarchically so that a wrong tool name does not wrongly reward its arguments.

## [Scalable Trust Discovery Architecture for the Internet of Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20095) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.20095) proposes a hierarchical, distributed trust discovery architecture for the Internet of Agents, built on three layers: Agent Root for registry governance, Agent Registry for registration and metadata publication, and Agent Resolver for distributed capability discovery. It introduces a registry-suffix-anchored composite identity scheme that binds an agent's native identifier to a trusted registry suffix, plus a dual-certificate and multi-level authentication mechanism, with a prototype achieving 58ms average registration latency, 25ms average discovery latency, over 19,000 registrations per second, and over 29,000 discovery requests per second. Current agent protocols such as tool-invocation and inter-agent messaging standards largely leave scalable registration, trustworthy identification, and capability-oriented discovery unresolved, which limits how autonomous agents can safely collaborate across heterogeneous platforms. This work offers a concrete, evaluated design for identity-trusted agent ecosystems, relevant to multi-agent systems, agent registries, and emerging Internet of Agents efforts by industry and standards bodies. The composite identity scheme generates a globally discoverable identity by anchoring an agent's native identifier to a trusted registry suffix, and trust is reinforced through dual certificates and multi-level authentication. The prototype's reported throughput and latency figures come from large-scale registration and resolution experiments, though the abstract does not detail the test environment, failure modes, or how the architecture handles registry compromise or revocation.

**Background**: The Internet of Agents (IoA) is a vision of an open, interoperable network in which large numbers of autonomous AI agents can discover, verify, and collaborate with each other across heterogeneous platforms, analogous to how the web connects humans and services. Existing work has focused mainly on agent communication and tool invocation, while identity and discovery remain open problems; related efforts include federated agent identity registries and capability-discovery frameworks. This paper addresses the missing trust and discovery layer by combining a hierarchical registry design with a suffix-anchored identity scheme.

## [Decoupled Multi-Dimensional Prosody Judge for Live Streaming TTS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20124) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces Live-ProsodyJudge (LPJ), a pairwise prosody evaluator distilled from Gemini into Qwen3-Omni, and then proposes Decoupled-Live-ProsodyJudge (D-LPJ) to fix a flaw called verdict coupling, where a nominally multi-dimensional judge collapses all dimension scores to match its overall preference. D-LPJ removes the overall verdict target, masks uncertain pair-dimensions during supervised fine-tuning, and uses a span-local GRPO strategy that applies normalized advantages only to their corresponding rationale spans. Fine-grained prosody evaluation is essential for live streaming TTS, where emotion, intonation, and energy matter far more than in neutral read speech, but proprietary LLM judges like Gemini are too expensive for massive inference and reinforcement learning feedback. A cheap, decoupled, multi-dimensional judge could make preference optimization for expressive TTS practical at scale. On curated human-annotated test sets, 10-sample balanced-order LPJ achieves higher point accuracy than a single Gemini call, and in a Best-of-8 TTS candidate selection tournament the LPJ-selected utterance falls within the human top-3 in 85.29% of high-confidence cases. The work is an arXiv preprint without community discussion, so its claims have not yet been independently replicated.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Live streaming speech synthesis (TTS) must produce highly expressive prosody, including emotion, intonation, and energy, which traditional MOS predictors are not designed to capture. LLM-as-a-judge systems can assess such qualities, but proprietary models are costly, so LLM distillation transfers the teacher model's judgments into a smaller student model such as Qwen3-Omni. Multi-dimensional evaluation aims to score each aspect separately, but verdict coupling causes the judge to lazily align every dimension score with its overall preference, reducing a rich rubric to a single preference bit.

## [Local Sparsity Enables Unsupervised LLM Safety Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20129) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.20129) proposes a locally masked sparse autoencoder (SAE) framework for unsupervised LLM safety detection, arguing that under the linear representation hypothesis (LRH), nearby points in the concept space share a small common active support. The authors provide theoretical justifications and validate the method across multiple architectures and datasets, showing that with only 1% out-of-distribution data for calibration, locally sparse methods achieve near-optimal performance while using just 1-2% of SAE neurons. Most deployment-time LLM safety methods are supervised and require unsafe training data, which cannot cover new attacks or harm categories that emerge over time. This work reframes safety detection as anomaly detection, offering a statistically feasible and computationally efficient path that could make LLM deployment safer without relying on labeled unsafe examples. The framework leverages local sparsity in the SAE concept space, where nearby points share a small common active support, to make anomaly detection statistically feasible in high-dimensional activation space. When allowed to use 1% out-of-distribution data for calibration, locally sparse methods achieve near-optimal performance while computing with only 1-2% of SAE neurons, suggesting strong efficiency gains.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: The linear representation hypothesis (LRH) posits that high-level concepts are encoded as linear directions in a model's internal representation space. Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) are a popular interpretability tool that recovers these concept directions by learning sparse, overcomplete feature representations of LLM activations. Anomaly detection in high-dimensional spaces is challenging because data becomes sparse and distances lose meaning, but the local sparsity property of SAE concept space may mitigate this issue.

## [AdaRepair-Mem: Adaptive Memory Retrieval for Repository-Level Repair](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20130) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper proposes AdaRepair-Mem, an adaptive experience retrieval framework for repository-level program repair that addresses three limitations of existing memory-augmented repair systems: episodic memory imbalance across repositories, the non-monotonic relationship between memory volume and repair success, and phase-misaligned memory accumulation. It introduces coverage-aware retrieval, quality-aware selection, and stage-aware routing, and is evaluated on SWE-Bench-Lite and SWE-Bench-Verified. Repository-level automated program repair is a fast-growing area of LLM-based software engineering, and this work shows that simply accumulating more historical repair experiences is not the answer — retrieving the right experiences for the right repair context matters more. If validated, the approach could improve repair performance on under-covered, low-resource repositories and reduce noisy memory retrieval, benefiting both researchers and practitioners building issue-resolution agents. The framework's three components are coverage-aware retrieval, which falls back to cross-repository or repair-type-based memories when same-repository memory is insufficient; quality-aware selection, which ranks memories by relevance, historical utility, specificity, and redundancy; and stage-aware routing, which separates memories for reproduction, localization, patch generation, patch refinement, and validation. Results show improvements on under-covered repositories, reduced noisy retrieval, and better support for failed-to-fixed patch refinement, though this is an arXiv preprint without community validation yet.

Memory-augmented approaches, such as ExpeRepair's dual episodic and semantic memory, reuse historical repair experiences to help LLMs solve new issues. ## [Verbose Prompts Act as Frequency Filters, Boosting VLM Robustness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20139) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.20139) shows that verbose prompts, such as rephrasing "Is there a cat?" into "Please look carefully and answer: is there a cat?", make vision-language models substantially more robust to image corruption, while semantically complex or fine-grained questions make them more fragile. The authors attribute both effects to question-conditioned cross-modal attention, which acts as a spectral filter over image patches: verbose questions broaden its frequency support, while fine-grained questions concentrate it onto fewer visual scales. This finding offers a mechanistic explanation for why prompt wording changes VLM reliability, and it suggests a practical, zero-cost recipe—simply padding the prompt—that improves accuracy even under image corruption. It could influence prompt engineering practices and robustness research for multimodal models deployed in noisy real-world settings. The filter view was tested on Qwen3-VL and LLaVA-OneVision across GQA and CLEVR, where verbose paraphrasing reduced drift variance by 70–81% on the 8B models. The model's answer drifts most when the induced spectral filter and the corruption share the same spatial frequencies, and prompt padding yielded measurable accuracy gains even under corruption.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language models (VLMs) combine a vision encoder, a projector, and a large language model, and they align visual and textual representations through cross-modal attention. Prior work has shown these models are fragile under image corruption, but the role of prompt wording in that fragility was not well understood. This paper connects prompt phrasing to the spatial-frequency content of cross-modal attention, borrowing concepts from frequency analysis of image corruption in deep learning.

## [Metacognitive Feedback Cuts Cognitive Offloading to LLM Assistants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20143) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A preregistered online experiment (N=704) with a 2×2 design plus a no-AI control tested two interventions to reduce cognitive offloading to an LLM assistant during fraction arithmetic practice. Metacognitive feedback reduced answer offloading (OR=0.47) and improved unaided test performance (OR=1.51), while an effort-based reward showed no significant effect on either outcome. This provides concrete, causally identified evidence that deskilling from AI assistance can be mitigated through interface design rather than by restricting AI access, which matters for educators, tool builders, and organizations deploying LLM assistants. It suggests a lightweight, scalable design lever—making the learning consequences of offloading explicit—that could be embedded in AI tutoring and productivity tools. The task required participants to practice fraction arithmetic with an LLM assistant that provided solutions only on explicit request, followed by an unaided test; the study was preregistered and used a 2×2 design with a no-AI control. The scope is limited to a specific arithmetic task in an online experiment, and the paper is an arXiv preprint without peer review or community discussion yet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Cognitive offloading refers to shifting mental tasks—such as memory, calculation, or judgment—onto external tools like calculators or AI assistants, which can reduce practice opportunities and lead to deskilling, a decline in individual proficiency. Metacognition involves awareness and regulation of one's own learning, and metacognitive feedback delivers information about desired learning behaviors (e.g., plan, monitor, reflect) rather than domain knowledge. Prior work shows feedback can enhance metacognition and self-regulated learning, but it was unclear whether such feedback could reduce offloading to LLMs without restricting access.

## [DB-SUiT: Surface-based Diffusion Bridge Translates MRI to PET on the Cortex](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20147) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces DB-SUiT, a surface-based diffusion bridge framework that translates MRI to FDG-PET directly on the cortical manifold using a conditional Spherical U-shaped vision Transformer (SUiT). Evaluated on two datasets including different dementia types, synthesized PET surfaces improved automated dementia classification by 14.2% over MRI and 11.3% over PET volumes, and achieved 85.5% diagnostic accuracy in a blinded reader study versus 75.8% for MRI and 95.2% for real PET. FDG-PET is a highly sensitive biomarker for dementia but is limited by high cost, radiation exposure, and poor accessibility; a reliable MRI-to-PET synthesis method could make metabolic brain imaging far more widely available. The approach also demonstrates cross-cohort and cross-pathology generalization, suggesting it may be useful in real clinical settings where dementia subtypes differ from training data. DB-SUiT combines spherical convolutional encoders for multi-scale surface feature extraction with bottleneck Transformers to capture long-range spatial dependencies, while incorporating demographic and subcortical conditions to refine synthesis. The model was evaluated without retraining on an external cohort containing a dementia subtype not represented during training, and code is available at https://github.com/ai-med/DB-SUiT.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: FDG-PET measures glucose metabolism in the brain, and cortical hypometabolism is a sensitive early sign of dementia, but PET scanners are expensive and involve radioactive tracers. MRI is far more accessible and radiation-free, so researchers have tried to synthesize PET-like images from MRI using generative models. Most existing methods work on 3D volumetric grids, which ignore the highly folded geometry of the cortex where disease-related metabolic patterns are concentrated; surface-based analysis instead models the cortex as a 2D manifold, and diffusion bridge models are a generative approach that learns a stochastic path between paired source and target distributions.

## [QUALS Balances Time Series Corpora for Efficient Zero-Shot Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20156) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.20156) by Yujie Li and nine co-authors introduces QUALS, a large-scale time series corpus equilibrium framework that combines pattern quantization with learnability synchronization. The framework enables existing forecasting models to achieve superior zero-shot performance using only a small fraction of the original training data. Most time series foundation model research focuses on architecture while relying on naive data sampling, leaving data diversity and training efficiency underexplored. QUALS addresses this gap, potentially lowering the massive compute and data costs of training universal forecasters for domains like transportation and power grids. QUALS operates through two mechanisms: a pattern quantization framework that decodes heterogeneous patterns from mixed corpora via vector quantization and uniform binning, and a learnability synchronization framework that calibrates sampling weights to bridge the optimization gap between simple and complex motifs. Benchmarks show consistently superior zero-shot performance even under substantially reduced training budgets.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Time series foundation models are pretrained on massive, diverse datasets so they can forecast new, unseen series without task-specific training, a capability known as zero-shot forecasting. Pattern quantization maps real-valued series into discrete symbolic representations, which aids storage and similarity search, while learnability refers to how easily a model can learn a given pattern. QUALS combines these ideas to balance mixed-domain corpora during pretraining.

## [Onboard Modular Vision-Language Navigation Stack for Aerial Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20191) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces 'VLN on the Fly', an onboard stack that keeps grounding, planning, and control as separate, inspectable stages for aerial robots. In 15 indoor flights over three everyday referents, it reached the target in 13 of 15 trials with 5.72 cm mean goal error and 39.3% average GPU utilization, and in 6 additional cluttered-environment trials it tracked collision-free trajectories under onboard perception gating. This matters because running vision-language navigation fully onboard an aerial robot is hard: grounding, planning, and control must share limited compute, and a single-stage error is difficult to isolate in flight. By keeping stages separate and inspectable, the stack preserves observability and safety checks that end-to-end aerial policies give up, which is valuable for debugging and safety in autonomous aerial systems. A quantized VLM grounds an instruction to a coarse image cell, depth lifts it to a 3D goal, a fast B-spline planner returns a feasible trajectory, and a pretrained reinforcement learning policy tracks it to motor commands across quadrotors. The reported results are limited to a controlled indoor volume and a small number of trials, so broader outdoor or dynamic-environment validation remains to be shown.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language navigation (VLN) is a task where an agent uses natural language instructions and visual input to determine and execute a sequence of navigational actions. End-to-end aerial policies fuse grounding, planning, and control into one network, which saves compute but makes errors hard to isolate. Modular stacks instead keep these stages separate, trading some efficiency for observability and safety checks. A quantized VLM is a vision-language model compressed to lower precision so it can run on limited onboard hardware, while B-spline planners are widely used to generate smooth, feasible trajectories for robots and autonomous vehicles.

## [First Music-Specific Study of Hallucination in Audio-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20195) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper presents the first music-specific, layer-wise, multi-paradigm empirical study of hallucination in audio-language models, formulating it as a hierarchical perceptual grounding failure across five layers: sound events, temporal properties, tonal attributes, style, and emotion. The authors introduce MuseDiag, a multi-paradigm diagnostic framework with contradiction-based verification, and evaluate nine models (four open-source and five closed-source), finding that vocal misperception is a universal weakness and that tonal perception is a major axis of architectural differentiation. Hallucination in audio-language models is relatively underexplored compared with vision-language models, so this work provides a structured diagnostic vocabulary and benchmark that could guide more reliable music description systems. Its findings on universal weaknesses and paradigm-specific vulnerabilities can inform model selection, evaluation design, and future mitigation research for anyone building or deploying audio-language applications. The study finds that affirmative bias, generation-mode effects, and layer-specific perceptual limitations are each empirically associated with observed hallucination patterns, though the authors present convergent evidence rather than strict causal attribution. Two training-free mitigation methods, Audio-Dependency-Aware Decoding for Music (ADD-M) and Taxonomy-Guided Perceptual Anchoring (TPA), reduce hallucination in probing but their gains vary by model and often do not carry over to free-form generation, showing mitigation must be evaluated across paradigms.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Audio-language models are neural architectures that align audio signals with text, using language as a supervision signal to describe and reason about sound, and they are increasingly used for music understanding and captioning. Hallucination in AI refers to generated descriptions or claims that are unsupported by the input, a well-known problem in large language and vision-language models that is now being studied for audio. This paper extends that line of inquiry to music, where perception involves layered attributes such as events, timing, tonality, style, and emotion.

## [Training Beats Prompting LLMs on Tabular Data, Benchmark Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20218) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.20218) introduces the labeled-data crossover N*, the training-set size at which a trained classical model's learning curve overtakes a frozen LLM's flat, training-free error. Aggregating 126 independent student evaluations of small GPT models under eight prompting configurations across 18 tabular datasets, the study finds a trained classical model beats the best-prompted frozen LLM using no more labeled data than is already on hand in 86% of cases, with a median crossover at roughly 6% of the training set. The finding gives practitioners a concrete, quantitative answer to a practical question now shipping in mainstream tools like Microsoft Copilot in Excel and Claude for Excel: whether to prompt a frozen LLM or collect labels and train a model. It suggests that for typical business tables, collecting a few hundred labels and training a gradient-boosted model is the better path, which could redirect effort away from LLM prompting for tabular prediction tasks. In-context few-shot examples do not behave like training, since error versus shot count does not follow a power law, and the same protocol re-run by independent implementers varies with a coefficient of variation of 0.148. A controlled probe indicates the LLM depends on recognizable feature-name semantics, which plausibly makes the crossover a conservative estimate; the authors do not claim memorization.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Tabular data—spreadsheets and database tables with rows and columns—remains the dominant format for business prediction problems, and classical models like gradient-boosted trees have long been the standard tool. Large language models can now label a tabular row from a plain-English description with no training, raising the question of when training a classical model is still worthwhile. Learning curves describe how a model's error decreases as training data grows, and power-law learning curves are a well-studied model for this behavior in classical machine learning.

## [CleanVideo: Adaptive Concept Erasure for Text-to-Video Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20267) ⭐️ 7.0/10

CleanVideo introduces a selective concept erasure framework for text-to-video diffusion models that performs low-dimensional subspace intervention controlled by a tri-modal gating mechanism, jointly processing spatiotemporal visual features, timestep signals, and textual semantics. Experiments on three video diffusion models show it effectively erases target concepts while maintaining visual fidelity and temporal coherence, outperforming existing baselines under frame-level and video-level evaluations and concept-recovery attacks. This work extends concept erasure from image diffusion models to video, a non-trivial step that addresses AI safety and content moderation needs for increasingly capable generative video systems. It could help platforms and model providers remove harmful or undesired visual semantics from pre-trained video generators without retraining them from scratch. The tri-modal gating mechanism determines where, when, and whether to intervene, steering erased content toward natural surrogate concepts when such surrogates can be clearly defined while preserving non-target content. The method is evaluated under concept-recovery attacks when the protected pipeline remains intact, though the abstract does not specify the exact attack settings or the magnitude of fidelity trade-offs.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Concept erasure aims to selectively eliminate undesired visual semantics from pre-trained generative models without compromising their general utility, and has been studied mainly for text-to-image diffusion models such as Stable Diffusion. Extending it to video is challenging because target concepts emerge gradually and vary across frames and denoising steps, so fixed interventions may miss the target or introduce blurring, jitter, and content distortion. CleanVideo addresses this by intervening in a low-dimensional subspace of the model's internal representations, a technique related to controlled subspace intervention frameworks used in other generative and language models.

## [Labeled Incidence Structures Let One Transformer Model Text, Graphs, and Hypergraphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20278) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.20278) introduces labeled incidence structures (LIS), a uniform representation that encodes every endpoint as a triple (x_d, s, e) — content, role/slot, and relation instance — so that a single standard transformer can process text, knowledge graphs, and hypergraphs natively without flattening them into token sequences. The paper also characterizes when the structural address A(s,e) = R_s R_e yields unique, path-independent addresses, and defines a relative transport operator P_{j→i} = A_i^{-1} A_j that gives attention a role- and relation-aware inductive bias. If validated, LIS could unify structured and unstructured data modeling under one transformer architecture, removing the need for specialized graph neural networks or flattening heuristics that discard role and relation structure. This matters for researchers in graph learning, knowledge-graph reasoning, and multi-modal modeling who currently must choose between architecture-level and representation-level solutions. The paper proves, via a controlled example family, that additive encodings of the form 'position term plus relation term' cannot capture information depending jointly on slot s and instance e, only their separate effects. It also analyzes persistent knowledge repositories, showing that storage-location-tied identifiers make models sensitive to storage order, while computing relation-instance operators from content avoids this issue and yields a capacity bound independent of repository size M under fixed architectural and Lipschitz assumptions.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Transformers natively operate on flat token sequences, so structured data such as knowledge graphs and hypergraphs must usually be serialized or handled by specialized architectures like graph neural networks. Flattening loses the distinct roles that elements play within a relation instance (for example, subject versus object in a triple), which is exactly the structure LIS aims to preserve. Hypergraphs generalize graphs by allowing edges to connect more than two nodes, making them useful for n-ary relations but harder to feed into standard sequence models.
