## [Modal Logic Meets Statistics to Detect AI-Generated Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20311) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.20311) proposes reading semantic neighborhood graphs as modal logic and giving that reading a statistical form, in order to quantify structural differences between human and machine-generated text. By treating texts as worlds in a finite frame whose accessibility relation is the k-nearest-neighbor relation of a transformer embedding, the authors compute symmetry, transitivity, Euclideanity, and seriality frequencies as degrees of validation of the modal axioms B, 4, 5, and D, finding consistently higher artificial degrees for axioms 4 and 5 in a prompt-balanced comparison. This work offers a fresh theoretical framework that bridges modal logic and statistics, potentially improving AI-generated text detection and deepening our understanding of how machine text differs structurally from human text. It could influence NLP research on detection, authorship attribution, and the interpretability of transformer embeddings. Each degree is simultaneously the proportion of instances of a rule licensed by the subframe in Negri's labelled calculus G3.K and a plug-in estimate of a population probability; the authors also add degrees of groundedness and situatedness and recast the licensing reading in Cuconato's one-sided sequent-style tableaux, where each degree becomes a rate of set membership. As a preprint without peer review or community discussion yet, the results should be considered preliminary.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Modal logic studies expressions like 'necessarily' and 'possibly', and its axioms B, 4, 5, and D correspond to frame properties such as symmetry, transitivity, Euclideanity, and seriality. Transformer embeddings map text into vector spaces where k-nearest-neighbor graphs capture semantic neighborhoods. Negri's labelled calculus G3.K is a proof system that makes semantic parameters explicit in the syntax, allowing frame conditions to be transformed into sequent rules.

A new arXiv paper (2609.20318) presents an automated pipeline that combines computer vision, Large Language Models (LLMs), and Augmented Reality (AR) to transform safe driving scenes into realistic safety-critical scenarios for testing Autonomous Driving Systems (ADS). Evaluated on the nuScenes dataset, the pipeline achieved 97.52% safety classification accuracy and generated realistic scenarios such as pedestrian crossings, rear overtaking vehicles, and sudden-stop events. Collecting safety-critical driving data from the real world is costly and unsafe, so automated generation of such scenarios can significantly reduce validation costs and improve the safety of autonomous driving systems. This approach offers a flexible, scalable way to stress-test ADS before deployment, potentially accelerating regulatory approval and public trust. The system detects and tracks road users, extracts safety features including distance, velocity, motion direction, and Time-to-Collision (TTC), and assesses scene criticality; safe scenes are then modified by an LLM that generates collision-inducing objects and behaviors integrated via AR. The 97.52% accuracy was measured on the nuScenes dataset, a large-scale multimodal benchmark for autonomous driving.

## [FL+FSDP and FL+HSDP Cut Communication Costs in Large-Scale Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20359) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.20359) introduces two hybrid algorithms, FL+FSDP and FL+HSDP, that interleave sharded data parallelism with FedAvg-style aggregation to decouple large data-parallel deployments into smaller, loosely-coupled federation groups. In a Llama3.1 8B pre-training run on 512 A100 GPUs, the methods achieved up to 8.04x faster data processing and 4.48x lower evaluation perplexity than their counterparts under identical hyperparameters. Communication overhead is the dominant bottleneck when training foundation models across thousands of GPUs on multi-tier interconnects, so reducing inter-group traffic while keeping the global batch size bounded could make large-scale pre-training substantially cheaper and more scalable. The approach also suggests a convergence between federated learning and high-performance distributed training, two fields that have largely evolved separately. The gains stem from decoupling large DP deployments into smaller federation groups, which minimizes inter-group traffic and bounds global batch size growth relative to group size; the paper provides formal communication-cost analysis plus experimental validation. The results are from an arXiv preprint that has not yet undergone peer review, and the evaluation is limited to a single Llama3.1 8B pre-training configuration on 512 A100 GPUs.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Sharded data parallelism, such as PyTorch's Fully Sharded Data Parallel (FSDP), splits model parameters, gradients, and optimizer states across GPUs so that very large models can be trained, but it requires heavy all-gather and reduce-scatter communication that becomes prohibitive at scale. Federated learning's FedAvg algorithm instead lets many clients train locally and periodically averages their weights through a central server, which is communication-efficient because clients exchange model updates rather than gradients. This paper borrows FedAvg's communication pattern and applies it inside sharded data-parallel training, forming federation groups that only occasionally synchronize.

## [Xeno-Interpretability: Studying LLM Representations Beyond Human Concepts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20408) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.20408) proposes 'xeno-interpretability,' a framework for studying LLM internal representations that have no adequate human conceptual counterpart, which the authors call 'xeno-representations.' The paper distinguishes the human-interpretable semantic space from a 'xeno-semantic space' of model-native representations, arguing that an internal structure can be reproducibly located, geometrically characterized, and causally manipulated even when its meaning cannot be expressed in human terms. This reframes interpretability from finding human concepts inside models toward discovering model-native structures that may drive behavior in ways humans cannot describe, with implications for AI safety and multi-agent systems where such representations could propagate between agents while remaining only partially visible. If adopted, it could change how researchers design probing, steering, and monitoring tools for frontier models. The paper is primarily a theoretical and programmatic contribution: it separates experimental identification from semantic interpretation and sketches an empirical programme for identifying xeno-representations, but does not present a validated empirical breakthrough. It argues that the space of possible internal distinctions in an LLM is substantially larger than the space expressible through finite human descriptions, and it is a preprint without community discussion or peer review yet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Interpretability research typically explains LLM internals using human concepts such as truthfulness, refusal, deception, personality, and harmfulness, often by probing internal activations or steering model behavior. A semantic space is a geometric representation in which meanings are mapped to points or directions, so that similar concepts cluster together. The paper's key move is to ask whether models encode distinctions that simply have no matching human concept, and whether those distinctions can still be studied rigorously without being translated into human language.

## [Large-Scale Study Questions Alignment Midtraining Effectiveness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20412) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.20412) presents a large-scale empirical evaluation of alignment midtraining (AMT), testing its assumptions on models up to 110 billion parameters and 1 billion midtraining tokens. The study finds that AMT can steer model motivations in simple settings, but a tiny fraction of finetuning data suggesting a competing motivation erases its effects, and rules must be demonstrated in either midtraining or post-training data to be robustly learned. This study fills a critical evidence gap for AMT, a widely discussed but under-evidenced alignment technique, and concludes there is insufficient public evidence to confidently claim midtraining can address core alignment difficulties in powerful AI systems. The findings will directly inform how researchers and practitioners design post-training pipelines for frontier models. The experiments span models up to 110B parameters and 1B midtraining tokens, and examine scenarios where post-training data is ambiguous between two motivations or where only a subset of desired rules is demonstrated. The authors emphasize that AMT's steering effects are fragile and do not generalize robustly, especially when competing signals appear during finetuning.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Post-training techniques such as finetuning and reinforcement learning from human feedback refine a pretrained model, but they cannot demonstrate every desired behavior in every deployment environment, so the model must generalize beyond the post-training distribution. Alignment midtraining (AMT) proposes continuing pretraining on large volumes of alignment-relevant documents to encourage such generalization before alignment finetuning. Despite its prominence in frontier model development, public evidence for AMT's effectiveness has been limited.

## [TouchSight predicts full-hand contact forces from egocentric video without tactile sensors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20414) ⭐️ 7.0/10

TouchSight is a monocular egocentric vision framework that predicts dense full-hand contact forces by training on 500 hours of pressure-glove recordings and using generative video models to re-render gloved footage as bare-hand observations. The authors built TwinTouch-20H, a 20-hour paired dataset that preserves the original measured tactile labels, and the model outperforms prior contact prediction methods on OakInk2 while generalizing to natural bare-hand videos from unseen datasets. This work shows that dense tactile signals can be recovered from egocentric vision alone, removing the need for intrusive instrumentation such as pressure gloves at capture time. It could significantly lower the cost of collecting large-scale tactile data and enable more natural human-robot interaction and dexterous robotic manipulation. The core technical challenge is the appearance gap between gloved training data and bare-hand real-world scenarios, which TouchSight bridges by using generative video models to re-render gloved recordings against new backgrounds while preserving the measured tactile labels. The model's performance improves consistently as glove supervision scales, and it qualitatively generalizes to natural bare-hand egocentric videos from unseen datasets.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Egocentric vision is a subfield of computer vision that analyzes images and videos captured by a wearable camera, typically worn on the head or chest, approximating the wearer's visual field. Tactile sensing provides direct contact and force measurements essential for understanding physical interactions and enabling dexterous robotic manipulation, but it requires direct measurement at contact interfaces, making large-scale data collection reliant on costly and restrictive instrumentation. TouchSight addresses this by learning to infer those tactile signals from ordinary first-person video.

<a id="item-175"></a>
## [Language-Grounded Explanations Fail for Sheep Pain Recognition, Concept Bottleneck Fixes It](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20427) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.20427) shows that language-grounded explanations for sheep facial pain recognition are misleading: ablating an entire clinical descriptor changes the predicted logit by only about 10^-4, and the most-attended cue agrees with the predicted pain level in just 32.6% of regions. The authors replace the appearance bypass with a concept bottleneck whose classifier reads only Sheep Pain Facial Expression Scale (SPFES) concept scores, costing 0.05–0.10 in Cohen's κ but recovering minority pain-indicating states at 3.5–8.3× their base rates. This work offers a cautionary tale for explainable AI: attention maps and generated text can propose explanations that do not reflect actual model behavior, which is especially dangerous in animal welfare where stockpersons need trustworthy justifications to act. It also provides a cross-validated, protocol-matched benchmark of seven methods on this dataset, advancing both interpretable ML and practical sheep pain assessment. The concept bottleneck removes the appearance bypass so the classifier reads only SPFES concept scores, supervised by per-region state annotations that image-level pipelines discard; ear and eye severity orderings emerge without severity supervision. Removing the supervision alone leaves κ unchanged while concept accuracy falls to 0.109, showing that architectural necessity does not imply semantic validity, and pooled concept accuracy is misleading under clinical imbalance.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: The Sheep Pain Facial Expression Scale (SPFES) is a standardized veterinary tool that assesses five facial areas for abnormal expression to detect pain in sheep, such as from footrot or mastitis. Concept Bottleneck Models (CBMs) are inherently interpretable architectures that first predict human-understandable concepts from input, then predict the final label from those concepts, improving transparency over end-to-end models. Attention mechanisms assign weights to input elements to prioritize relevant information, but this paper shows such weights can be unreliable as explanations.

## [First Multimodal Model Fingerprinting: AttnPrint and DistillTrace](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20457) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper presents the first study on multimodal large language model (MLLM) fingerprinting, introducing AttnPrint for white-box provenance and DistillTrace for black-box auditing. AttnPrint extracts cross-modal attention distributions and isolates their low-frequency components as fingerprints, while DistillTrace uses hypothesis testing of MLLM outputs to detect distillation relationships. This work addresses the growing risk of illicit deployment and unauthorized distillation of MLLMs, offering a way to protect model ownership and intellectual property. It could impact AI security, model auditing, and compliance with emerging regulations like the EU AI Act. The methods were evaluated on 154 model instances across 19 multimodal architectures; AttnPrint showed strong derivative-model detection and robustness to five downstream modification techniques, while DistillTrace provided evidence under three parameter-independent techniques. The approach leverages the finding that self-attention acts as a low-pass filter, but may face challenges with adaptive adversaries.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) process and reason across text, images, and other modalities, enabling tasks like image-text reasoning. Model fingerprinting embeds unique signatures into AI models for ownership verification and provenance tracking, while distillation detection identifies when a smaller model is trained to mimic a larger one. Existing provenance solutions for MLLMs are confounded by shared language backbones and struggle to detect distillation violations.

## [greCAPTCHA Verifies Research Authorship Through Proctored Understanding Tests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20481) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces greCAPTCHA, a proctored assessment method that measures authors' understanding of their manuscripts as evidence of human authorship, evaluated through a user study with 31 researchers. The prototype's automated scores predicted which papers were or were not authored by study participants with an AUC of 0.90. As generative AI makes it harder for conferences, journals, and funders to trust that listed authors actually did the work, this framework offers a concrete way to verify expertise rather than relying on names alone. If adopted, it could reshape submission and review workflows across academia and research funding. greCAPTCHA is built on the construct of "capacity to verify"—the knowledge and reasoning needed to critically assess the contents underlying one's contributions—and generates questions at multiple levels of understanding, producing an evaluative report from authors' responses. Participants reported positive experiences and appropriate construct validity but suggested important changes before real-world deployment.

## [Edustories Dataset Reveals LLMs Lag Behind Teachers in Predicting Classroom Outcomes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20484) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced Edustories, a dataset of 1,492 teacher-written case studies describing real elementary and high-school classroom situations involving challenging student behavior, pedagogical interventions, and their outcomes. Testing the latest models from four language-model families against expert assessments, they found the strongest LLMs reach only 58% accuracy in predicting intervention success, compared to 64% for human experts. Most AI-in-education research has focused on individualized student assistance, while the majority of real-world teaching happens in collective classroom settings. Edustories fills this gap by enabling evaluation of LLMs as assistants for practicing teachers, and the 6-point gap shows current models are not yet reliable enough to give teachers trustworthy feedback on pedagogical interventions. The dataset covers authentic classroom situations with challenging student behavior, teacher interventions, and their outcomes, and was compared against expert assessments across four language-model families. The 58% versus 64% accuracy gap highlights both the limitations and the emerging potential of AI as a teaching assistant.

## [Model-Agnostic Voice Pipeline Boosts Agricultural ASR Accuracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20504) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers from Digital Green present a modular, model-agnostic pipeline that improves automatic speech recognition (ASR) quality for FarmerChat, an agricultural advisory assistant, without fine-tuning or replacing the underlying ASR model. The pipeline combines gated audio enhancement, speaker diarization with target-speaker selection, domain-aware correction using a weighted agricultural lexicon, and a quality gate, reducing word error rate (WER) by 16-23% relative on three cloud ASR models and 5% on an on-device model across Hindi, Telugu, and Odia recordings. This work shows that targeted preprocessing, speaker selection, and domain-aware post-processing can substantially improve agricultural speech transcription for smallholder farmers in low-resource languages, without requiring costly model fine-tuning or replacement. The approach is broadly applicable to other domain-specific voice applications facing noisy field conditions and could accelerate adoption of voice interfaces in agriculture and similar sectors. Only the diarization stage is fine-tuned; all other stages use off-the-shelf models behind common interfaces. The largest gains occur on multi-speaker recordings, where target-speaker selection prevents competing speech from entering the transcript, yielding 32-42% relative WER reductions for cloud models and 16% for the on-device model, with all reported reductions statistically significant.

## [CURB Framework Mitigates Retaliatory Algorithmic Collusion in Repeated Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20548) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper formalizes the connection between Q-learning collusion and classical Simple Penal Codes (SPCs), showing that any non-trivial SPC induces a quantifiable conditional dependence in agents' policies detectable via total variation distance. The authors introduce CURB (Collusion Unwinding via Reward shaping and Belief injection), a reward-shaping framework that penalizes this TV distance signal during Q-learning and is guaranteed to convert any SPC fixed point into a trivial one, empirically reducing collusion in Bertrand and Cournot repeated games and extending to deep Q-network agents. Algorithmic collusion poses a significant risk as pricing and trading increasingly rely on reinforcement learning agents, and existing mitigation approaches are largely tied to specific economic settings like two-sided platforms and auctions. CURB provides a general, theoretically grounded intervention for arbitrary repeated games, which could inform AI safety research, multi-agent RL design, and antitrust policy for autonomous pricing systems. The detection signal is the total variation distance between an agent's action distributions across cooperation and defection histories, and CURB penalizes this signal during Q-learning with a guarantee that any SPC fixed point becomes trivial, precluding collusive equilibria sustained by punishment threats. Empirical validation covers tabular Q-learning in Bertrand and Cournot competition repeated games, plus deep Q-network agents in Bertrand competition, though the paper does not claim evaluation in real-world market deployments.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Reinforcement learning agents trained to maximize their own reward in repeated interactions can converge to supra-competitive outcomes resembling explicit collusion, even without communication or shared design. Simple Penal Codes, from Abreu's 1988 work in repeated game theory, describe strategies where any deviation is punished by reverting to a player's worst equilibrium, sustaining cooperation through credible punishment threats. Q-learning is a foundational RL algorithm that learns action values through repeated interaction, and prior research has shown that competing Q-learning pricing algorithms can autonomously learn collusive strategies. Reward shaping is a technique that modifies an agent's reward signal to guide learning toward desired behaviors in multi-agent reinforcement learning.

## [SGPS Combines Sampling-Based MPC with Policy Gradients for Visual Robot Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20575) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers propose Sampling-Guided Policy Search (SGPS), a hybrid method that couples sampling-based model predictive control (MPC) with first-order policy gradients (FoPG) to train visual policies for locomotion and manipulation. Using a decoupled FoPG formulation that excludes rendering from the computation graph, SGPS learns policies on a single GPU for simulated Unitree Go2 and G1 robots, and the distilled policy transfers zero-shot to a real Go2 for trotting, crawling, hurdle clearing, and behavior switching. This work addresses a key limitation of differentiable simulation, where first-order policy gradients can converge to unintended contact patterns and local optima. By combining sampling-based refinement with gradient updates, it offers a more robust and computationally efficient path to visual policy learning, with demonstrated zero-shot sim-to-real transfer on legged robots. SGPS initializes the policy via behavior cloning from sampled actions, then alternates sampling-based refinement with short-horizon FoPG updates under perturbed initial states and randomized dynamics. The decoupled FoPG formulation enables direct learning from depth observations without a state-policy teacher, and experiments show refinement improves learning beyond initialization and tracking alone.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Model predictive control (MPC) optimizes control inputs over a receding horizon, and sampling-based variants such as Model Predictive Path Integral (MPPI) control are gradient-free and well-suited to non-linear, non-convex dynamics. First-order policy gradients (FoPG) use differentiable simulation to reduce training cost, but local optimization can converge to unintended contact patterns. Behavior cloning is a foundational imitation learning method that converts expert demonstrations into supervised examples for robot policies.

## [SPAR: A Simulation Platform for LLM-Based AUV Fault Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20620) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces SPAR (Simulation Platform for AUV Recovery), a closed-loop simulation architecture that couples real-time C vehicle software with an orchestration layer for physics-based fault injection, structured prompting, LLM interaction, mission file generation, validation, execution, and LLM-judge scoring. Across 480 trials of a mass-shift fault, the frontier model placed the CG-shift mechanism in its top three hypotheses in 85-90% of trials, versus 60-78% for the best locally deployable model. AUVs operating beyond reliable communications must recover from failures without human intervention, so extending unanticipated-fault recovery from detection to mitigation could significantly improve autonomous underwater mission reliability. The ensemble methodology for evaluating stochastic LLM-assisted mission management on low-power AUVs also offers a rigorous template for testing LLM-based autonomy more broadly. Model choice dominates diagnosis quality, and diagnosis performance does not appear coupled with operational decision performance in this dataset; reasoning analysis shows local-model success correlates with following the complete diagnostic procedure, while weaker models often prematurely commit to elevator failure even though the actuator tracks its command.

Large language models (LLMs) are stochastic, meaning the same prompt can yield different outputs, so evaluating them rigorously requires ensemble testing across many repeated trials rather than single demonstrations. SPAR combines conventional deterministic layered control for normal operations with an invokable LLM that acts as a diagnostic and recovery planner when onboard anomaly detection flags performance outside expected limits.

## [Chronicle: Cut-Point Replay for LLM Agent Regression Testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20625) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers released Chronicle, a record-and-replay system that captures LLM agent runs at non-deterministic boundaries as immutable envelopes and replays them via a novel operation called cut-point replay. On a benchmark of 6 recorded failures, recording adds only 23 microseconds per boundary crossing, full replay issues zero model calls and is bit-stable across 20 repetitions, and cut-point tests correctly fail on faulty code while passing on guarded and benign changes for all 6 incidents. LLM agent failures are notoriously hard to reproduce because model outputs are non-deterministic, tools read changing state, and multi-step trajectories rarely repeat exactly. Chronicle turns recorded production incidents into reproducible regression tests that run in CI, giving developers a practical way to verify fixes and catch regressions without expensive live model calls. The key mechanism is cut-point replay, which serves a chosen subset of boundaries from the record while executing the complementary subset live with new code, allowing targeted testing of a fix while the rest of the incident stays frozen. In a mutation study of guarded tools, cut-point tests caught every mutant that let the recorded unsafe action through, whereas a baseline that stubs every boundary with the same assertion caught none. Chronicle and its benchmark are publicly available on GitHub.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: LLM agents are AI systems that use large language models to make decisions and call external tools over multiple steps. Because model inference is not bitwise reproducible and tools may read changing state, re-running an agent rarely reproduces the same failure, making debugging and regression testing difficult. Record-and-replay is a classic technique that captures a run so it can be replayed deterministically, but existing agent tooling uses it mainly for tracing or scoring rather than for testing code changes. Chronicle adapts this idea to the specific challenge of testing fixes against recorded agent incidents in continuous integration.

## [HIL-UMI enables robot-free human-in-the-loop post-training of VLA models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20659) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces HIL-UMI, a policy-guided Universal Manipulation Interface (UMI) framework that performs human-in-the-loop post-training of vision-language-action (VLA) models without executing a physical robot. During handheld UMI demonstrations, it queries the current policy on the same observation stream and uses an Energy Score to compare human action trajectories with policy inference, triggering data collection when the discrepancy signals an out-of-distribution region. A separate feedback loop uses low online advantage predictions to refine a progress-based advantage estimator, which then guides advantage-conditioned behavioral cloning on a balanced mixture of base demonstrations and new policy data. This work addresses two persistent limitations of supervised fine-tuning for VLA deployment: static data provide limited coverage of out-of-distribution states, and standard imitation objectives do not distinguish progressing behavior from less useful data. By decoupling data collection from robot deployment, HIL-UMI offers a potentially scalable path for VLA post-training across operators and locations, reducing the need for repeated physical robot interaction. Experiments on four real-world tasks show consistent improvement over SFT and better performance than HG-DAgger on Clean Up Table with lower per-frame collection time. HIL-UMI queries the policy on the same observation stream during handheld demonstrations but does not execute its predictions, and the Energy Score triggers collection only when human-policy discrepancy indicates an out-of-distribution region. The method combines targeted collection with advantage refinement, and its evaluation spans four real-world tasks covering long-horizon and precise manipulation; the paper reports lower per-frame collection time than HG-DAgger on Clean Up Table.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language-action (VLA) models are large models that connect visual observations and natural-language instructions directly to physical actions, providing powerful priors for robot manipulation. The Universal Manipulation Interface (UMI) is an embodiment-agnostic data collection, action representation, and policy deployment framework that allows direct skill transfer from in-the-wild human demonstrations to deployable robot policies. Human-in-the-loop learning typically requires repeated policy execution and human intervention on a physical robot, which HIL-UMI aims to avoid by keeping the loop robot-free during handheld demonstrations.

## [ActObs: Supervising Observation Tokens Improves RL Agent Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20715) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new paper introduces ActObs, a supervised fine-tuning method that applies loss not only to agent-authored action tokens but also to the environment observation tokens already present in each trajectory. On Qwen3-4B, GRPO trained from ActObs achieves higher pass@k at every evaluated sampling budget on Terminal-Bench 2.0, and on Qwen3-8B it gains +3.4 pp at pass@16 while solving more distinct tasks, all without adding data, parameters, sequence tokens, or forward passes. This finding challenges the standard convention that observation tokens should only serve as context during SFT, showing that joint supervision produces a better initialization for downstream reinforcement learning. It could influence how agent policies are initialized across the field, since the benefit appears without extra data or compute and even transfers to unseen cross-domain tasks. The advantage extends to cross-domain code editing on aider-polyglot (+4.2 pp at pass@1 at 4B), whose tasks are unseen during both SFT and RL. ActObs retains more entropy during RL while requiring less policy movement, leaving the final policy closer to its SFT initialization; analysis traces this to action and observation gradients rapidly becoming orthogonal, whereas action-only training leaves a large residual observation gradient and degrades environment prediction below the base model.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) is the standard first stage for training LLM agents, typically applying loss only to the action tokens the agent generates while treating environment observations as context. GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization), proposed by DeepSeek, is a reinforcement learning algorithm designed for LLM training that optimizes policies using group-relative rewards. pass@k measures the probability that at least one of k independently sampled solutions is correct, making it a common metric for evaluating exploration and capability in code and agent benchmarks such as Terminal-Bench 2.0.

<a id="item-186"></a>
## [LLM-Falsifier Uses Language Models to Find CPS Counterexamples](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20752) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces LLM-Falsifier, an LLM-based method that falsifies Signal Temporal Logic (STL) specifications in cyber-physical systems by minimizing the STL robustness degree. It exposes the LLM to semantic information such as natural-language input/output names, output trajectories, and critical-time witnesses, and outperforms existing falsification tools on 14 of 21 ARCH-COMP benchmarks by average simulations needed to find a counterexample. This work bridges two previously separate fields—LLM-based optimization and formal verification of cyber-physical systems—showing that semantic, language-friendly information can make robustness search more sample-efficient than purely numerical black-box methods. If it generalizes, it could reduce the testing burden for safety-critical systems such as autonomous vehicles and industrial controllers, where finding counterexamples is expensive. The approach formulates falsification as a robustness optimization problem and augments generic prompt-based optimization with semantic cues absent from standard numerical optimizers, including natural-language signal names and critical-time witnesses. Evaluation is on the ARCH-COMP falsification benchmarks, where it beats surrogate-based, Bayesian, and search-based testing tools on 14 of 21 specifications when measured by average number of simulations to a counterexample.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Cyber-physical systems (CPS) tightly couple software with physical processes, and their correctness is often specified in Signal Temporal Logic (STL), a formal language for expressing temporal properties over real-valued, continuous-time signals. STL comes with a quantitative robustness degree that measures how strongly a signal satisfies or violates a specification, so falsification—searching for inputs that violate the specification—can be cast as minimizing this robustness. Traditionally this minimization is done with black-box search algorithms, while large language models have recently shown promise as optimizers when guided by iterative prompting.

<a id="item-187"></a>
## [RetireOPD Lets Agentic RL Students Retire Their Teachers Adaptively](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20784) ⭐️ 7.0/10

RetireOPD introduces a self-retiring on-policy distillation framework for agentic reinforcement learning, in which a skill-free student first learns from a decoupled, skill-conditioned teacher and then drops the teacher once their discrepancy stops shrinking and the student reaches a target fraction of the teacher's success rate. Across Qwen2.5 models from 1.5B to 7B, it improves ALFWorld success rate by 14.1% to 18.8% and WebShop accuracy by 11.8% to 19.0% over an RL baseline, surpassing its own skill-conditioned teacher in every setting. This work challenges the common assumption that privileged information always makes a teacher reliable and that teacher supervision helps at every training stage, offering a more robust recipe for training multi-turn LLM agents. If the adaptive retirement mechanism generalizes, it could reduce wasted computation and teacher-induced bias in agentic RL pipelines used for tool use, web navigation, and embodied tasks. The method first optimizes a decoupled, skill-conditioned teacher with environment rewards, then trains a skill-free student jointly with RL and on-policy distillation, replacing a predefined distillation schedule with an adaptive retirement criterion based on discrepancy shrinkage and success-rate ratio. Experiments cover Qwen2.5 models from 1.5B to 7B parameters on ALFWorld and WebShop, and the student ultimately outperforms its own teacher in every setting.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student on its own sampled trajectories while a teacher provides dense token-level supervision, which is attractive for multi-turn agents because reinforcement learning typically gives only one scalar reward per whole trajectory. In the self-teacher variant, the teacher is derived from the same model but has access to privileged task skills, so the student can internalize those skills without them at test time. RetireOPD builds on this paradigm but questions two of its assumptions: that privileged information guarantees a reliable teacher, and that teacher supervision is equally useful at all training stages.

## [ERCPMP-Gx: Multi-Modal Endoscopic Dataset for Colorectal Polyposis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20815) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers released ERCPMP-Gx, a publicly accessible dataset that links endoscopic images and video clips with histopathology and germline genomic findings at the patient level for colorectal polyposis syndromes. It contains 160 images plus video clips captured mostly with the Olympus EVIS X1 system using white-light endoscopy, narrow-band imaging (NBI), magnifying NBI, and NBI with near focus, with about 80% of cases being confirmed hereditary polyposis syndromes such as FAP, PJS, JPS, and GNS. Existing public endoscopic datasets are organized around individual sporadic polyps and do not link polyposis phenotype to histopathology and germline findings at the patient level, so this dataset fills a clear gap. It could enable AI systems to recognize and classify hereditary polyposis syndromes earlier, supporting timely diagnosis, individualized management, and targeted family surveillance. Each record is linked, where available, to standardized endoscopic annotations, representative histopathology, and clinically reported germline findings, forming an AI-ready patient-level annotation framework. The remaining 20% of cases are non-hereditary polyps and polyp-mimicking lesions with overlapping morphology, included to support differential classification; the dataset is hosted on Mendeley with updates via the DataBioX website.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Hereditary polyposis syndromes are inherited conditions marked by numerous polyps in the colon, rectum, and sometimes other parts of the gastrointestinal tract, and they raise the risk of colorectal cancer and extracolonic tumors. Narrow-band imaging is an endoscopic technique that uses specific blue and green wavelengths to enhance mucosal and vascular detail, helping clinicians predict histology in real time. The Olympus EVIS X1 is a modern endoscopy platform that integrates advanced imaging modes such as NBI and magnifying NBI, which this dataset uses to capture polyp morphology.

## [Paint-Anything Enables Any-Color Control via Hex Prompts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20816) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced Paint-Anything, a method that learns a shared hex-prompt interface for object-level color control in both image generation and editing, trained on a new 500K dataset called Paint-500K. On FLUX.2-4B, it improves ACBench-T2I and ACBench-Edit scores by 85.3% and 28.3% respectively over the base model, and achieves the highest average CompColor score among compared methods. This work offers a unified, precise color control mechanism that could simplify professional design workflows by letting users specify exact 24-bit hex colors for objects in both generation and editing. It also contributes a large-scale dataset and a new benchmark, which may accelerate research on controllable image synthesis. The training recipe combines real-image supervision with pure-color anchors whose pixels exactly match their paired hex values, using these anchors only at high-noise timesteps while leaving low-noise training to natural images. The authors also introduce the Any Color Benchmark (ACBench), comprising ACBench-T2I and ACBench-Edit, to measure object-level hex color fidelity.

**Background**: Controllable image generation and editing with diffusion models has advanced rapidly, but precise color control often requires dedicated color representations or specialized inference procedures. Prior work such as Control Color explores multimodal colorization, yet specifying an exact 24-bit hex value for an object's color in both generation and editing remains challenging. Paint-Anything addresses this by leveraging large language models' ability to associate hex values with color semantics, using a simple shared hex-prompt interface.

## [FAMOS: Feed-Forward 3D Articulation Modeling from Sparse Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20817) ⭐️ 7.0/10

FAMOS is a new feed-forward model that predicts movable-part segmentation and joint parameters directly from a sparse, unordered set of partial point clouds, jointly reasoning over multiple observations and supporting a variable number of inputs, including a single view. It introduces a Multi-state Articulation Transformer with alternating state-wise and global attention, plus an observed articulation span objective that supervises the motion range each part exhibits across the input observations. Most existing feed-forward methods infer articulation from a single observation and rely heavily on learned category-level shape priors, so FAMOS's ability to aggregate cues across multiple sparse views could improve robustness for 3D vision and robotics applications where only partial geometry is available. Experiments on PartNet-Mobility, ACD, and ArtiCraft-10K show consistent improvements over both feed-forward and optimization-based baselines. To overcome the limited scale and diversity of existing datasets, the authors introduce a procedural data generator that synthesizes self-annotated assets during training. The model naturally handles a variable number of inputs, including a single view, and the observed articulation span objective encourages it to leverage the full observation set rather than relying on a single view.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Articulated objects such as drawers, doors, and scissors consist of multiple rigid parts connected by joints, and modeling their movable-part segmentation and joint parameters is important for robotics and 3D scene understanding. Feed-forward models predict these properties in a single forward pass, unlike optimization-based methods that fit articulation per object, but they typically require dense observations or strong category-level priors. Sparse monocular views are challenging because each observation reveals only partial geometry and motion evidence, motivating methods that can jointly reason over multiple partial observations.

## [Workspace Tokens: Lightweight Robotic Memory via Saliency Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20820) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new paper (arXiv:2609.20820) introduces 'workspace tokens,' a lightweight latent memory for robot policies trained by distilling train-time VLM saliency queries into a compact representation. The method uses a VLM to identify task-salient current and historical information, then distills it via a set-reconstruction decoder loss, enabling long-horizon manipulation without in-the-loop VLM calls at deployment. This addresses a real bottleneck in long-horizon manipulation: conditioning on full histories causes spurious correlations, while in-the-loop VLM queries are computationally expensive. By moving expensive VLM reasoning to train-time, the approach enables efficient deployment and reportedly improves policy performance, which could benefit robot learning and embodied AI researchers. The workspace token is trained in two stages: a VLM identifies necessary current and historical information, then a set-reconstruction decoder loss distills this into the token. It serves as a drop-in replacement for observations during deployment, and the authors validated it in both simulation and hardware, finding it not only more lightweight but also yielding better policy performance.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Long-horizon robotic manipulation requires remembering past events and actions, but conditioning policies on full histories can degrade performance. Vision-language models (VLMs) can extract task-salient information, but querying them in-the-loop is expensive. This work instead distills VLM saliency into a lightweight latent memory that can be queried efficiently at deployment.

## [Coding Agents Fail at Obstacle Avoidance, SafeHarness Fixes It](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20822) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.20822) evaluates coding agents — language models that write robot controllers as programs — under a safety constraint where each task pairs a manipulation goal with an obstacle the robot must not touch. The agent collides with the obstacle in most cases, and the authors trace the failure to planning rather than perception or instruction, then introduce SafeHarness, which achieves 71.9% task success and 87.5% collision avoidance, surpassing the previous state of the art by 6.5% and 27.0% respectively. This work exposes a critical safety gap in the emerging code-as-policy paradigm for robotics: even when obstacles are perceived and instructions explicitly forbid collisions, the agent's planning never treats safety as a priority. As coding agents move from benchmarks into real-world robot deployment, this failure mode could cause physical damage or injury, making the proposed harness an important step toward safe AI-driven manipulation. The authors decompose manipulation into a route phase and a contact-rich moment to locate the failure: along the route the model has no notion of a clearing path or replanning when a chosen route becomes infeasible, and at contact it is unaware that the same constraint bounds contact execution. SafeHarness addresses both with obstacle-aware route planning, which grounds objects as bounding boxes and draws candidate waypoint routes that the agent plans, verifies, and replans before execution, plus obstacle-aware contact execution that selects contact positions avoiding the obstacle.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Coding agents for robot manipulation, also called code-as-policy (CaP) systems, use a language model to generate executable robot control programs that compose perception and control primitives, allowing robots to operate without robot-specific training. Benchmarks such as CaP-X and CaP-Gym have systematically studied these agents across tasks in Robosuite, LIBERO-PRO, and BEHAVIOR, but prior work focused on capability rather than safety. Safety constraints in robot planning are typically handled with formal methods or conservative policies, and this paper is, to the authors' knowledge, the first coding agent for manipulation whose harness itself enforces obstacle avoidance throughout the task, including contact-rich phases.

Researchers introduced oMeBench, the first large-scale, expert-curated benchmark for organic reaction mechanism reasoning, containing over 10,000 annotated mechanistic steps with reaction type labels, intermediate structures, and difficulty ratings. They also proposed oMeS, a dynamic scoring framework that jointly evaluates step-level logical consistency and chemical structural similarity. This benchmark addresses a critical gap in evaluating whether LLMs possess genuine chemical reasoning rather than superficial pattern matching, which matters for both AI and chemistry communities. It enables more rigorous assessment of models' ability to generate valid intermediates and maintain coherent multi-step pathways, potentially guiding future model development. Systematic evaluation of state-of-the-art LLMs shows they exhibit promising chemical intuition but struggle with correct and consistent multi-step reasoning; notably, combining prompting strategies with fine-tuning allows smaller models to match closed-source frontier models. The benchmark includes reaction type labels, intermediate structures, and difficulty ratings to support fine-grained analysis.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Organic reaction mechanisms describe the step-by-step elementary processes by which reactants transform into intermediates and products, and are fundamental to understanding chemical reactivity and guiding molecular design. While LLMs have shown promise on tasks like synthesis design, it remains unclear whether this reflects genuine chemical reasoning—the ability to generate valid intermediates, maintain consistency across steps, and follow logically coherent pathways. Existing benchmarks often lack the scale and expert annotation needed to rigorously test these capabilities.

## [TripScore: Expert-Calibrated Benchmark Aligns LLMs for Real-World Travel Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.09011) ⭐️ 7.0/10

TripScore introduces a behavior-grounded benchmark and reward framework for LLM-based travel planning, built from real user logs and calibrated against 1,468 pairwise judgments from 203 travel experts. Using TripScore as both evaluator and reward signal, the authors benchmark direct prompting, test-time compute, neuro-symbolic solvers, code agents, and fine-tuning, finding that reinforcement learning fine-tuning (e.g., GRPO) consistently outperforms other approaches under the same base model and practical latency. Existing travel-planning benchmarks assume structured constraint checklists, but real users give minimal or free-form inputs, so TripScore closes a gap between benchmark design and deployed service behavior. The finding that GRPO-based RL fine-tuning consistently wins could guide practitioners toward more effective alignment strategies for real-world LLM applications beyond travel. TripScore couples a hierarchical feasibility gate (format and commonsense) with a unified point-wise reward that aggregates soft quality and preference fulfillment, and it was calibrated using 1,468 pairwise expert judgments from 203 travel experts. The benchmark compares five method families under the same base model and practical latency constraints, with GRPO-based RL fine-tuning showing consistent gains.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Large language models are increasingly deployed in travel-planning services, but aligning them to produce feasible, high-quality itineraries requires evaluation frameworks that reflect real user behavior. GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization) is a reinforcement learning algorithm proposed by DeepSeek that drops PPO's value network and instead samples a group of answers per prompt, using the group's average reward as a baseline. Neuro-symbolic solvers combine neural networks with symbolic reasoning to improve compositionality and verifiability, while reward modeling converts human value judgments into numerical signals that shape LLM behavior.

## [NeuroCognition Benchmark Tests LLM Cognitive Abilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02540) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2603.02540v2) introduces NeuroCognition, a benchmark grounded in three adapted neuropsychological tests — Raven's Progressive Matrices, Spatial Working Memory, and the Wisconsin Card Sorting Test — to evaluate foundational cognitive abilities in LLMs. Evaluating 156 models, the authors find that performance is strong on text but degrades on image-based and more complex tasks, and that LLMs and humans fail at different parts of the same tasks. Current benchmarks mostly measure task completion rather than the foundational cognitive components behind intelligent behavior, so this work offers a more diagnostic way to see where LLMs align with human-like intelligence and where they lack core adaptive cognition. It could influence how the field designs evaluations and guide efforts to improve LLMs using verifiable, scalable cognitive signals. The benchmark adapts Raven's Progressive Matrices for abstract relational reasoning, a Spatial Working Memory test for goal-directed spatial updating, and the Wisconsin Card Sorting Test for cognitive flexibility; factor analysis of 156 models confirmed a unified general capability factor across 10 benchmarks. Notably, complex reasoning was not universally beneficial, while simple human-like strategies yielded partial gains, and NeuroCognition correlated positively with standard general-capability benchmarks while still measuring distinct abilities.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Raven's Progressive Matrices is a nonverbal psychometric test developed in 1936 that measures abstract reasoning and fluid intelligence by asking test-takers to identify the missing element in a pattern. Spatial working memory refers to the limited-capacity system that holds visual and spatial information in mind, often assessed with tasks like the Corsi Block Tapping Test. The Wisconsin Card Sorting Test, developed by Grant and Berg, measures set-shifting and cognitive flexibility by requiring people to adapt to changing sorting rules. This paper adapts these classic neuropsychological instruments into a benchmark for LLMs.

Researchers introduced Oph-Guid-RAG, a multimodal visual retrieval-augmented generation system that treats each clinical guideline page as an independent evidence unit and retrieves page images directly, preserving tables, flowcharts, and layout. On the hard subset of HealthBench, it improved the overall score from 0.2969 to 0.3861 (+30.0%) and accuracy from 0.5956 to 0.6576 (+10.4%) compared to GPT-5.2, with an even larger accuracy gain of +24.4% over GPT-5.4. This work shows that combining vision-based retrieval with controllable reasoning can improve evidence grounding and robustness in clinical AI, particularly on challenging cases that require precise, evidence-based reasoning. It points toward more traceable and reliable clinical decision support tools that cite specific guideline pages, which could help clinicians verify AI-generated recommendations. The system integrates query decomposition, query rewriting, retrieval, reranking, and multimodal reasoning, and uses a controllable retrieval framework with routing and filtering to selectively introduce external evidence and reduce noise. Ablation studies show that reranking, routing, and retrieval design are critical for stable performance, especially under difficult settings, though the authors note further work is needed for completeness.

**Background**: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is a technique that lets large language models retrieve and incorporate information from external sources before generating a response, reducing hallucinations and enabling domain-specific or up-to-date knowledge without retraining. HealthBench is an open-source benchmark introduced by OpenAI in May 2025, built with input from over 250 physicians, that evaluates models on realistic, open-ended, multi-turn healthcare conversations rather than multiple-choice medical exams. <a id="item-197"></a>
## [SFA Repairs Agent Prompts Without Costly Rollout Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10513) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers present Semantic Feature Analysis (SFA), a pipeline that repairs ambiguous agent specifications by reading execution traces the agent has already produced, clustering each workflow node's outputs, decomposing them into semantic feature classes via an extended subject-verb-object schema, ranking features with a decision tree, and injecting the surviving features as corrective statements into the affected node's system prompt. SFA is evaluated against five prompt optimizers (GEPA, MIPROv2, SIMBA, BootstrapFewShot with random search, InferRules) and a single-reflection control, budget-matched in dollars at three budget levels across IF-Bench, HotpotQA, HoVer, and GAIA. Prompt optimization typically pays for the agent twice — once to generate candidate prompts and again to rank them — and on tool-using agents whose rollouts cost dollars and minutes, that ranking cost dominates, causing budget-constrained optimizers to fail to find any improvement. By eliminating rollout-based selection entirely, SFA offers a practical path to improving real-world agents where each evaluation is expensive, and it shows the largest gains precisely where rollouts are most costly. SFA never ranks candidate prompts, so it never spends a rollout on selection; on GAIA, where budget-constrained optimizers cannot afford to score even one candidate, SFA improves accuracy while the other arms return their seed prompt unchanged. The approach relies on an extended subject-verb-object schema for semantic feature decomposition and a decision tree to rank features by their contribution to outcome separation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Natural-language agent specifications are inherently ambiguous: when a system prompt leaves behavior underdetermined, identical inputs can follow divergent execution paths and produce inconsistent outcomes. The standard remedy is prompt optimization, which proposes candidate prompts, runs the agent to score them, and keeps the best — a loop that is expensive because each agent run (a rollout) can cost real money and time. SFA instead mines execution traces the agent has already generated, so it repairs the specification without running any new search.

## [SCICONVBENCH benchmarks LLMs on multi-turn clarification in computational science](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18630) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced SCICONVBENCH, a new benchmark that evaluates large language models on multi-turn clarification when formulating computational science tasks across four domains: fluid mechanics, solid mechanics, materials science, and partial differential equations. The benchmark pairs a structured task ontology with a rubric-based evaluation framework measuring clarification behavior, conversational grounding, and final-specification fidelity, and finds that even the best frontier model resolves only 52.7% of disambiguation cases in fluid mechanics. Existing LLM benchmarks for science typically assume the problem is already well-posed, but real scientific assistance often starts from vague or contradictory user requests that must be refined through dialogue. SCICONVBENCH fills this gap by measuring the upstream conversational reasoning that a reliable computational science assistant requires, highlighting a significant weakness in current frontier models. The benchmark targets two complementary capabilities: eliciting missing information (disambiguation) and detecting and correcting internally contradictory requests (inconsistency resolution). It also finds that frontier LLMs frequently make silent assumptions and perform implicit specification repairs that are not grounded in the conversation with users.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: In scientific computing, an ill-posed problem is one where small changes in the data can cause arbitrarily large changes in the results, so the problem must be carefully specified before computation. Large language models are increasingly used as scientific assistants, but most benchmarks evaluate them on well-posed tasks such as knowledge retrieval, reasoning, code generation, and tool use. Multi-turn dialogue systems, where an LLM asks clarifying questions before answering, are an active area of research, and SCICONVBENCH extends this to computational science task formulation.

## [Routing State Yields Consistent Multi-Resolution Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22866) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2605.22866v2) shows that the routing state accumulated by adaptive hierarchical systems already defines a coherent attribution over the hierarchy, where a leaf receives the product of local routing weights along its path and an internal node receives the corresponding prefix product. The authors demonstrate that every finer readout sums exactly to its coarser counterpart, and validate the approach across LLM, Census, agentic, and telecom-network hierarchies. This offers a hierarchical explanation that requires no separate attribution model, since the same routing state supports consistent explanations at multiple levels of the system. It could simplify interpretability work for any adaptive system that routes among models, tools, services, or policies, and enables direct comparison with Shapley attribution to check whether deployed preferences match counterfactual capabilities. The attribution describes the preferences learned by the deployed router rather than an intrinsic or counterfactual value of a component, and the clearest structure need not appear at the leaves — in the telecom study, Site- or Region-level readouts usually reveal clearer structure than Cell-level readouts. Comparison with Shapley attribution can then show whether deployment-time preferences match capabilities revealed by counterfactual coalitions.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Adaptive hierarchical systems increasingly choose among models, tools, services, or policies through a hierarchy of selectors, accumulating routing state as they learn which components to select. Attribution methods such as Shapley values explain a prediction by treating features as players in a game and averaging their marginal contributions across coalitions, but they typically require a separate attribution model and can be computationally expensive. This paper argues that the router's own learned state already encodes a mathematically consistent attribution, avoiding the need for an extra model.

## [LLM Compression Can Distort Financial Investment Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.29251) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2606.29251v3) introduces the concept of "information fidelity" in LLM-based context compression, showing that compressed financial documents can remain fluent and factually plausible while still altering the downstream investment decision supported by the original source. The authors identify two diagnostic failure patterns—decontextualization and model dependency—and propose Agentic Context Compression, which generates multiple candidate compressions and audits their disagreements against the source. As LLM agents increasingly handle financial filings and earnings-call transcripts, silent fidelity loss could compound across intermediate steps and propagate distorted judgments through entire decision pipelines. This suggests that compression should be evaluated not only for efficiency or factuality but also for its ability to preserve decision-relevant context, affecting both AI/ML researchers and financial practitioners deploying agentic systems. The paper analyzes financial filings and earnings-call transcripts, finding that decontextualization retains salient evidence but strips away the caveats and contextual qualifiers needed for correct interpretation, while model dependency means different compressors expose different views of the same source. The proposed Agentic Context Compression mitigates this by generating multiple candidate compressions and auditing their disagreements against the original source.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Context compression is a technique for shortening long documents so that LLMs with limited context windows can process them, and it is widely used in retrieval-augmented and agentic pipelines. Prior work has largely measured compression quality by efficiency or factual accuracy, but this paper argues that a compressed summary can be factually correct yet still mislead a decision-maker by omitting qualifiers that change the meaning of the retained facts.

## [AI Training Manager: LLM-Guided Bounded Control of Training Recipes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.29871) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2606.29871v2) introduces the AI Training Manager, a bounded LLM-based metacognitive layer that asynchronously monitors training telemetry and applies constrained, deterministically verified interventions. In a GPT-2-style model trained on TinyStories, it reduced final validation loss by 54.3% under an induced overfitting failure, and in robotic reaching RL it raised safe success from 0.413 to 0.705 (conservative regime) and from 0.121 to 0.764 (aggressive regime) using the same manager instructions. This work suggests that bounded LLM reasoning can serve as a practical metacognitive control layer over ongoing learning processes, potentially reducing the need for manual hyperparameter tuning and making training more robust to stress regimes. If validated, such an approach could benefit practitioners in both supervised learning and reinforcement learning who struggle with overfitting and unstable training dynamics. The manager operates through a constrained, deterministically verified action interface, ensuring interventions are bounded and safe. The same manager instructions and action surface were used across both the conservative and aggressive RL stress regimes, demonstrating generality, though the work is a preprint without community discussion yet.

Reinforcement learning for robotic reaching involves training agents to move a robot arm to target positions while satisfying safety constraints. The AI Training Manager builds on the idea of metacognitive monitoring, where a higher-level controller observes and adjusts an ongoing learning process.

## [Model Capability, Not Message Format, Governs Multi-Hop Agent Relay Fidelity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09678) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2607.09678v2) introduces a controlled relay testbed where briefs of twelve programmatic atomic facts are re-encoded hop by hop in five formats (free natural language, precision-instructed NL, JSON, triples, key-value) over six hops, scored against programmatic ground truth by a fixed strong grader. The study finds that a strong relay model is nearly lossless across all formats (hop-6 QA recall ≥ 0.973), while under a weak 1.5B relay the across-format dispersion of hop-6 recall grows by a factor of 8.7, driven by an encode-drift trade-off that flips the format ranking in transit. This work addresses a disagreement between format-optimization and format-restriction literatures by showing that in multi-hop relays, copy fidelity rather than one-shot generation quality dominates, and that model capability is the primary determinant of information fidelity. The finding that structure buys a faithful, error-localizing channel rather than an error-correcting code has direct implications for designing multi-agent systems and choosing relay models. Per-hop cognitive load raises generation cost by 24-53% while fidelity changes stay within ±1.8 points, and once an injected error is present, every format propagates it faithfully (surface persistence 83-100%) with no format cascading collateral damage onto neighboring facts. The residual loss in the strong relay is concentrated at the first encoding step, and the weak relay's format ranking flips in transit due to an encode-drift trade-off.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Multi-hop LLM agent relays involve agents passing information to one another across multiple steps, where copy fidelity (preserving the original message) matters more than one-shot generation quality. Prior work disagreed on whether structured message formats like JSON or key-value pairs help or hurt, with format-optimization studies reporting cost savings and format-restriction studies finding degradation. This paper builds a controlled testbed to measure what actually happens over multiple hops, using programmatic ground truth and a fixed strong grader to isolate format effects from model capability.

Researchers introduced FitAQA, a benchmark for evaluating Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) on fitness action quality assessment, containing 2,219 videos and 5,512 QA instances across 30 bodyweight exercises. Developed with sports science experts, it defines a unified form error taxonomy of 38 recurring errors across six quality dimensions—alignment, symmetry, stability, coordination, tempo, and completeness—and formulates three tasks: perception, judgement, and temporal grounding. Fitness AQA is important for intelligent sports training, yet MLLM capabilities in this setting remain underexplored, and existing benchmarks rely on action-specific annotation schemes that offer limited insight into how models assess exercise quality. FitAQA provides a shared, cross-exercise assessment framework that could guide future MLLM development for sports and rehabilitation applications. Extensive evaluation shows current MLLMs still struggle to comprehensively assess exercise quality and precisely localize form errors; controlled experiments indicate visual perception is a key bottleneck, since judgement performance improves substantially when ground-truth perceptual evidence is provided. The dataset is publicly available on Hugging Face.

## [Spectral Foundation Models' Preprocessing Invariance May Be an Artifact of Input Normalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14227) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2608.14227v3) argues that the standard test for preprocessing invariance in spectral foundation models is confounded, because these models normalize each input before any learned parameter is applied. The authors propose a normalization-only attribution control and find that on six Raman datasets the encoder does not measurably improve transfer over its normalization alone, with an audit of eighteen configurations across five modalities confirming the issue is widespread. This is a methodologically important critique: it suggests that many reported invariance and transfer gains in scientific foundation models may be credited to learned representations when they are actually produced by a fixed normalization step. If adopted, the normalization-only control could change how researchers evaluate and report representation learning in scientific domains such as spectroscopy and beyond. The proposed control compares an encoder against its own normalization before interpreting transfer as learned invariance, and a controlled experiment confirms invariance only develops when variation reaches the encoder past normalization. Across three systems, no encoder improved relative retention over its normalization, and the audit of eighteen configurations across five modalities indicates the problem is not limited to Raman data.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Spectral foundation models are pretrained models intended to work across many spectroscopy tasks, and a key selling point is that they stay accurate even when different labs preprocess spectra differently. The usual test trains a classifier under one preprocessing pipeline and evaluates it under another, treating preserved accuracy as proof of learned invariance. However, these models typically rescale each spectrum by its own mean and standard deviation before the encoder sees it, so differently preprocessed spectra can be mapped to identical vectors, meaning the encoder receives the same input either way.

## [AutoResearch: Two-Stage System Grounds Ideas to Cut Hallucination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17906) ⭐️ 7.0/10

AutoResearch, a new two-stage autonomous research system, links grounded Idea Generation with coordinated Idea Execution to reduce hallucinations in long research workflows. On the RSICD cross-modal retrieval benchmark, an AutoResearch-generated idea raised mean Recall from 32.84 to 34.69 while producing only 5 audit-confirmed issue events, versus 11-27 for other autonomous research systems. Automation alone does not guarantee scientific grounding, so a system that verifies ideas before experimentation and conclusions before acceptance could make LLM-driven research more trustworthy. This matters for AI/ML researchers and labs adopting autonomous agents for scientific discovery, where unreliable results can waste compute and mislead follow-up work. Idea Generation continuously integrates emerging research signals with accumulated domain knowledge, extracts transferable mechanistic insights, and uses multi-model generation plus cross-review to produce testable plans. Idea Execution then has coordinated agents decompose plans into experiments, iteratively implement and diagnose them, and apply independent evidence-based review before accepting conclusions, deciding to continue, revise, or terminate a direction.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Autonomous research systems built on large language models can already run long research workflows, but they often generate plausible-sounding yet unsupported claims, a problem known as hallucination. Grounding techniques, such as retrieval-augmented generation, tie model outputs to external evidence, while multi-agent systems split research tasks among specialized agents. AutoResearch combines both ideas, applying grounding at the idea stage and evidence-based cross-review at the execution stage.

## [LayerRoute Adds Action-Conditioned Layer Routing to VLA Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06079) ⭐️ 7.0/10

LayerRoute introduces an action-conditioned representation routing interface for Vision-Language-Action policies, combining a Layer Mixture Router that dynamically forms mixtures of cached VLM layer representations with an Action-State Reread mechanism that explicitly reuses earlier action representations. Across simulation and real-world benchmarks it consistently improves StarVLA-π and π0.5, gaining up to 7.2 points on LIBERO Long while adding only 0.31% to 3.87% extra parameters. Existing VLA interfaces expose VLM features through fixed layer assignments and only propagate intermediate action states implicitly via residual streams, which limits how flexibly a policy can draw on hierarchical visual-semantic information. By making representation access adaptive and action-conditioned, LayerRoute could improve robot manipulation performance at very low parameter cost, which matters for robotics and multimodal learning researchers building on pretrained VLMs. The method caches VLM layer representations and routes among them per action layer, while Action-State Reread reintroduces earlier action representations rather than relying solely on residual propagation. Ablations confirm the benefit of action-conditioned layer routing, and routing analyses show structured allocation patterns across action layers and task settings; the abstract is truncated, so full experimental scope remains to be verified.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) policies use pretrained vision-language models to guide robot control, binding camera images and language instructions to motor actions. VLM representations are hierarchical: early layers capture local visual geometry while deeper layers encode abstract, language-aligned semantics, so different manipulation tasks may need different mixtures of layers. Mixture-of-layers routing, inspired by mixture-of-experts gating, lets a network dynamically choose which layer representations to use instead of following a fixed order.

## [Inference-Time Nash Alignment for General Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08082) ⭐️ 7.0/10

This paper initiates the study of inference-time alignment under general preferences by formulating it as finding a Nash equilibrium of a two-player zero-sum game between policies. It proposes two algorithms, Best-of-Nash (BoN) and Nash Mirror Descent (NMD), and proves both achieve a duality gap matching the problem's lower bound. Existing inference-time alignment methods rely on a scalar reward model derived under the Bradley-Terry assumption, which cannot represent general preferences, so this work extends cost-effective alignment to richer preference structures without updating model parameters. It matters for practitioners who cannot access model weights of state-of-the-art models but still need alignment. The two algorithms are evaluated on three datasets, substantially outperforming the base policy and converging to the performance of fine-tuned models, with NMD remaining robust across the regularization parameter. The theoretical guarantee is a duality gap that matches the problem lower bound.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Preference-based fine-tuning methods such as RLHF and DPO require substantial compute, large preference datasets, and direct access to model parameters, which many state-of-the-art models do not provide. Inference-time alignment offers a cheaper alternative that leaves parameters unchanged, but it typically assumes a scalar reward model derived from the Bradley-Terry model, which maps latent utility differences to preference probabilities and cannot capture general preferences. A two-player zero-sum game is a setting where one player's gain equals the other's loss, and its Nash equilibrium can be found by solving a linear programming problem.

## [Generator Synthesizes Consistent Fictional Enterprises Without Real Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11286) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.11286v2) describes a generator that, given an industry, company size, business model, application set, and random seed, produces a complete fictional enterprise — workforce, customers, deals, tickets, calls, chats, and documents — all mutually consistent. One entity graph is projected into the native formats of 66 business products, and realism is verified by reference-free measurement; over 23 generated companies, mean realism rose from 60.3 to 99.1 and an adversarial detector that initially flagged 55.2% of records now flags none. Enterprise software testing and AI training often require realistic multi-system business data, but real datasets are scarce, privacy-sensitive, and hard to obtain. Realism is built in from cited reference statistics and checked by a five-axis scorecard of 28 statistical checks, an adversarial detector, and soundness checks including a classifier test against an independently shuffled copy of the data. A second generator builds relational databases from a list of business questions, forcing qualifying rows, adding controlled near misses, and computing exact labels; the service runs at console.era.eon.io and is exposed via MCP and REST, with simulators also published as container images for offline use.
