## [SoftTri: Smooth Differentiable Triangular Membership Functions for Fuzzy Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20194) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.20194) introduces SoftTri, a differentiable triangular membership function built from a smooth soft-hinge mechanism inspired by Swish-type activations. It provides C-infinity smoothness with respect to both the input and the membership parameters (a, b, c) for any finite sharpness parameter beta > 0, with closed-form analytical gradients enabling fully differentiable backpropagation. Classical triangular membership functions are widely used in fuzzy systems for their interpretability and low parameter complexity, but their non-differentiability at knot points has long hindered gradient-based optimization in adaptive neuro-fuzzy architectures. SoftTri offers a practical compromise that preserves interpretability while enabling stable gradient-based learning, potentially benefiting researchers building adaptive fuzzy inference systems. SoftTri was integrated into a Takagi-Sugeno fuzzy neural network with grid-partitioned rules and evaluated on one-dimensional and two-dimensional nonlinear approximation benchmarks plus the real-world Airfoil Self-Noise regression dataset. Experiments show it consistently improves optimization stability and approximation accuracy over classical triangular MFs, and matches or exceeds Gaussian MFs under identical rule structures and training settings.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Fuzzy inference systems combine interpretable rule-based reasoning with the learning capability of adaptive models. Membership functions define how input values map to fuzzy degrees between 0 and 1; triangular MFs use three parameters (a, b, c) for the start, peak, and end points, making them simple and interpretable but non-differentiable at those knot points. Neuro-fuzzy systems such as Takagi-Sugeno networks rely on gradient descent, so non-differentiability forces workarounds like subgradient approximations or heuristic smoothing, which SoftTri aims to replace with a principled smooth formulation.

Urban cellular activity forecasting underpins network capacity planning and resource allocation, and SCRR-Net's ability to adapt spatial dependencies and cross-task knowledge to changing urban scenes could improve accuracy over fixed-graph or static multi-task baselines. SCRR-Net combines a context encoder, a spatial graph expert routing module, a temporal Transformer encoder, and a task knowledge routing module, with urban context jointly controlling spatial dependency selection and cross-task knowledge transfer. Evaluation covers SMS, network traffic, and call activity forecasting on two Italian city datasets, though the abstract does not report specific error metrics or computational costs.

**Background**: Urban cellular activity forecasting involves predicting multiple related signals, such as SMS volume, mobile data traffic, and call counts, across city regions over time. Traditional approaches separate temporal modeling, spatial relation learning, and multi-signal prediction, often relying on fixed graph structures or static multi-task learning that cannot adapt to changing urban scenes. ## [Lens: Training-Free Multimodal Representation Learning via Semantic Perspective Anchoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20252) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces Lens, a training-free framework for multimodal representation learning that addresses 'semantic perspective misalignment' — the tendency of existing semantic-elicitation methods to produce representations dominated by salient input content rather than the perspective required by the downstream task. Lens combines Semantic Perspective Anchoring, which ties the task-required perspective to a task-specific readout phrase, with Contextualized Phrase Readout, which places that phrase after the full input and aggregates its token states. Without parameter updates, architectural modification, or reranking, Lens achieves an overall Precision@1 of 63.9 across all 36 MMEB datasets, outperforming the closest same-backbone training-free embedding baseline by 10.2 points. This work suggests that large pretrained autoregressive and multimodal LLMs can serve as strong embedding models without any fine-tuning, potentially lowering the cost of building task-specific retrieval and representation systems. If the 10.2-point gain generalizes, it could shift practitioners away from dedicated embedding models toward prompt-based readout from existing LLMs. Lens requires no parameter updates, architectural changes, or reranking, and its evaluation spans all 36 MMEB datasets with a same-backbone comparison. However, the abstract is truncated and the work is an arXiv preprint without peer review, so details on the readout phrase design, computational overhead, and generalization beyond MMEB remain to be verified.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Multimodal representation learning aims to project information from different modalities, such as text and images, into a shared latent space so that semantically similar content maps to nearby points, enabling tasks like cross-modal retrieval. Dedicated embedding models are explicitly trained for this purpose, but their training data are often smaller and less diverse than the corpora used to pretrain modern LLMs and multimodal LLMs. Training-free approaches try to extract useful representations from these pretrained models without additional optimization, but they can be unreliable when the extracted states do not reflect the semantic perspective the downstream task needs.

A new arXiv study (2609.20273) proposes an asynchronous hybrid BCI paradigm that uses eye-tracking for direct target selection and motor imagery (MI) for confirmation, evaluated with 15 healthy participants on a 16-channel EEG system. The hybrid paradigm reached up to 100% accuracy and outperformed conventional MI across all channel configurations, while limited-channel setups (SVM: 0.58) matched full-montage performance (SVM: 0.54). The study found that MI-related information is predominantly localized in motor cortex regions, and that visual fixation enhances neural response stability. The evaluation was limited to 15 healthy participants using a 16-channel EEG system, so the reported 100% hybrid accuracy should be treated as preliminary until tested with larger and clinical populations.

Researchers propose STR-Agent, an LLM-driven agent framework that unifies intent perception, tool-based execution, experience accumulation, and reflection-based policy adaptation for QoS-aware routing in LEO satellite networks. Simulations on a Walker-Delta constellation show up to 60% lower end-to-end delay versus DQ-Dijkstra, intent-understanding accuracy rising from 45.4% to 92.45% after supervised fine-tuning, and an additional 120 ms delay reduction at 600 Mbps from the Reflection Module. This work addresses a practical gap in intent-based networking: translating unstructured natural-language service requests into adaptive routing decisions in highly dynamic LEO constellations. If validated beyond simulation, such LLM-agent architectures could simplify network management for satellite operators and support differentiated QoS for diverse services. The Perception Module converts natural-language requests into structured routing semantics, while the Reflection Module dynamically adjusts the service-to-routing-policy mapping based on real-time congestion and historical routing outcomes instead of a fixed objective. The authors also build a domain-specific supervised fine-tuning dataset for LEO service understanding, though results are limited to simulation and the paper is an unrefereed arXiv preprint.

## [SCGFM-ART Aligns Heterogeneous Graphs via Amortized Relational Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20419) ⭐️ 6.0/10

SCGFM-ART proposes a structure-centric graph foundation model that aligns arbitrary graphs onto a shared relational atlas using Amortized Relational Transport (ART), which directly predicts graph-to-base transport plans instead of running costly Gromov-Wasserstein optimization at inference time. Benchmarked on 14 cross-domain graph- and node-level classification tasks, it achieves state-of-the-art average ranks of 2.29 and 1.14, and delivers 44.2 to 85.1 times faster frozen target-domain inference. Graph foundation models aim to learn transferable representations across severely heterogeneous domains, but domain shifts in topology, scale, and feature semantics make a unified representation space hard to build. By turning alignment into a learned, amortized prediction rather than a per-test optimization, SCGFM-ART could make cross-domain graph transfer substantially more practical for the graph learning community. The framework models graphs and atlas bases as finite measured relational spaces, establishing coordinate fidelity bounds, stability under predicted transport plans, and an amortized coverage bound that guarantees the learning objective tightly surrogates ideal relational coverage. Topological perturbation analyses show that node-role transport retains fine-grained structural nuances beyond global coordinates, though the work is currently a preprint without community validation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Graph foundation models (GFMs) extend the foundation-model paradigm to graph-structured data, seeking scalable, general-purpose representations that transfer across tasks and domains. Gromov-Wasserstein optimization is a classic optimal-transport technique for aligning graphs or point clouds by comparing their internal relational structure, but it is computationally expensive and typically solved iteratively per pair. SCGFM-ART replaces this runtime alignment with a learned predictor that maps any graph onto a fixed set of relational landmarks, called a relational atlas, which acts as a universal coordinate system.

Global affect functions as a central control signal that modulates learning rate, representational valence, and the exploration-exploitation balance. The formalization also covers secondary perception, representational competition, curiosity, procedural gaps, and action selection aimed at limiting allostatic violations.

## [Bayesian Smoothing Method Improves Disaggregated AI Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20758) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces prediction-powered smoothing (PP-S), a Bayesian method that fits a model to each domain's prediction-powered estimate and borrows strength across a reporting taxonomy (PP-TS) to improve domain-level mean estimates in disaggregated AI evaluation. The authors also derive an approximately unbiased design-based cross-validation score for selecting among direct and smoothed estimators, validated on a curated benchmark and deployed agent traffic. Disaggregated evaluation is essential for understanding how AI systems perform across different task types or conversation domains, but exhaustive labeling is expensive and direct estimators become imprecise when labels are scarce. This work offers a principled statistical workflow that improves both point and interval estimates at the same sampling budget, which could make domain-level AI evaluation more reliable and cost-effective. The method treats the evaluation set as a finite population and builds on small area estimation, with PP-S fitting a Bayesian model to each domain's prediction-powered estimate and PP-TS extending it to borrow strength across a reporting taxonomy. The proposed estimators achieve near-nominal coverage, and the new cross-validation score selects estimators as well as an independent validation sample while estimating the selected estimator's error far more accurately.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Prediction-powered inference (PPI) is a statistical framework that combines abundant machine-learning predictions with a small set of gold-standard labels to produce valid and efficient inference. Small area estimation is a related family of techniques that improves estimates for small subpopulations by borrowing strength from similar areas or auxiliary data, often using models such as Fay-Herriot. This paper brings these ideas together for AI evaluation, where performance varies across domains and labeled data is limited.

## [GeoAAC Adapts Action Horizons via Denoising Trajectory Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20776) ⭐️ 6.0/10

GeoAAC introduces a geometry-based adaptive action chunking method for flow-based Vision-Language-Action (VLA) policies that adjusts the action horizon based on the reliability of the current action prediction. It uses the prefix-wise geometry of Flow Matching denoising trajectories to build a horizon-wise geometric profile, determining the horizon from a single generation without additional training, and achieves up to 8.7 percentage points improvement in simulation and raises real-world success from 53.3% to 74.4% with GR00T N1.5 and π0.5. Fixed action horizons cannot accommodate changing control requirements across task stages, so this adaptive approach could make VLA policies more robust and precise in real-world robotic manipulation. It offers a training-free, plug-in improvement that may benefit embodied AI systems relying on flow-based action generation. The method exploits the positive correlation between geometric variation across action prefixes and predictive uncertainty, constructing a horizon-wise geometric profile from a single denoising process. It requires no additional training and was validated on LIBERO, LIBERO-Pro, RoboCasa365, and real-world manipulation tasks, consistently outperforming fixed-horizon and existing adaptive baselines.

## [Continual Learning Bound for Multi-Agent Policy Reuse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.06813) ⭐️ 6.0/10

This paper formalizes a continual learning problem in decentralized multi-agent reinforcement learning, showing that peer policy updates change each agent's induced rewards and dynamics even when the joint Markov game is stationary. It derives a sharp conditioning bound limiting coverage loss to ε/p0 and, combined with bounded peer updates of size η, certifies Ω(1/η) survival of reusable successful patterns, with a matching Θ(1/η) first-exit law under an effective-conflict condition. This provides a theoretical foundation for understanding when and why reusable successful behaviors in multi-agent systems degrade as peers keep learning, which is central to building robust decentralized AI systems. It could inform the design of policy libraries, transfer mechanisms, and intervention strategies in continual multi-agent settings. The analysis introduces an invariant core representing maximal abstract patterns shared by a high fraction of successful trajectories, where survival refers to a fixed pattern's coverage rather than an unchanged maximal frontier. Empirical support comes from an exactly solvable corridor test, two registered 64-stream studies in continual control and cue-MNIST showing coverage erosion predicts impending failure and enables near-oracle intervention, and an exploratory reanalysis of eight learned-partner Level-Based Foraging pairings.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Decentralized multi-agent reinforcement learning involves multiple agents learning simultaneously without a central controller, so each agent faces a moving target as peers update their policies. Continual learning concerns retaining previously acquired knowledge while learning new tasks, and policy reuse saves and reuses past policies to speed up learning in similar tasks. This paper connects these areas by studying how long successful reusable structures survive under ongoing peer learning.

## [AIREP proposes a vendor-neutral protocol for per-decision AI runtime evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21363) ⭐️ 6.0/10

An arXiv preprint (arXiv:2608.21363v2) presents AIREP, a vendor- and model-independent protocol that records AI runtime governance evidence as one JSON record per decision, split into four artifact families: Decision, Control, Execution, and Effect. The released beta includes a first-party four-family producer, Python and Node reference verifiers, adversarial and lifecycle corpora, and a three-level assurance model (AIREP-Core, AIREP-Authenticated, AIREP-Witnessed). As AI regulation and safety requirements tighten, organizations need auditable records of what an AI system actually decided and did, and AIREP offers a structured, vendor-neutral format that could become a common substrate for such audits. Its separation of decision, control, execution, and effect evidence addresses a real gap, since current audit logs often collapse materially different events into a single record. Artifacts use closed core schemas, explicit identities and digests, declared scope limits, RFC 8785 canonical JSON, domain-separated SHA-256 hashing, and pure Ed25519 signatures, while a structured reconciler keeps failure, missing evidence, unevaluated prerequisites, and indeterminate outcomes as distinct states. The paper explicitly notes that the assurance classes do not establish event truth, that independent implementations target different frozen versions (v0.1.2 producer vs. v0.2 consumer/verifier) and therefore do not demonstrate same-version interoperability, and that AIREP remains experimental.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: AI runtime governance refers to the controls and records that determine whether an AI system's output is released, blocked, deferred, redacted, or escalated, and evidence of those decisions is increasingly needed for compliance and incident investigation. RFC 8785 defines a canonical JSON serialization so that identical data always produces identical bytes, which is essential for reliable hashing and signature verification, while Ed25519 is a widely used modern digital signature algorithm. AIREP builds on these primitives to make each governance decision independently verifiable rather than relying on a single opaque audit log.

## [AquiLLM: Expert Study Tests Faithfulness of Open-Weight RAG-LLM for Science](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16519) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.16519v2) presents a domain-expert evaluation of AquiLLM, an open-weight, offline retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) platform designed to help scientific research groups use and preserve tacit and formal knowledge. Using an astronomy case study, the authors define faithfulness as the degree to which generated responses stay grounded in retrieved scientific context without unsupported claims or omissions, and find that AquiLLM is most reliable on explicit retrieval-oriented questions but degrades on queries requiring synthesis or ambiguity resolution. As research institutions seek data privacy and infrastructure control, open-weight, offline RAG systems are becoming an attractive alternative to proprietary cloud LLMs, so evidence about where their faithfulness breaks down directly informs adoption decisions. The paper also argues that standard benchmark leaderboards are insufficient for scientific use, underscoring the value of domain-expert evaluation for high-stakes research workflows. The evaluation covers both retrieval and scientific analysis tasks in astronomy, and the key caveat is that faithfulness is not uniform: it holds up for questions explicitly grounded in the RAG collection but weakens for synthesis-heavy or ambiguous queries. The study is a specialized arXiv preprint with no accompanying community discussion, and its contribution is framed as an incremental, domain-focused assessment rather than a new model or benchmark.

**Background**: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is a technique in which a large language model first retrieves relevant documents from an external knowledge base and then generates an answer grounded in that retrieved content, which can reduce hallucination compared with relying on model parameters alone. Open-weight models are LLMs whose trained parameters are publicly downloadable, allowing institutions to run and fine-tune them locally instead of using proprietary APIs. Faithfulness is a common RAG evaluation metric measuring whether every claim in a generated answer can be traced back to the retrieved context. ## [Physics-Informed Support Vector Kernels via Green-Function Analogies and Jackson-Chebyshev Spectral Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.11153) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper proposes a physics-informed kernel selection strategy that uses functional forms and spectral structures associated with Green's functions to motivate kernel choice, without requiring an exact identification between a machine-learning kernel and a physical propagator. Its principal construction is a Jackson-damped Chebyshev kernel inspired by the kernel polynomial method (KPM), whose explicit feature map yields a positive-semidefinite Gram matrix by construction and provides an inspectable spectral prior for structured observables. Kernel selection for regression of physical observables is often heuristic, so a principled physics-informed strategy could improve structured regression tasks where data are finite and expensive to compute. This work connects kernel methods, spectral methods, and physics-informed machine learning, potentially benefiting materials science, photonics, and quantum simulation workflows. The authors evaluate standard and custom SVR models on copper-conductivity proxies, local Dirac-like band dispersion, quartic-oscillator energy levels, photonic-crystal transmission, and Fibonacci-chain transmission, using repeated nested validation, learning curves, random-forest and multilayer-perceptron baselines, and low-rank Nyström tests where relevant. The framework is intended for finite-data regression of precomputed observables, while boundary conditions remain part of the physical model that generates those observables.

**Background**: A Green's function is the impulse response of a linear differential operator with specified boundary conditions, and it can be viewed as an integral kernel that solves differential equations. The kernel polynomial method (KPM) expands functions in Chebyshev polynomials, and Jackson damping factors are used to suppress Gibbs oscillations and preserve positivity in such expansions. Support vector regression (SVR) is a kernel-based regression technique, and the choice of kernel strongly affects performance on structured physical data.

A new arXiv paper (2506.17277v2) systematically studies how document segmentation and embedding model choice affect retrieval-augmented generation for chemistry, using ChemQuests, a corpus of 952 question-answer pairs from 151 ChemRxiv papers across 17 subfields. The authors screen 41 embedding models on external chemistry retrieval benchmarks and evaluate shortlisted models across five chunking strategies, seven chunk sizes, and multiple overlap settings. This work provides benchmark-based evidence on the interaction between chunking and embedding for chemistry-specific retrieval, an area where such evidence was previously limited. It offers practical guidance for building chemistry-aware RAG systems and introduces MTEB-compatible benchmarks that can standardize future evaluation. Embedding choice was associated with the largest observed differences in evidence retrieval, with retrieval-tuned E5, BGE, and Nomic models among the strongest overall. Medium-to-large chunks combined with fixed-token, recursive-token, or hierarchical-section chunking provided a practical starting point, and low overlap was generally favored where overlap variation was evaluated.

A new arXiv paper (2510.21736v2) introduces Learn2Drive, a neural network-based control framework for adaptive cruise control (ACC) that incorporates social value orientation (SVO) and physics-informed constraints. The authors report that when the AV shifts from self-interested energy conservation to prosocial traffic-flow optimization, downstream vehicle average speed improves by at least 38.39% and traffic oscillations are dampened, with code released on GitHub. Most existing AV control strategies optimize individual vehicle or platoon performance while ignoring interactions with human-driven vehicles, which can worsen congestion. By treating AVs as mobile traffic regulators that account for their influence on human drivers, this work points toward system-wide efficiency gains and lower energy consumption as AVs enter mixed traffic. The framework combines neural networks with physics-informed constraints and uses SVO to parameterize how much weight the AV places on others' outcomes versus its own. The reported 38.39% improvement in downstream average speed is a numerical simulation result, and the paper is a preprint that has not yet undergone peer review.

## [L2R: Low-Rank and Lipschitz-Controlled Routing for Mixture-of-Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.21349) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces L2R (Low-rank & Lipschitz-controlled Routing), a unified routing framework for Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models that performs expert assignment in a shared low-rank latent routing space and introduces Saturated Inner-Product Scoring (SIPS) to explicitly control the Lipschitz behavior of routing functions. It also incorporates a parameter-efficient multi-anchor routing mechanism, and experiments on an OLMoE-based language MoE model and a ViT-based ImageNet setting show improved overall performance and better routing geometry and expert discrimination. Many modern MoE systems still rely on linear routers in raw high-dimensional representation spaces, where representation mismatch, angular concentration, and scale-sensitive scoring can undermine routing discriminability and stable expert specialization. By reshaping both the routing space and scoring geometry, L2R could help improve the stability and effectiveness of expert specialization in large sparse models, which is relevant to ongoing efforts to scale neural networks efficiently. L2R performs expert assignment in a shared low-rank latent routing space and uses Saturated Inner-Product Scoring (SIPS) to yield smoother and more stable routing geometry, while a parameter-efficient multi-anchor routing mechanism enhances expert expressiveness. The paper reports experiments on an OLMoE-based language MoE model and a ViT-based ImageNet setting, with OLMoE diagnostics showing improved routing geometry and expert discrimination.

A new arXiv paper (2602.06578v2) systematically investigates how the norm parameter p in Lp-constrained adversarial attacks affects the sparsity and smoothness of perturbations for p in [1,2]. The authors adopt two established sparsity measures and introduce three new smoothness measures, including a general framework based on smoothing operations and one based on first-order Taylor approximations. The findings show that the commonly used L1 and L2 norms are suboptimal in most cases, and that p in [1.3, 1.5] yields the best trade-off between sparse and smooth attacks, which could guide more principled norm selection in adversarial robustness research and evaluation. The empirical evaluation spans multiple real-world image datasets and diverse model architectures, including both convolutional and transformer-based networks. The optimal p value is shown to be task-dependent, and the paper emphasizes that norm choice should be made systematically rather than by default.

## [Drag-Aware Aerodynamic Manipulability for Torque-Limited Redundant Multirotors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.07998) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces a capacity-aware extension of aerodynamic promptness for redundant multirotors with arbitrary numbers of heterogeneous rotors and wrench components. It defines a symmetric acceleration capacity (SAC) as the largest zero-centered subset of each asymmetric instantaneous rotor-acceleration interval under bounded motor torque and aerodynamic drag, then propagates its co-metric through the nonlinear rotor-speed-to-wrench differential to obtain a state-attached task-rate capability matrix and ellipsoid. This work provides a task-coordinate-invariant criterion for selecting task-equivalent actuator states, which can improve thrust allocation and reduce saturation-induced force-tracking degradation in multirotor control. It matters for aerial robotics researchers and engineers designing torque-limited redundant multirotor platforms that must operate near actuator limits. The inverse quadratic form of the task-rate capability matrix equals the minimum normalized rotor-acceleration effort required to realize a prescribed wrench rate, while the ellipsoid volume defines the drag-aware aerodynamic manipulability (DAAM) index. Fiberwise DAAM maximization exists on compact regular domains and can be nonconvex and set valued; low-dimensional two- and three-rotor studies illustrate the geometry, and a two-rotor use case shows reduced saturation-induced force-tracking degradation relative to the pseudoinverse in faster command bands.

Manipulability measures how effectively a robot can generate motion or force in different directions, and aerodynamic promptness quantifies how rotor-speed variations translate into wrench variations; this paper extends that concept by accounting for bounded motor torque and aerodynamic drag.

## [Green-ELM: Non-Iterative Neural Training via Random Projections](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.15613) ⭐️ 6.0/10

Green-ELM is a non-iterative neural architecture that replaces gradient-based output-layer optimization with a closed-form analytic solution over a fixed high-dimensional random feature space (d ≫ 784). Using the Moore-Penrose pseudoinverse, LU and Cholesky decomposition, it achieves 98.10% accuracy on MNIST (d=4000) and 86.63% on Fashion-MNIST, with a CPU baseline reaching 97.15% on MNIST in 1.5 seconds — an 11.6× training-time reduction over an SGD baseline. This approach offers a viable alternative for real-time Edge AI, where the traditional iterative training phase is bypassed in favor of non-iterative manifold representation and readout. It also suggests that feature-space expansion alone can contribute substantially to classification performance, potentially reducing reliance on costly backpropagation. The paper observes a near-logarithmic scaling behavior between hidden dimensionality and accuracy over the tested range, and proposes an Empirical Scaling Hypothesis modeling accuracy bounds as a function of dimensionality and intrinsic dataset complexity. The method is also tested with a pre-trained frozen ResNet-18 backbone, showing one-shot solvers can work beyond simple datasets, though evaluation remains limited to MNIST and Fashion-MNIST.

**Background**: Extreme Learning Machines (ELMs) are feedforward neural networks where hidden node parameters are randomly assigned and never updated, and only the output weights are learned in a single step, effectively as a linear model. Green-ELM follows this paradigm by projecting inputs into a high-dimensional random feature space and solving for the output layer analytically using the Moore-Penrose pseudoinverse, which computes a least-squares best-fit solution to linear systems. ResNet-18 is a lightweight 18-layer convolutional neural network with residual skip connections, commonly used as a feature extractor.

A new paper (arXiv:2606.01670v3) proposes TDPM, a generative recommendation framework that applies time-aware diffusion on semantic ID (SID) tokens instead of treating all historical interactions uniformly. TDPM disentangles user preference into a long-term period preference and a recent point preference, and reports average improvements of up to 29.21% in HR@20 and 25.45% in NDCG@20 over state-of-the-art baselines on three public real-world datasets. The method operates on SID tokens and separates preference into period preference (consistent over long time spans) and point preference (triggered by recent focal events), with an ablation study confirming that time-aware token diffusion is necessary. The reported gains are averages across three public datasets, so absolute performance and generalization to other domains or industrial-scale data remain to be validated.

<a id="item-333"></a>
## [Local AI Cascade De-Identifies Educational Dialogue Without Cloud](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.18372) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2606.18372v2) proposes a fully local AI cascade framework that reframes educational dialogue de-identification as constrained privacy triage, combining a recall-first union proposer of lightweight encoders and deterministic rules with a context-aware reviewer making binary Redact/Keep decisions. The strongest local configuration reaches 0.958 macro F1, beating a same-family LLM-only baseline (0.767) and a commercial API (0.706), while running entirely on a single laptop. This matters because educational transcripts often contain personally identifiable information entangled with curricular content, and existing approaches force a tradeoff between data governance and accuracy. The results suggest that for educational de-identification, problem formulation matters more than model scale, enabling privacy-preserving research without sending student data to third parties. The cascade uses two lightweight encoders (DeBERTa and ModernBERT) plus deterministic rules to intentionally over-generate candidate PII spans, optimizing for recall, after which a context-aware reviewer uses surrounding dialogue and speaker role to decide Redact or Keep. On a targeted challenge set of curricular-personal name ambiguity (e.g., 'Riemann' as a student or a math concept), the strongest configuration degrades by only 0.03 F1 versus 0.19–0.25 for smaller reviewers.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: De-identification is the process of removing personally identifiable information (PII) from data so it can be used for research while protecting privacy. Named entity recognition (NER) systems detect entities like names and locations, but local NER often over-redacts curricular terms that look like names, while commercial LLMs handle ambiguity better but require sending sensitive data to third parties. This paper's cascade approach separates the task into high-recall candidate proposal followed by contextual review to balance privacy and accuracy locally.

## [MERIT: Simple Multi-Key Memory Retrieval for Ultra-Long Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07663) ⭐️ 6.0/10

Researchers propose MERIT (Multi-key Episodic Retrieval with Inference-time Temporal expansion), an agentic framework that builds query-agnostic episodic memories using a multi-key representation and defers query-specific relation composition to inference time. It achieves state-of-the-art results on three long-video benchmarks: EgoLifeQA, LVBench, and Video-MME (Long). Ultra-long video understanding is a practical bottleneck for multimodal LLMs, and MERIT shows that a simple high-recall retrieval design can outperform more complex memory-construction pipelines. This could lower the engineering cost of building video agents for applications like egocentric lifelogging, surveillance review, and long-form media analysis. MERIT uses a simple key-matching mechanism for precise retrieval of fine-grained memories, plus a neighbor filtering mechanism that expands the temporal scope only around retrieved segments at inference time, avoiding the overhead of global memory construction. The approach is query-agnostic during memory building, meaning the same memory store can serve many different downstream questions.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can process text, images, and audio, but feeding hours or days of video directly into them is impractical due to context and compute limits. A common workaround is a two-stage paradigm: first build a memory of the video without knowing the question, then retrieve relevant parts to answer a specific query. Prior work often invests heavily in complex memory construction to pre-model high-level relations, even though the downstream query is unknown at build time.

<a id="item-335"></a>
## [Target-Aligned Validation for Scarce Confirmatory Amyloid PET Measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22223) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2608.22223v3) proposes a target-aligned validation rule for allocating scarce confirmatory amyloid PET measurements in Alzheimer's disease trials, where the first-order value of validating a subject equals target influence multiplied by residual protocol uncertainty. Applied to the A4/LEARN PET archive, the authors show that for the primary APOE4 carrier versus non-carrier contrast in Centiloid 24-or-higher PET positivity, simple APOE4-balanced validation recovers nearly all the target-specific gain: at a PET budget of 200, the confidence-interval width ratio relative to random validation is 0.923 for APOE4 balancing and 0.914 for target-specific scoring, while generic uncertainty sampling only reaches 0.980. This work offers an operational, transparent framework for deciding when simple validation suffices and when a fitted residual-uncertainty score justifies added complexity, which could directly inform budget-constrained clinical trial design and payer-facing evidence packages. It also challenges the default use of generic uncertainty sampling in medical machine learning, showing that spending scarce measurements on merely hard-to-predict subjects can be misaligned with the scientific, clinical, or commercial claim being validated. The paper treats observed PET as a design laboratory for scarce-confirmation studies and finds that other targets behave differently: target-specific scoring yields larger gains for an age-slope analysis and for cutoff-indexed PET positivity, whereas generic uncertainty sampling ignores target influence entirely. The core caveat is that the approach is evaluated on a single archive (A4/LEARN) and its benefits are target-dependent, so the simple transparent rule is not universally dominated by the more complex fitted score.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Alzheimer's disease is characterized by brain accumulation of amyloid plaques and tau tangles, and amyloid PET imaging is a gold-standard research tool for measuring amyloid burden, though scans cost roughly $3,000 or more and are not routinely covered by insurers. Anti-amyloid therapies and blood-based biomarkers are pushing Alzheimer's workups toward a two-stage workflow: broad screening with cheaper information, followed by scarce confirmatory amyloid measurements. Active learning is a machine learning paradigm in which a model selectively queries the most informative labels, and uncertainty sampling is its most popular strategy, querying instances with maximally uncertain predictions. This paper argues that in protocol settings, validation should be allocated according to the claim being validated rather than prediction uncertainty alone.

<a id="item-336"></a>
## [D³-MOPD: Dynamic Domain Scheduling for Multi-Teacher Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24987) ⭐️ 6.0/10

D³-MOPD introduces a zero-overhead asynchronous scheduler that dynamically adjusts per-domain data mixtures during multi-teacher on-policy distillation by tracking each domain's reverse-KL trajectory. On a Qwen3.6-35B-A3B student distilled from four domain-expert teachers, it closes 97% of the average student-to-teacher performance gap versus 63% for vanilla MOPD, reaching the same peak performance with roughly 3× fewer rollout steps and surpassing specialist teachers on three of seven benchmarks. Fixed per-domain data mixtures waste compute on fast-converging domains while undertraining slower ones, a real inefficiency in multi-teacher distillation pipelines. This scheduler addresses that problem without modifying the core training loop, making it a practical drop-in improvement for teams building capable multi-domain student models. The scheduler runs asynchronously outside the training process as an off-process watcher that periodically estimates each domain's remaining headroom and current improvement rate, then adjusts domain sampling ratios without altering the core training loop. It scales naturally to arbitrary numbers of domains, and the expected benefit grows as more domains introduce more diverse convergence patterns.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) trains a single student model by minimizing per-domain reverse-KL divergence on the student's own rollouts, using several domain-expert teachers. Reverse-KL divergence is a Kullback-Leibler divergence variant commonly used in knowledge distillation to align a student's output distribution with a teacher's. Existing MOPD approaches typically fix the per-domain data mixture before training, overlooking that different domains converge at substantially different rates.

## [UFO: Unified Chain-of-Evaluation for Multi-Modal Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12397) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (arXiv:2609.12397v2) proposes UFO, the first unified framework for simultaneously evaluating omni-condition alignment in multi-modal image generation. UFO introduces an Atomized Chain-of-Evaluation paradigm that decomposes alignment into sequential Atomic Evaluation Units (AEUs) and reports an average 15.25% improvement in correlation with human preferences, alongside a new benchmark called UFO-Bench. Existing evaluation methods assess each modality condition in isolation, which conflicts with the simultaneous alignment objective of multi-modal generation and leads to poor agreement with human judgment. A unified, human-aligned evaluation framework could give researchers and practitioners a more reliable way to compare subject-driven customization models and guide future model development. UFO first generates AEUs conditioned on the visual and textual inputs, assigns each AEU a modality-relevance class, and then applies general or dedicated functional calls to verify different AEU types. The paper also presents UFO-Bench to holistically evaluate existing customization models under diverse interactions between textual and visual conditions.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Multi-modal image generation, especially subject-driven customization, lets users supply both a reference image and a text prompt so that a model preserves a subject while following requested edits. Evaluating such outputs is hard because a good image must satisfy several conditions at once, yet most existing metrics—whether embedding-based or MLLM-based—check each condition separately. Chain-of-evaluation approaches, inspired by chain-of-thought style decomposition, break a complex judgment into smaller verifiable steps, which is the strategy UFO adopts.

## [Legal Planning Stack Uses Deontic Logic to Constrain Robot Motion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15113) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.15113v2) introduces a legal planning stack that combines Defeasible Deontic Logic (DDL) with learned world models to constrain robot motion planning, enabling ex ante governance that intervenes before an illegal action is executed. Deployed on a simulated robot arm pushing a cube across a 3x3 grid, the legislated agent abided by legal norms substantially more often than a non-legislated one, and modeling perception uncertainty further improved compliance. This work addresses a core challenge in AI governance and robot ethics: how to translate abstract legal norms into runtime constraints that prevent illegal actions rather than merely punishing them afterward. If scalable, such ex ante legislation could let increasingly general robots operate safely in human societies while keeping legal reasoning auditable and adaptable to changing rules. The paper formalizes two isomorphism gaps: a grounding gap, where perception errors feed false atoms into the legal reasoner, and an ontological gap, where a single legal conclusion admits multiple faithful translations into planning constraints that yield drastically different abidance. The DDL reasoner ran efficiently at runtime with auditable verdicts and adapted to exogenous signals and endogenous rule changes, but the demonstration is limited to a simple 3x3 grid task.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: Deontic logic is the formal study of obligations, prohibitions, and permissions, and its defeasible variant handles default reasoning where rules can be overridden by exceptions. World models are learned predictive models that let robots anticipate the consequences of actions, and ex ante governance means constraining behavior before execution rather than auditing it afterward. This paper sits at the intersection of these three areas, aiming to make robot behavior align with society's legal norms.

<a id="item-339"></a>
## [CSWAM adds V-JEPA 2.1 causal semantics to boost OOD generalization in world action models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18462) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces CSWAM (Causal Semantic World Action Model), which augments FastWAM-style world action models with a causal semantic expert built on V-JEPA 2.1. On RoboTwin 2.0 Clean-to-Randomized transfer, CSWAM raises success from 10.16% to 45.18% (a 35.02 percentage point gain over FastWAM), and across two real-robot tasks and three OOD difficulty levels it improves average success by 42.5 percentage points, from 27.5% to 70.0%. Out-of-distribution generalization under visual distribution shifts remains a major bottleneck for embodied AI policies, and this work shows that injecting temporally grounded, appearance-independent semantic representations can deliver large, measurable gains without sacrificing FastWAM's efficient action-only inference. If the results hold, it could make world action models substantially more deployable in real-world robot settings where lighting, textures, and object appearances differ from training. The causal semantic expert learns the future evolution of V-JEPA representations from a sparse history of current and past observations, and shares this history-derived context with both the video and action streams through causal attention. At inference, CSWAM conditions action denoising on the current video state and observed semantic history, retaining efficient action-only inference; however, the work is an arXiv preprint with no code release mentioned and builds on existing FastWAM and V-JEPA components rather than introducing a new paradigm.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 18, 04:00

**Background**: World action models (WAMs) learn a video world model during training but predict actions directly at inference time, as in FastWAM, which avoids the cost of generating future frames. V-JEPA 2.1 is Meta's self-supervised video representation model that learns dense, temporally grounded features with less reliance on appearance-specific details. CSWAM combines these ideas by using V-JEPA 2.1 features as a causal semantic signal that helps the policy identify task-relevant state changes and motion even when visual conditions are unfamiliar.
